1. Actinomyces israelii

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: lumpy jaw after dental work or trauma, with a painless indurated mass that develops draining sinus tracts.
  • Dx: pus with yellow 'sulfur granules' (bacterial colonies); gram-positive branching filaments; not acid-fast.
  • Tx: prolonged high-dose penicillin G (weeks to months) plus surgical drainage if needed.
  • Buzzwords: normal oral, GI, and female genital flora; anaerobic; IUD associated with pelvic actinomycosis; aspiration causes thoracic disease.
  • Look-alike: Nocardia is aerobic, weakly acid-fast, has no sulfur granules, and is treated with TMP-SMX.
  1. 1Israeli soldier: Actinomyces israelii is a gram-positive bacillus (the 'Israeli' soldier gives the species name israelii).
  2. 2Purple hues: Actinomyces israelii is a gram-positive bacillus, so it stains purple on Gram stain. It is not acid-fast, which distinguishes it from Nocardia.
  3. 3Branching tree: A. israelii forms filamentous, branching rods that resemble fungal hyphae, though it is a bacterium. Its branching filaments give it the 'tree' appearance on Gram stain.
  4. 4Anaerobic gasmask: A. israelii is classified as a facultative anaerobe in Sketchy's teaching, though it grows best under anaerobic conditions. It is often listed simply as an anaerobe, which is why wounds with poor blood supply and low oxygen favor infection.
  5. 5Jaw wrap: A. israelii is normal flora of the mouth, GI tract, and female genital tract. After mucosal injury (e.g., dental procedures, extractions, or trauma) it can cause slowly progressive cervicofacial actinomycosis, beginning as a painless, non-tender jaw lump that evolves into an abscess ('lumpy jaw').⭐ Cervicofacial disease is the most common form (lumpy jaw).
  6. 6Storm drain: Cervicofacial actinomycosis lesions form sinus tracts draining to the skin (the storm drain), releasing purulent discharge that contains hard yellow 'sulfur granules'. The sinus tracts cross tissue planes.
  7. 7Yellow rocks: The yellow 'sulfur granules' are rock-like aggregates of Actinomyces filaments, and they contain no sulfur. Crushing a granule and Gram staining it shows gram-positive branching rods.⭐ Sulfur granules = Actinomyces.
  8. 8Purple pencil: Penicillin G is the treatment of choice for Actinomyces infection, usually for a prolonged course (weeks to months). Complicated cases may also need surgical drainage; doxycycline is an alternative in penicillin allergy.
Show the original legend picture

2. Adenovirus

  1. 1Den of lions: Adenoviruses are DNA viruses in the Adenoviridae family.
  2. 2Cool colors: Adenoviruses are DNA viruses.
  3. 3Statue of David: Adenoviruses lack envelopes, classifying them as naked viruses.
  4. 4Water drips: Adenoviruses are primarily spread through respiratory droplets.
  5. 5Poop: Adenovirus can be transmitted through the fecal-oral route, though this is generally less common than respiratory transmission or direct contact with contaminated surfaces.
  6. 6Contaminated pool: Public swimming pools, if not properly treated, can be hotspots for adenovirus transmission due to contaminated water.
  7. 7Camo: Outbreaks of adenovirus, especially serotypes 4 and 7, are known to occur in military barracks, particularly among new recruits due to close living conditions.
  8. 8Kids: Children, especially those in daycare settings or schools, are commonly affected by adenoviruses.
  9. 9Throat cave: Tonsillitis is inflammation of the palatine tonsils, usually presenting with sore throat, fever, and enlarged, erythematous tonsils; most acute cases are viral (e.g., adenovirus), while bacterial causes include Streptococcus pyogenes (Group A strep).
  10. 10Back of throat: Adenoviruses can cause a wide range of clinical illnesses, including pharyngitis, tonsillitis, fever, bronchitis, pneumonia, gastroenteritis, and conjunctivitis.
  11. 11Crotch blood: Adenovirus infection, especially with serotypes 11 and 21, can cause hemorrhagic cystitis, an infection of the bladder mucosa presenting with painful urination and gross hematuria, particularly in children and immunocompromised patients.
  12. 12Red eyes: Adenovirus infection commonly causes viral conjunctivitis, an inflammation of the conjunctiva (the membrane lining the eyelids and covering the sclera), also known as “pink eye,” characterized by conjunctival redness, itching, tearing, and watery discharge.
  13. 13“Adeno live lions”: The adenovirus vaccine is a live, oral vaccine that provides protection against serotypes 4 and 7 and is primarily used in military recruits, especially those entering basic training, to prevent infection and reduce the risk of respiratory illnesses in close-quarter environments.
  14. 14Syringe + camo: The adenovirus vaccine is a live, oral vaccine that provides protection against serotypes 4 and 7 and is primarily used in military recruits, especially those entering basic training, in close-quarter environments.

3. Anaplasma phagocytophilum & Ehrlichia chaffeensis (Anaplasmataceae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: fever, headache, myalgias after tick exposure (summer, wooded areas), with leukopenia, thrombocytopenia, and elevated LFTs.
  • Obligate intracellular gram-negative bacteria that form morulae inside white cells.
  • Diagnosis: blood smear with morulae (Anaplasma in granulocytes, Ehrlichia in monocytes); PCR; serology.
  • Treatment: doxycycline (start empirically, do not wait for confirmation).
  • Buzzwords: mulberry (morulae), Ixodes tick, lone star tick, no rash typically.
  1. 1Indoor saloon: Anaplasma phagocytophilum and Ehrlichia chaffeensis are obligate intracellular bacteria.⭐ They live in vacuoles of white blood cells.
  2. 2Red checkered tablecloths: Both organisms are gram-negative bacteria (red on Gram stain), though they stain poorly and are not usually seen this way.
  3. 3Ana: Anaplasma phagocytophilum (causes human granulocytic anaplasmosis).
  4. 4Black leg chaps: Anaplasma phagocytophilum is transmitted by the black-legged (deer) tick, Ixodes scapularis; the same tick also transmits Lyme disease (Borrelia burgdorferi) and babesiosis, so coinfection is possible.⭐ Suspect coinfection if Lyme patients have cytopenias or fail to improve.
  5. 5Mouse running under Ana: The white-footed mouse is a reservoir host of Anaplasma phagocytophilum.
  6. 6Deer taxidermy between Ana and Earl: White-tailed deer are reservoir hosts of both Anaplasma phagocytophilum and Ehrlichia chaffeensis.
  7. 7Earl licking chops: Ehrlichia chaffeensis (causes human monocytic ehrlichiosis).
  8. 8Texas flag shirt: Ehrlichia chaffeensis is transmitted by the lone star tick (Amblyomma americanum), found in the south-central and southeastern US.⭐ Lone star tick = Ehrlichia (also Southern tick-associated rash illness).
  9. 9Red face + fanning self: Anaplasmosis and ehrlichiosis present with flu-like symptoms: fever, chills, headache, myalgias, and fatigue.⭐ Rash is uncommon in anaplasmosis and present in a minority of ehrlichiosis, unlike RMSF.
  10. 10Bloated belly: Both may also cause gastrointestinal symptoms such as nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain.
  11. 11Empty pans: Both infections can cause leukopenia, thrombocytopenia, mild anemia, and in severe cases pancytopenia; transaminases are often elevated.⭐ Fever plus leukopenia, thrombocytopenia, and high LFTs after a tick bite suggests ehrlichiosis or anaplasmosis.
  12. 12Mulberry pie on serving dish: Wright-Giemsa peripheral smear shows leukocytes containing morulae (intracellular clusters of bacteria, mulberry-like); Anaplasma infects granulocytes, Ehrlichia infects monocytes.⭐ Morulae in neutrophils = Anaplasma; in monocytes = Ehrlichia.
  13. 13Bicycle: Treatment of anaplasmosis and ehrlichiosis is doxycycline.⭐ Doxycycline is used even in young children when these infections are suspected; rifampin is an alternative in pregnancy.
Show the original legend picture

4. Aspergillus fumigatus

No Sketchy video/scene available for this organism in the current deck — notes below are compiled directly from your Anki cards.

ID / Micro
  • Mold only (not dimorphic); catalase-positive (along with Candida); forms conidiophores with fruiting bodies
  • Acute-angle (<45°) septate branching hyphae
  • Transmitted via inhalation; aflatoxins (from Aspergillus-contaminated grains/nuts) are a risk factor for hepatocellular carcinoma
Clinical syndromes
  • Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA) — Type I hypersensitivity in asthma/cystic fibrosis patients; wheezing, fever, migratory infiltrates, ↑IgE, eosinophilia; treated with corticosteroids
  • Aspergilloma — fungus ball colonizing a pre-existing cavity (TB, Klebsiella); gravity-dependent on upright CXR; needs surgical excision + azole
  • Invasive aspergillosis — immunocompromised patients; invades blood vessels → fever/cough/hemoptysis, disseminates to kidneys (failure), heart (endocarditis), brain (ring-enhancing lesions); treated with IV voriconazole
  • Can spread to paranasal sinuses causing necrosis around the nose (shared with mucormycosis)
Treatment
  • Mild/local: azoles (voriconazole) or echinocandins (caspofungin). Invasive: IV voriconazole. Chronic granulomatous disease patients are especially susceptible (catalase-positive organism).

5. Babesia species

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: fever, hemolytic anemia, and dark urine after a summer tick exposure in the Northeast/upper Midwest; severe in asplenic or immunocompromised patients.
  • Microbiology: intraerythrocytic protozoan (B. microti) transmitted by Ixodes scapularis; reservoir in white-footed mice and deer; co-infection with Borrelia and Anaplasma is common.
  • Diagnosis: Giemsa blood smear showing ring forms and 'Maltese cross' tetrads (no pigment or schizonts, unlike malaria); PCR confirms.
  • Treatment: atovaquone plus azithromycin; clindamycin plus quinine for severe disease; exchange transfusion in severe cases.
  • Buzzwords: 'Maltese cross', 'Ixodes', 'asplenia', 'Nantucket/Cape Cod', 'hemolytic anemia without travel'.
  1. 1Vampire babes: Babesia species are protozoa that infect red blood cells. Most infections are asymptomatic and healthy people may recover spontaneously, but asplenic individuals or those with severe underlying illness are at higher risk of severe disease.
  2. 2Robin of Ixodes: The Ixodes tick is both vector and reservoir for Babesia: it transmits sporozoites while feeding, which invade RBCs and multiply as trophozoites and merozoites, causing hemolysis.⭐ Ixodes also transmits Lyme disease and anaplasmosis; co-infection occurs.
  3. 3Tick shield: Babesiosis is transmitted to humans by the bite of an infected Ixodes scapularis tick (deer or black-legged tick); the risk of transmission rises the longer the tick stays attached.
  4. 4Deer antlers: Babesiosis is transmitted to humans by the bite of an infected Ixodes scapularis tick (deer or black-legged tick); the risk of transmission rises the longer the tick stays attached. (Deer are the reservoir for the tick's adult stage; white-footed mice are the main Babesia reservoir.)
  5. 5'NE': Babesia infections are mainly reported in the northeastern and upper midwestern United States, matching the distribution of Ixodes ticks.⭐ Think Nantucket and Cape Cod.
  6. 6Shattered red stained glass: Babesia infection causes hemolytic anemia from destruction of infected RBCs, with fever, fatigue, jaundice, and dark urine.
  7. 7Yellow dress: Babesia infection causes hemolytic anemia from destruction of infected RBCs, with fever, fatigue, jaundice, and dark urine. (jaundice)
  8. 8Sweat: Babesia infection causes hemolytic anemia from destruction of infected RBCs, with fever, fatigue, jaundice, and dark urine. (fever and sweats)
  9. 9Irregular tunic hem: Patients may have an irregular, cyclical fever pattern from intermittent lysis as Babesia multiply and rupture host cells.
  10. 10Sickle: Patients with sickle cell disease are at higher risk of severe babesiosis because their abnormal RBCs hemolyze more easily and are less effectively cleared by a (functionally absent) spleen.
  11. 11Spleen-shaped hole: Asplenia (surgical, congenital, or functional, as in sickle cell disease) increases the risk of severe, life-threatening babesiosis because the spleen normally clears infected RBCs.⭐ Asplenic patients can have very high parasitemia.
  12. 12Thick red carpet: A blood smear shows Babesia inside RBCs, often as small ring-shaped trophozoites, and may form a tetrad ('Maltese cross'), which differentiates babesiosis from malaria.⭐ Babesia has no hemozoin pigment and no schizont stage on smear.
  13. 13Maltese Cross: Tetrad forms of Babesia in RBCs form a 'Maltese cross', a diagnostic feature that distinguishes babesiosis from malaria.
  14. 14Ato-vampire queen: Atovaquone combined with the macrolide azithromycin is an effective treatment for babesiosis; clindamycin plus quinine is an alternative, used for severe disease.⭐ Atovaquone + azithromycin is first-line.
  15. 15Macrolide crows: Azithromycin (a macrolide) is combined with atovaquone to treat babesiosis; clindamycin plus quinine is sometimes used.
Show the original legend picture

6. Bacillus anthracis & cereus

Exam snapshot
  • Anthrax: cutaneous (painless black eschar with edema), inhalational (woolsorters' disease with hemorrhagic mediastinitis and widened mediastinum), and a bioterrorism agent
  • B. anthracis: large gram-positive rod in chains (boxcar/bamboo), spore-forming, non-motile, with a unique poly-D-glutamate capsule
  • Toxin = protective antigen + edema factor (calmodulin-dependent adenylate cyclase, raises cAMP) and lethal factor (protease)
  • B. cereus: reheated fried rice, preformed toxin, vomiting in 1-6 h (self-limited)
  • Anthrax treatment: ciprofloxacin/levofloxacin or doxycycline; inhalational disease needs combination therapy and antitoxin
  1. 1King Anthra's axe: Bacillus anthracis, the cause of anthrax, is a large gram-positive spore-forming rod. It is non-motile and non-hemolytic on blood agar.
  2. 2Purple hues: B. anthracis and B. cereus are both gram-positive bacilli (purple on Gram stain).
  3. 3Ring of fire: Cutaneous anthrax (the most common form) begins as a painless papule or vesicle that undergoes central necrosis, leaving a black eschar with surrounding edema. It is typically painless, and with treatment mortality is low.⭐ Painless black eschar = cutaneous anthrax.
  4. 4Chain of ships: B. anthracis and B. cereus are gram-positive bacilli that grow in long chains (boxcar-shaped, bamboo-like in B. anthracis).
  5. 5"D" buckles + leather armor: B. anthracis has a unique capsule made of poly-gamma-D-glutamic acid (a polypeptide, not a polysaccharide). The capsule is antiphagocytic.⭐ Only bacterial capsule composed of D-amino acids (polypeptide).
  6. 6Aerobic bellows: B. anthracis and B. cereus are aerobic or facultatively anaerobic, which differentiates them from the obligate anaerobic Clostridium.
  7. 7Spored walnuts: Both organisms form endospores, which are metabolically inactive and resistant to heat, radiation, desiccation, and chemicals. Anthrax spores persist in soil for years, which makes them usable as a bioweapon.
  8. 8"EF" shield: Edema factor (EF) is a calmodulin-dependent adenylate cyclase that converts ATP to cAMP. Raised cAMP causes tissue edema and impairs neutrophil function, helping the infection become established.
  9. 9Camps: Edema factor (EF) raises intracellular cAMP (calmodulin-dependent adenylate cyclase), causing local edema and impaired phagocyte function. EF and LF enter cells through pores formed by protective antigen (PA); PA is the target of the anthrax vaccine.
  10. 10"LF" shield: Lethal factor (LF) is a zinc protease that cleaves MAP kinase kinases (MAPKK/MEK), disrupting MAPK signaling pathways and the host immune response, which leads to cell death.
  11. 11"LF" viking burning map: Lethal factor (LF) cleaves MAP kinase kinases, disrupting MAPK signaling and leading to impaired immunity, macrophage death, and shock. Together with EF, it is responsible for systemic toxicity.
  12. 12Sheep wool: Inhalational anthrax was historically called woolsorters' disease, from inhaling spores in animal wool, hides, and hair. It is now rare in developed countries but is a bioterrorism concern.
  13. 13Bloody axe: Inhalational anthrax causes hemorrhagic mediastinitis (and often hemorrhagic pleural effusions), followed by rapid sepsis and shock. It has high mortality, and some patients also develop hemorrhagic meningitis.
  14. 14Widened mast: Inhalational anthrax shows a widened mediastinum on chest X-ray, from hemorrhagic mediastinal lymphadenitis, with little true pneumonia.⭐ Widened mediastinum + wool/hide exposure or bioterror = inhalational anthrax.
  15. 15Flower shield: Treatment of anthrax is a fluoroquinolone (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin) or doxycycline. Systemic disease needs combination therapy, and antitoxin monoclonal antibodies can be added.
  16. 16Bicycle tire shield: Post-exposure prophylaxis and treatment are with ciprofloxacin/levofloxacin or doxycycline (prolonged course, ~60 days after inhalational exposure), plus the anthrax vaccine.
  17. 17Rice bowl: Bacillus cereus is a gram-positive spore-forming rod that causes food poisoning, classically linked to rice dishes (fried rice).
  18. 18Vomiting: Emetic B. cereus food poisoning, from a preformed toxin, presents with nausea and vomiting about 1-6 h after ingestion, with abdominal cramps. It is self-limited, and a diarrheal form (toxin produced in the gut, onset 6-15 h) also exists.
  19. 19Reheating rice: Cooked rice left warm lets surviving heat-resistant spores germinate and produce a preformed heat-stable toxin, and reheating or flash-frying does not destroy it. Short incubation, so early-onset food poisoning.⭐ Reheated fried rice = B. cereus; mayo/custard = S. aureus.
Show the original legend picture

7. Bartonella henselae (Cat-scratch disease)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: young person with tender regional (often axillary) lymphadenopathy after a cat scratch or bite.
  • Gram-negative bacillus, hard to culture; visualized with Warthin-Starry silver stain; vector cat flea.
  • Immunocompromised (HIV) patients develop bacillary angiomatosis: vascular red papules and nodules that bleed.
  • Treatment: usually self-limited in healthy hosts; azithromycin for severe/lymph node disease; doxycycline or erythromycin for bacillary angiomatosis.
  • Buzzwords: cat scratch, Warthin-Starry, bacillary angiomatosis, axillary lymphadenopathy.
  1. 1Bart the leopard: Bartonella henselae is a gram-negative bacillus.
  2. 2Leopard: B. henselae is the causative agent of cat-scratch disease, a zoonosis transmitted by cat scratches or bites.⭐ Kittens are the usual source.
  3. 3Red pillow: Bartonella henselae is a gram-negative bacillus (stains red on Gram stain, though poorly).
  4. 4Silver Starry Night: Warthin-Starry silver stain is used to visualize B. henselae in tissue samples.⭐ Warthin-Starry silver stain highlights the organism in lymph node biopsy.
  5. 5Cat scratches: Cat-scratch disease is transmitted by cat scratches, bites, or via cat fleas.⭐ Cat fleas maintain infection among cats.
  6. 6Armpit ruffles: Cat-scratch disease often presents with tender regional lymphadenopathy (commonly axillary) after exposure.⭐ Lymphadenopathy appears about 1 to 3 weeks after exposure.
  7. 7Healthy young princess: Cat-scratch disease typically affects immunocompetent, often young, individuals and is usually self-limited.
  8. 8Cat scratches + immunocompromised cane: In immunocompromised patients, B. henselae can cause bacillary angiomatosis, a vascular proliferative disease.⭐ Bacillary angiomatosis is also caused by Bartonella quintana (body louse).
  9. 9Red polka dots: Bacillary angiomatosis skin lesions start as red papules and can progress to large, highly vascular, friable nodules.⭐ Lesions can resemble Kaposi sarcoma.
  10. 10Immunocompromised cane: Patients with HIV/AIDS (low CD4 count) or other immunocompromised states are especially susceptible to bacillary angiomatosis.
  11. 11Bicycle wheel: B. henselae infections are treated with doxycycline (azithromycin is also commonly used).⭐ Mild cat-scratch disease in healthy hosts often needs only supportive care.
  12. 12Macrolide crow: Erythromycin (a macrolide) is an alternative treatment for Bartonella henselae infection.
Show the original legend picture

8. Blastomyces dermatitidis

  1. 1Cannon blast: Blastomyces dermatitidis is a dimorphic fungus.
  2. 2Ohio and Mississippi River valleys + Great Lakes map: Blastomyces dermatitidis is predominantly found in the eastern and central United States, particularly around the Ohio and Mississippi River valleys and the Great Lakes region.
  3. 3Dimorphic butterfly: Blastomyces dermatitidis exhibits dimorphism, growing as mold in cooler environments (25–30°C) and as yeast at human body temperature (35–37°C).
  4. 4Breathing in dust: Infection with Blastomyces dermatitidis occurs through inhalation of airborne mold spores.
  5. 5Lung-shaped cracks in dirt: After inhalation, Blastomyces dermatitidis transforms into yeast and colonizes the lungs; pulmonary infection may be asymptomatic or present with fever, cough, and pleuritic chest pain, while chronic disease can mimic tuberculosis.
  6. 6Doubled cannonballs: The yeast form of Blastomyces dermatitidis is characterized by its broad-based budding and a double refractile cell wall, generally similar in size to an RBC.
  7. 7Dust clouds in lungs: CXR in pulmonary blastomycosis may show patchy infiltrates, mass-like lesions, or cavitation; these reflect granulomatous infection caused by Blastomyces dermatitidis, with the lungs the most commonly affected organ.
  8. 8Cannonballs in lungs: CXR in pulmonary blastomycosis may show patchy infiltrates, mass-like lesions, or cavitation; these reflect granulomatous infection caused by Blastomyces dermatitidis, with the lungs the most commonly affected organ.
  9. 9Immunocompromised cane: Immunocompromised individuals are at higher risk of disseminated blastomycosis, affecting skin, bones, and the genitourinary system.
  10. 10Statue erosion: Disseminated blastomycosis may involve the skin via hematogenous spread from the lungs and can present with various lesions, resulting in papules, pustules, ulcers, or verrucous growths.
  11. 11Exposed bone: Disseminated blastomycosis can involve bone via hematogenous spread from the lungs, representing a form of hematogenous osteomyelitis that presents with painful lytic lesions, often affecting the vertebrae, long bones, or skull and may be associated with swelling of soft tissue inflammation.
  12. 12Yellow river: Diagnosis of Blastomyces dermatitidis can be supported by detection of urine antigen (UAg). In tissue or sputum, the yeast are extracellular with broad-based budding, which can be rapidly visualized by KOH prep. Culture or histopathology remains the definitive test.
  13. 13Pinecones: Azoles—typically itraconazole or fluconazole—are the recommended treatment for mild to moderate blastomycosis.
  14. 14Amphibians: Amphotericin B is the treatment of choice for severe or disseminated blastomycosis.

9. Bordetella pertussis

  1. 1Boarded soldier: Bordetella pertussis is a gram-negative coccobacillus.
  2. 2Red hues: Bordetella pertussis is a gram-negative coccobacillus.
  3. 3Filamentous streamers: Bordetella pertussis bacteria utilize filamentous hemagglutinin as an adhesin for attachment to the respiratory epithelium.
  4. 4Bowtie + GI uniform: Bordetella pertussis toxin ribosylates inhibitory Gi proteins, inactivating them and preventing inhibition of adenylate cyclase, which leads to increased cAMP production and, in turn, impaired phagocyte function and mucus accumulation.
  5. 5Leg cast + wheelchair: Bordetella pertussis toxin ribosylates inhibitory Gi proteins, inactivating them and preventing inhibition of adenylate cyclase, which leads to increased cAMP production and, in turn, impaired phagocyte function and mucus accumulation.
  6. 6Army camp: Bordetella pertussis toxin ribosylates inhibitory Gi proteins, inactivating them and preventing inhibition of adenylate cyclase, which leads to increased cAMP production and, in turn, impaired phagocyte function and mucus accumulation.
  7. 7WBC popcorn: The pertussis toxin disrupts chemokine receptor signaling, impairing lymphocyte migration to lymphoid tissues and leading to their accumulation in the blood (lymphocytosis).
  8. 8"EF" shield: The pertussis toxin (an adenylate cyclase toxin) increases cAMP through a mechanism similar to that of Bacillus anthracis's edema factor (EF).
  9. 9Cutting tracheal grass: The tracheal cytotoxin of Bordetella pertussis specifically targets and damages ciliated respiratory epithelial cells.
  10. 10"WHOOP!": Pertussis infection occurs in three stages: the catarrhal stage (mild upper respiratory symptoms), the paroxysmal stage (typically 1-2 weeks after symptom onset, characterized by severe, repetitive "whooping" coughs that can last 2-6 weeks or longer), and the convalescent stage, during which symptoms gradually resolve.
  11. 11"100 Days War!": Pertussis (whooping cough) progresses through the three stages above (catarrhal, paroxysmal, and convalescent), which together can last up to three months, hence its nickname the "100-day cough."
  12. 12Crows: Macrolides are effective against Bordetella pertussis and can ameliorate symptoms when started early after infection onset, before the appearance of the paroxysmal cough.
  13. 13Covering beak: Whooping cough is transmitted via respiratory droplets.
  14. 14Syringe + a cellular phone: The DTaP and Tdap vaccines combine acellular Bordetella pertussis antigens with toxoids for tetanus and diphtheria; DTaP is typically administered to infants and young children, while Tdap is recommended for older children and adults.

10. Borrelia burgdorferi (Lyme disease, Spirochetes)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: erythema migrans (bull's-eye rash) after a tick bite in the Northeast/upper Midwest, later facial palsy, heart block, or large-joint arthritis.
  • Spirochete transmitted by Ixodes scapularis (needs about 36 hours of attachment); reservoir white-footed mouse, deer host adults.
  • Stains poorly with Gram; seen with Wright or Giemsa; diagnosis clinical or ELISA then confirmatory Western blot.
  • Treatment: doxycycline for early disease (amoxicillin or cefuroxime alternatives); ceftriaxone for late/severe (meningitis, carditis).
  • Buzzwords: bull's-eye rash, bilateral Bell palsy, AV block, migratory large-joint arthritis, three stages.
  1. 1Bow: Borrelia burgdorferi is a spirochete bacterium (Lyme disease is named after Lyme, Connecticut).
  2. 2Spiraled arrow: The spiral shape reflects its identity as a spirochete.
  3. 3Northeast archery competition: Borrelia burgdorferi is primarily endemic to the northeastern and upper midwestern United States, though it is becoming more common in other regions.
  4. 4Robin of Ixodes + tick: Borrelia burgdorferi is transmitted by the blacklegged (deer) tick, Ixodes scapularis, usually after 36 or more hours of attachment.⭐ Prompt tick removal prevents most transmission.
  5. 5White-footed mouse: The white-footed mouse is the main reservoir of Borrelia burgdorferi and hosts the larval and nymphal stages of Ixodes ticks.
  6. 6White-tailed deer: The white-tailed deer is the definitive host of adult Ixodes scapularis ticks (it does not become infected itself).
  7. 7Sir Wright: Borrelia takes up Gram stain poorly but can be visualized with Wright stain.
  8. 8Sir Giemsa: Borrelia can also be visualized with Giemsa stain.⭐ Diagnosis in practice is clinical or serologic (ELISA, then Western blot).
  9. 9Lyme-green outfits in front of three stages: Borrelia burgdorferi causes Lyme disease in three stages: early localized, early disseminated, and late disseminated.
  10. 10Stage 1 + bullseye: Early localized (stage 1) Lyme disease is marked by erythema migrans, an expanding red rash with central clearing (bull's-eye or target appearance), usually starting at the bite site.⭐ Erythema migrans is diagnostic clinically; treat without serology.
  11. 11Stage 1 + sweat: Early localized disease may also have flu-like symptoms: fever, chills, headache, and myalgias.
  12. 12Stage 2 + heart shield: Early disseminated (stage 2) Lyme disease can involve the heart, including myocarditis and AV block (heart block).⭐ Lyme carditis typically causes varying degrees of AV block.
  13. 13Stage 2 + bilateral bells: Early disseminated Lyme disease can cause facial nerve (CN VII) palsy, classically bilateral Bell's palsy, and other cranial neuropathies or meningitis.⭐ Bilateral Bell palsy is highly suggestive of Lyme.
  14. 14Stage 3 + moving dummy: Late disseminated (stage 3) Lyme disease causes migratory arthritis, typically of large joints.⭐ Arthritis often appears months after the bite.
  15. 15Stage 3 + arrow in knee: Late Lyme arthritis classically affects the knee, often as an oligoarticular, monoarticular, or migratory arthritis of large joints.
  16. 16Stage 3 + arrow in head: Late Lyme disease may cause neurologic complications such as encephalopathy (cognitive dysfunction, memory loss, mood and sleep changes), polyneuropathy, and encephalomyelitis.
  17. 17Unicycle: Doxycycline is recommended for early Lyme disease (early localized or mild early disseminated).⭐ Amoxicillin or cefuroxime is used in pregnancy and young children.
  18. 183 axes shield: Ceftriaxone is used for late-stage or severe Lyme disease, including meningitis and carditis with high-grade block.

11. Borrelia recurrentis

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: refugee or homeless patient in overcrowded, louse-infested conditions with recurrent fever episodes
  • Spirochete (Borrelia) with antigenic variation of surface proteins (vlp/vsp) causing relapses
  • Dx: Wright-Giemsa blood smear during fever (also dark-field); not easily Gram stained
  • Treatment: penicillin G or doxycycline; watch for Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction
  • Buzzwords: louse-borne relapsing fever, Pediculus humanus corporis, 2-part crisis (chill/flush), humans only reservoir
  1. 1Bow: Borrelia recurrentis is a spirochete bacterium.
  2. 2Recurring nightmare: Borrelia recurrentis causes louse-borne relapsing fever (LBRF).
  3. 3Spiral-shaped bed posts: Borrelia recurrentis is a spiral-shaped spirochete.
  4. 4Illuminating stars + dark ceiling: Borrelia recurrentis can be seen by dark-field microscopy as thin, helical, motile spirochetes.
  5. 5Sir Wright + Sir Giemsa: Borrelia recurrentis is visualized on a peripheral blood smear with Wright or Wright-Giemsa stain (best during febrile episodes).⭐ Large spirochetes visible in blood: Borrelia is one of the few spirochetes seen on a blood smear.
  6. 6Variable color lights: Borrelia recurrentis has variable surface antigens in its outer membrane (antigenic variation) that let it evade the immune system.⭐ Antigenic variation explains the relapsing fevers.
  7. 7Pile of stuffed animals: Risk factors for louse-borne relapsing fever are living in resource-poor settings with overcrowding (for example refugee camps), war and famine.
  8. 8Louse stuffed toy: The vector is Pediculus humanus corporis (human body louse).
  9. 9Scratching: Infected lice transmit B. recurrentis when a person crushes a louse while scratching, letting spirochetes enter through the broken skin (lice bites do not directly inject the organism).⭐ Transmission is by crushing the louse, not by its bite.
  10. 10Rubbing eyes: Rubbing the eyes can inoculate B. recurrentis from crushed lice into mucous membranes.
  11. 11Person suffering recurring nightmare: The reservoir host is humans (unlike tick-borne relapsing fever, which has animal reservoirs).
  12. 12Flame bandana hanging from spinning mobile: Relapsing fever causes cyclical high fevers with headache, chills, sweats, myalgia, arthralgia and nausea, with each episode ending in a two-part crisis phase.⭐ Fever recurs every 1-2 weeks as new antigenic variants emerge.
  13. 13"Crisis Part 1 - Fire Frenzy": Crisis phase part 1 is the "hot" phase: hyperthermia, tachycardia and hypertension.
  14. 14"Crisis Part 2 - Cold Crash": Crisis phase part 2 is the "cold" phase: hypothermia and hypotension (can be life-threatening).
  15. 15Yellow pajamas: Louse-borne relapsing fever can cause jaundice from hemolysis and hepatic involvement during spirochetemia.
  16. 16Fallen white plates: Louse-borne relapsing fever can cause thrombocytopenia (platelet consumption/destruction and marrow suppression during spirochetemia).
  17. 17Purple pencil: Treatment is penicillin G or doxycycline; monitor for the Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction, an acute febrile reaction (fever, chills, myalgias, hypotension) occurring within hours of therapy from rapid spirochete lysis.⭐ Jarisch-Herxheimer is common in LBRF, more so than in Lyme disease.
  18. 18Bicycle: Treatment is penicillin G or doxycycline (a single dose of doxycycline or erythromycin is often used in LBRF); monitor for the Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction.

12. Brucella spp

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: farmer/vet/abattoir worker or traveler with undulating fever, night sweats, and arthralgias after raw dairy or animal exposure
  • Gram-negative coccobacillus, small, aerobic, non-motile, facultative intracellular; culture (blood/bone marrow) with prolonged incubation, plus serology
  • Zoonosis: B. abortus (cattle), B. suis (pigs), B. melitensis (goats/sheep; most severe), B. canis (dogs)
  • Hepatosplenomegaly, sacroiliitis/vertebral osteomyelitis, epididymo-orchitis; relapses are common
  • Treatment: doxycycline + rifampin (or doxycycline + streptomycin/gentamicin) for at least 6 weeks; prevent with pasteurization and animal vaccination
  1. 1Bruce farms: Brucella spp. are small gram-negative coccobacilli (Bruce farms = Brucella). They are non-motile, non-spore-forming aerobes.
  2. 2Red barn: Brucella are gram-negative coccobacilli, so they stain pink-red (red barn). Their lipopolysaccharide-containing outer membrane helps them evade host defenses.
  3. 3Cow: Brucellosis is a zoonosis acquired through direct contact with infected animals such as cows, pigs, sheep, and goats. B. abortus is the classic cattle species.⭐ Occupational risk: farmers, veterinarians, slaughterhouse workers, and lab personnel (aerosol).
  4. 4Pig: Brucellosis is a zoonosis spread by direct contact with infected animals such as cows, pigs, sheep, and goats. B. suis is the pig species; B. melitensis (goats/sheep) causes the most severe disease.
  5. 5Milk bucket: A major route of transmission is ingestion of unpasteurized dairy products (milk, soft cheese) from infected animals. Pasteurization prevents this route.
  6. 6Open gate: Brucella are facultative intracellular bacteria that survive and replicate inside host macrophages. This explains granuloma formation, chronic/relapsing disease, and the need for prolonged combination therapy with drugs that penetrate cells.
  7. 7Sweating + thin farmer: Acute brucellosis presents with nonspecific symptoms: fever, night sweats, malaise, anorexia, and weight loss.
  8. 8Undulating hills: Brucellosis causes undulant fever, with waves of fever that rise and fall over time (also called Malta fever).⭐ Undulant fever + raw milk/farm animals = Brucella.
  9. 9Liver + spleen spots: Brucella localizes in the reticuloendothelial system (liver, spleen, lymph nodes, bone marrow), commonly causing hepatosplenomegaly and sometimes granulomas.
  10. 10Fish bones: Musculoskeletal involvement is the most common focal complication, especially sacroiliitis, spondylitis, and osteomyelitis. Epididymo-orchitis can also occur.
  11. 11Windmill wheel: Treatment requires combination therapy for several weeks (at least 6): doxycycline plus rifampin, or doxycycline plus streptomycin (gentamicin is an alternative aminoglycoside). Monotherapy risks relapse.
  12. 12Rifle: Combination therapy is used because the intracellular location and relapses make single agents inadequate: doxycycline + rifampin, or doxycycline + streptomycin.

13. Burkholderia spp.

  1. 1Burkhold burglar: Burkholderia spp. bacteria are gram-negative.
  2. 2Red rod-shaped roof: Burkholderia spp. bacteria are gram-negative bacilli.
  3. 3Cat: Burkholderia spp. bacteria produce catalase (i.e., are catalase-positive), an enzyme that converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen.
  4. 4Bellows: Burkholderia spp. bacteria are obligately aerobic pathogens.
  5. 5Blue ring: Burkholderia spp. bacteria produce oxidase (i.e., are oxidase-positive), an enzyme that plays a vital role in aerobic respiration.
  6. 6Muddy bath: Burkholderia spp. bacteria are transmitted through direct contact with contaminated soil or water.
  7. 7Running water faucet: Peak incidence of Burkholderia spp. infection occurs during the wet season, reflecting environmental exposure to contaminated soil or water.
  8. 8Inside near the melody: Burkholderia pseudomallei causes melioidosis, which can present acutely with pneumonia, sepsis, and multiple organ abscesses (commonly liver and spleen), or chronically with skin lesions, localized abscesses, and low-grade fever.
  9. 9Melody: Burkholderia pseudomallei causes melioidosis, which can present acutely with pneumonia, sepsis, and multiple organ abscesses (commonly liver and spleen), or chronically with skin lesions, localized abscesses, and low-grade fever.
  10. 10Palm tree: Melioidosis is seen mostly in tropical areas (e.g., Southeast Asia, South Asia, northern Australia).
  11. 11Lung armor plates near melody: Burkholderia pseudomallei can cause pneumonia.
  12. 12TB cactus with holes: Melioidosis may present like tuberculosis, with or without cavitary lung lesions.
  13. 13Outside palace complex: Burkholderia cepacia complex (BCC) can also cause pneumonia.
  14. 14Lung armor plates outside palace: Burkholderia cepacia complex (BCC) can also cause pneumonia.
  15. 15Crutches: Burkholderia cepacia complex commonly affects immunocompromised individuals (e.g., cystic fibrosis patients, chronic granulomatous disease patients).
  16. 16Sappy tree branch: BCC commonly infects cystic fibrosis patients, causing chronic lung infections and sometimes leading to "cepacia syndrome," a life-threatening necrotizing pneumonia associated with severe, acute respiratory distress and bacteremia (bacteria in the bloodstream).
  17. 17Red ball lantern lights: Burkholderia spp. can cause extrapulmonary manifestations, including encephalomyelitis, as well as skin and visceral abscesses.
  18. 18Pills: Burkholderia spp. commonly exhibit multidrug resistance, making infections difficult to treat; therapy is guided by susceptibility testing and may include TMP-SMX, ceftazidime, carbapenems (meropenem or doripenem), minocycline, or piperacillin-tazobactam.

14. Campylobacter jejuni

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: bloody diarrhea with fever and cramping after undercooked chicken or contact with poultry/animals.
  • Curved (comma/S or seagull-wing), oxidase-positive gram-negative rods; microaerophilic and grow at 42°C on Campylobacter (Skirrow) agar.
  • Treatment: usually supportive; macrolides (azithromycin) if severe or prolonged.
  • Complications: Guillain-Barre syndrome (most common antecedent infection), reactive arthritis (HLA-B27).
  • Most common cause of bacterial diarrhea in the US; mimics Salmonella/Shigella (also causes bloody diarrhea).
  1. 1Camping bears: Campylobacter jejuni is a gram-negative bacillus and one of the most common causes of bacterial gastroenteritis worldwide, including in the United States.
  2. 2Red rod-shaped cooler: C. jejuni is a gram-negative bacillus (pink rods on Gram stain), with a thin peptidoglycan layer and an outer membrane containing LPS. It is motile with polar flagella.
  3. 3Fire: C. jejuni is thermophilic: it grows optimally at about 42°C (selective culture is incubated at 42°C under microaerophilic conditions on Campylobacter agar). The heat explains its association with the intestinal tract of warm-bodied birds (poultry body temperature is about 41-42°C).⭐ Remember Campylobacter grows at 42°C, microaerophilic, on Campy (Skirrow) agar.
  4. 4Chicken: C. jejuni resides in the intestinal tract of animals, especially poultry, and transmission to humans occurs via the fecal-oral route, often by undercooked chicken, contamination during slaughter/food preparation, or unpasteurized milk. Common reservoirs include poultry, cattle, sheep, and dogs.
  5. 5Red stool: Symptoms include cramping, abdominal pain, fever, and diarrhea, which is often bloody. If bacteremia occurs, patients may develop systemic signs such as high fever, chills, malaise, hypotension, and confusion. The illness is usually self-limited (about a week); treat supportively, with a macrolide such as azithromycin for severe disease.
  6. 6Curved mustache: C. jejuni is a curved bacillus: comma-shaped or S-shaped, often described as a "seagull wing" on microscopy, with darting corkscrew motility.
  7. 7Blue oxidase ring: C. jejuni is oxidase positive: it produces oxidase, an enzyme in the aerobic respiratory chain. Together with the curved shape, this resembles Vibrio, but Campylobacter is microaerophilic and grows at 42°C. It is also catalase positive.
  8. 8Bear invading cooler: C. jejuni invades the epithelial cells of the gastrointestinal tract (ileum, jejunum and colon), penetrating the mucosa and causing inflammatory, often bloody diarrhea. In some cases it enters the bloodstream, resulting in bacteremia.
  9. 9Slapping knee: Campylobacter infection is associated with reactive arthritis (formerly Reiter syndrome), a seronegative spondyloarthropathy with joint pain and swelling occurring one to four weeks after certain infections. It is associated with HLA-B27 and the classic triad of arthritis, urethritis and conjunctivitis ("can't see, can't pee, can't climb a tree").⭐ Reactive arthritis: HLA-B27, after Campylobacter, Salmonella, Shigella, Yersinia, or Chlamydia.
  10. 10Guy and the bears: Campylobacter infection is the most common antecedent infection of Guillain-Barre syndrome, a post-infectious autoimmune disorder in which antibodies cross-react with peripheral nerve gangliosides (molecular mimicry between bacterial LPS and GM1), causing demyelination of peripheral nerves. It presents 1-3 weeks later with progressive, bilateral, ascending weakness and areflexia.
  11. 11Sausage links around ankle: Guillain-Barre syndrome classically starts with weakness and paresthesias in the legs (beginning at the ankles/feet) and ascends, with areflexia. CSF shows albuminocytologic dissociation (high protein, normal cell count). Treat with IVIG or plasmapheresis and monitor respiratory function (FVC) because the diaphragm can be paralyzed.⭐ GBS = ascending paralysis after a diarrheal illness (Campylobacter) with high CSF protein and normal WBC.

15. Candida albicans

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: white scrapable thrush, curd-like vaginal discharge with itching, diaper rash with satellite lesions, esophagitis (CD4 <200), or candidemia in neutropenic or IV-catheter patients.
  • Microbiology: opportunistic yeast with pseudohyphae and budding; forms germ tubes at 37 C; normal flora of mouth, gut, and vagina; catalase positive.
  • Diagnosis: KOH prep showing budding yeast with pseudohyphae; germ tube test; culture; vaginal pH remains normal (4-4.5).
  • Treatment: nystatin or clotrimazole (topical/oral), fluconazole for most infections; echinocandins for severe/invasive disease; amphotericin B for refractory cases.
  • Buzzwords: 'germ tube', 'pseudohyphae', 'thrush', 'cottage cheese', 'satellite lesions', 'esophagitis AIDS-defining', 'CGD', 'neutropenia'.
  1. 1Canada: 'Can-dida': Candida albicans, an opportunistic fungus that causes both cutaneous and systemic infections.
  2. 2Dimorphic butterfly: C. albicans is dimorphic but differs from the classic dimorphic fungi: it grows as yeast at 23-25 C and forms filamentous (pseudohyphal/hyphal) growth at 37 C, the reverse of the usual temperature pattern.⭐ Classic dimorphs are mold at 25 C and yeast at 37 C.
  3. 3'37 flavors' + germ tube straw: C. albicans forms germ tubes (the start of hyphal growth) when incubated in serum at 37 C.⭐ Germ tube test is positive for C. albicans.
  4. 4'20 C' + yeast snowballs: At 23-25 C, C. albicans exists as yeast that reproduces by budding.
  5. 5Catalase cat: C. albicans is catalase positive (converts hydrogen peroxide to water and oxygen), so patients with chronic granulomatous disease are susceptible to infection.⭐ Catalase-positive organisms in CGD: S. aureus, Candida, Aspergillus, Nocardia, Serratia, Burkholderia.
  6. 6Open mouth + tube slide: Candida species are normal flora of the GI tract and oral cavity and can commonly contaminate sputum cultures, so isolation from sputum often reflects colonization.
  7. 7Baby in red swing: C. albicans commonly causes candidal diaper dermatitis, thriving in warm, moist skin folds, with erythematous, beefy-red, confluent plaques and satellite pustules or papules around the main rash.
  8. 8Immunocompromised cane: Immunocompromised patients, particularly those with neutropenia, are at higher risk of disseminated candidiasis.⭐ Neutropenia = disseminated candidiasis; T-cell defects = mucocutaneous disease.
  9. 9Snow on tongue + inhaler: C. albicans causes oral candidiasis (thrush) with white, scrapable plaques on the oral mucosa (leukoplakia does not scrape off). It is common with inhaled corticosteroids and immunocompromise.⭐ Rinse mouth after inhaled steroids.
  10. 10Shoveling snow: Oral thrush plaques can be scraped off (shoveled), leaving an erythematous base; this distinguishes thrush from non-scrapable leukoplakia, including hairy leukoplakia.
  11. 11KOH salt: KOH mount of scrapings from oral, vaginal, or skin lesions shows budding yeast and pseudohyphae.⭐ KOH dissolves human cells, leaving fungal elements.
  12. 12Snow inside tube slide: C. albicans can cause esophageal candidiasis in immunocompromised patients, especially with HIV/AIDS, with gray-white pseudomembranes, odynophagia, and dysphagia.⭐ Linear white plaques on endoscopy.
  13. 13'Max 200 LBS': Esophageal candidiasis is an AIDS-defining illness, most often seen when the CD4+ count is below 200 cells/mm3.
  14. 14Snowball thrown in crotch: C. albicans causes vulvovaginal candidiasis with intense pruritus and thick, white, curd-like ('cottage cheese') vaginal discharge.
  15. 15Candy: Vulvovaginal candidiasis can be precipitated by uncontrolled diabetes, antibiotic use, and hormonal changes (e.g., oral contraceptives). Hyperglycemia increases glucose in tissues and mucosal secretions, feeding Candida overgrowth.
  16. 16Medication bottles: Vulvovaginal candidiasis can be precipitated by uncontrolled diabetes, antibiotic use, and hormonal changes (e.g., oral contraceptives). Antibiotics kill protective vaginal flora (lactobacilli), allowing Candida to overgrow.
  17. 17Birth control pills: Vulvovaginal candidiasis can be precipitated by uncontrolled diabetes, antibiotic use, and hormonal changes (e.g., oral contraceptives). Estrogen increases glycogen in vaginal epithelial cells, which Candida uses as a nutrient.
  18. 18'Playground open until 4 pH': In vulvovaginal candidiasis the vaginal pH stays normal (about 3.8-4.5) because Candida tolerates acid and does not raise pH.⭐ Contrast with bacterial vaginosis and trichomoniasis (pH >4.5).
  19. 193 heart roofs: C. albicans can cause endocarditis, mostly of the tricuspid valve in IV drug users (contaminated venous blood reaches the right heart first), and also in patients with prosthetic valves or indwelling catheters.
  20. 20Pinecone: Azoles, especially fluconazole, are the antifungals of choice for a broad range of Candida albicans infections.⭐ Fluconazole is first-line for oropharyngeal, esophageal, and vaginal candidiasis.
  21. 21Amphibians: Amphotericin B is used for severe or fluconazole-resistant Candida infections (amphibian = ampho).
  22. 22'Play NYCE': Nystatin is effective for cutaneous and oral Candida infections (swish and swallow for thrush); it is too toxic for systemic use.
  23. 23Cap: Echinocandins (caspofungin, micafungin) are options for severe or refractory candidiasis, including esophageal and systemic disease; 'cap' = caspofungin.⭐ Echinocandins are first-line for candidemia.
Show the original legend picture

16. Cestodes

Exam snapshot
  • Taenia solium (pork): eggs cause cysticercosis/neurocysticercosis (seizures, 'Swiss cheese' brain CT); undercooked pork gives intestinal taeniasis.
  • Taenia saginata (beef): intestinal tapeworm only, no hooks, no cysticercosis; praziquantel treats the adult worm.
  • Diphyllobothrium latum (fish): raw freshwater fish, B12 deficiency with megaloblastic anemia, up to 10 m long.
  • Echinococcus granulosus (dog): hydatid liver cyst with eggshell calcification; spillage causes anaphylaxis; albendazole plus surgery.
  • Treatment: praziquantel for adult intestinal tapeworms; albendazole (plus steroids and antiepileptics) for neurocysticercosis and hydatid cysts.
  1. 1Tents: Taenia species are cestodes (tapeworms) that infect humans. Two species matter: T. saginata (beef) and T. solium (pork).
  2. 2Sagging tent: Taenia saginata is the beef tapeworm. Its scolex has suckers but no hooks, and humans get only the adult intestinal worm (taeniasis) from undercooked beef.
  3. 3Cow: Cattle are the intermediate hosts for T. saginata and harbor the larval cysts (cysticerci) in muscle. Humans are the definitive host, harboring the adult worm in the intestine.
  4. 4Sun + "SOLD": Taenia solium is the pork tapeworm. It causes intestinal taeniasis (adult worm) and, if eggs are ingested, cysticercosis.
  5. 5Pig: Pigs are the intermediate hosts for T. solium and carry the larval cysts (cysticerci) in muscle. Eating undercooked pork gives humans the adult intestinal worm.
  6. 6Meat hooks: The scolex of T. solium has a rostellum with two rows of large and small hooks plus suckers, which anchor it to the intestinal wall. T. saginata lacks hooks.
  7. 7Pigs on carousel: Ingestion of T. solium eggs (not the cysts in meat) causes cysticercosis, including neurocysticercosis, an infection of the CNS. It is the most common cause of acquired epilepsy in endemic areas.
  8. 8Poop water: Humans ingest eggs from feces of a person carrying the adult worm, via contaminated food or water (fecal-oral), producing cysticercosis. Eating cyst-containing undercooked pork instead causes intestinal taeniasis. A tapeworm carrier can autoinfect.
  9. 9Motion lines: Neurocysticercosis commonly presents with seizures from cysts in brain parenchyma. It may also cause headache, hydrocephalus or raised intracranial pressure.
  10. 10Swiss cheese: CT/MRI shows multiple cystic lesions in the brain ('Swiss cheese'), well-defined, round and hypodense, some with a hyperdense dot (the scolex). Surrounding edema or ring enhancement occurs in the degenerating stage, and old lesions calcify.⭐ Multiple ring-enhancing cysts with a central dot + seizures = neurocysticercosis.
  11. 11Egg-shaped cheese in poop water: Eggs hatch in the intestine, releasing larvae (oncospheres) that penetrate the gut wall, enter the bloodstream and disseminate to the brain, muscle and eye, forming cysticerci.
  12. 12Pretzel tent: Praziquantel treats intestinal taeniasis (T. saginata and T. solium adult worms) by increasing membrane permeability to calcium, causing paralysis and death of the worm.
  13. 13Strong man named ‘Al' + bendy metal bar: Neurocysticercosis is treated with albendazole (often with praziquantel), along with corticosteroids to blunt inflammation from dying cysts and antiepileptics for seizures. Without steroids, killing cysts can worsen symptoms.
  14. 14Bathroom: Diphyllobothrium latum is the fish tapeworm, found in cold-water regions (e.g., Scandinavia, Great Lakes, Alaska).
  15. 15Fish: Humans are infected by eating undercooked or raw freshwater fish (e.g., pike, salmon) containing larvae (plerocercoids). Think sushi or gefilte fish.
  16. 16"Must B12 to buy fireworks": The worm competes for vitamin B12 in the gut, causing B12 deficiency and megaloblastic anemia. This is the key complication of D. latum.⭐ Raw fish + megaloblastic anemia = Diphyllobothrium latum.
  17. 17Cobalt blue firework: The cobalt-blue firework is cobalamin (B12). Deficiency can also cause neurologic findings such as subacute combined degeneration, with elevated methylmalonic acid and homocysteine.
  18. 18Red firework blasts: The red blasts represent the resulting megaloblastic (macrocytic) anemia, with large RBCs and hypersegmented neutrophils.
  19. 19Long toilet paper trail: D. latum is the longest human tapeworm, reaching up to about 10 meters, with a very large number of proglottids.
  20. 20Toilet paper segments in mud: Diagnosis is by stool exam showing the characteristic operculated eggs and/or proglottid segments, which are wider than long.
  21. 21Pretzel + toilet paper trail: Praziquantel is first-line for D. latum infection and eliminates the adult tapeworm from the intestine. Niclosamide is an alternative.
  22. 22Nickel coin slot: Niclosamide (nickel coin) is an alternative that kills the adult worm in the gut. Where unavailable, praziquantel is used.
  23. 23Cocker Spaniel: Echinococcus granulosus is the dog tapeworm, causing hydatid disease. Dogs are the definitive hosts, and it is common in sheep-raising regions.
  24. 24Cocker Spaniel — 1st place: Dogs are the definitive hosts of E. granulosus and harbor the adult tapeworm in the intestine, shedding eggs in feces.
  25. 25Sheepdog — 2nd place: Sheep are the intermediate hosts for E. granulosus and harbor the larval forms (hydatid cysts). Humans are accidental intermediate hosts, a dead end.
  26. 26Dog poop: Humans are infected by ingesting eggs from dog feces (contact with dogs or contaminated food), not by eating infected meat.
  27. 27Egg: Hydatid liver cysts on CT appear as well-defined cysts with a calcified 'eggshell' rim, often with internal daughter cysts, which helps distinguish them from other hepatic cysts.
  28. 28Ribbon on liver spot: Eggs hatch in the gut, larvae enter the portal circulation and form slowly growing hydatid cysts, most often in the liver (then lung) and other organs. They may be asymptomatic for years.
  29. 29Crumbling ribbon + red and puffy face: Rupture or spillage of a hydatid cyst releases highly antigenic fluid and can trigger anaphylaxis (type I hypersensitivity). Spillage can also seed new cysts.⭐ Do not aspirate or biopsy a suspected hydatid cyst. Treat with albendazole plus surgery or PAIR.
  30. 30Slingshot kid + pink granules + orange-pink outfit: Eosinophilia can accompany E. granulosus infection, especially with cyst leakage or rupture, but may be mild or absent when cysts are intact. Eosinophils have bilobed nuclei and red-orange granules.

17. Chlamydia/Chlamydophila spp.

  1. 1Clams: Chlamydia spp. are gram-indeterminate bacteria with three clinically significant species: Chlamydia trachomatis (serovars D-K, L1-L3, and A-C), Chlamydophila pneumoniae, and Chlamydophila psittaci, each with distinct clinical presentations.
  2. 2Ship on island: Chlamydia spp. are obligate intracellular pathogens, requiring a host cell to replicate.
  3. 3White island: Chlamydia spp. exhibit weak gram-negative staining due to their unique cell wall structures and are often classified as gram-indeterminate.
  4. 4"No mermaids": The cell wall of Chlamydia spp. lacks muramic acid, a typical component of peptidoglycan.
  5. 5Pearls outside of clam: Elementary bodies are the infectious, non-replicating, extracellular form of Chlamydia, formed in the first of two developmental stages.
  6. 6Pearls inside of clam: Reticulate bodies are the intracellular, replicating form of Chlamydia, formed in the second of two developmental stages.
  7. 7Pearls outside of clam again: Following replication, Chlamydia reticulate bodies transform back into elementary bodies, which are then released to infect new host cells.
  8. 8Piles of pearls: Intracytoplasmic reticulate bodies of Chlamydia can be seen as intracytoplasmic inclusion bodies on microscopy.
  9. 9Pink gems: Giemsa stain can be used to visualize Chlamydia intracytoplasmic inclusion bodies.
  10. 10Gnats: Nucleic acid amplification tests (NAATs) are the gold standard for detecting Chlamydia infections.
  11. 11Sexy mermaid: Chlamydia trachomatis serovars D-K and L1-L3 cause sexually transmitted infections (STIs).
  12. 12Water leak: Infection with Chlamydia trachomatis serovars D-K often presents with watery or mucopurulent vaginal or urethral discharge.
  13. 13Uterus + fallopian tubes flag: STIs caused by Chlamydia trachomatis serovars D-K can lead to pelvic inflammatory disease (PID) if left untreated, which can result in infertility, ectopic pregnancy, and chronic pelvic pain.
  14. 14Mermaid baby: Chlamydia trachomatis serovars D-K can be vertically transmitted during childbirth, causing neonatal conjunctivitis or pneumonia.
  15. 15Mermaid baby + covered eyes: Chlamydia trachomatis serovars D-K can be vertically transmitted during childbirth, causing neonatal conjunctivitis that appears after 5 days of life with watery to mucopurulent discharge (versus N. gonorrhoeae, which presents within the first 2-5 days with thick, purulent discharge).
  16. 16Mermaid baby + clam bra: Chlamydia trachomatis serovars D-K can be vertically transmitted during childbirth, causing neonatal conjunctivitis or pneumonia, the latter characterized by a repetitive staccato cough, tachypnea, and absence of high fever, with onset typically 1-3 weeks after birth.
  17. 17Barnacle belt: Chlamydia trachomatis serovars L1-L3 cause lymphogranuloma venereum (LGV), an STI characterized by an initial painless genital ulcer followed by tender inguinal or femoral lymphadenopathy (often described as "buboes"), which may progress to chronic inflammation and lymphatic obstruction if untreated.
  18. 18Eye patches: Chlamydia trachomatis serovars A-C are the causative agents of trachoma, a chronic conjunctival infection that leads to repeated inflammation, scarring of the eyelid, and corneal damage, making it the leading cause of preventable infectious blindness.
  19. 19Hand near eye: Trachoma is predominantly transmitted through direct contact with eye and nasal discharge from infected individuals, and through contact with fomites such as contaminated towels and washcloths.
  20. 20Slapping knee: Reactive arthritis, which develops in response to infections caused by bacteria such as Chlamydia trachomatis (due to cross-reactivity between bacterial antigens and host tissues, causing the immune system to mistakenly attack the eyes, urethra, and joints), is characterized by the classic triad of uveitis (or conjunctivitis), urethritis, and arthritis.
  21. 21Can't see: Reactive arthritis, which develops in response to infections caused by certain bacteria such as Chlamydia trachomatis, is characterized by a triad of symptoms: uveitis or conjunctivitis (inflammation of the eye causing redness, pain, and blurred vision), urethritis, and arthritis.
  22. 22Can't pee: Reactive arthritis, which develops in response to infections caused by certain bacteria such as Chlamydia trachomatis, is characterized by a triad of symptoms: uveitis, urethritis (inflammation of the urethra causing dysuria or discharge), and arthritis.
  23. 23Can't climb tree: Reactive arthritis, which develops in response to infections caused by certain bacteria such as Chlamydia trachomatis, is characterized by a triad of symptoms: uveitis, urethritis, and arthritis (asymmetric inflammatory joint pain typically affecting the knees, ankles, or feet).
  24. 24Algae on bra: Chlamydophila pneumoniae is associated with atypical pneumonia, typically causing a mild, slowly progressive respiratory illness with low-grade fever, nonproductive cough, and malaise, often in older children and young adults.
  25. 25Parrot pooping on bra: Chlamydophila psittaci, transmitted from infected birds (e.g., parrots, pigeons, poultry), can cause atypical pneumonia characterized by fever, nonproductive cough, headache, and myalgias; this condition is also known as psittacosis.
  26. 26Macrolide crows: Macrolide antibiotics, especially azithromycin, are effective in treating chlamydial infections (oral administration is required for neonatal conjunctivitis to ensure systemic treatment).
  27. 27Bicycle tire wheel: Tetracycline antibiotics, particularly doxycycline, are effective in treating chlamydial infections.
  28. 28Mac's detective hat: The standard treatment for suspected chlamydial infection often presumes a Neisseria gonorrhoeae co-infection; ceftriaxone targets Neisseria gonorrhoeae, while azithromycin or doxycycline targets Chlamydia trachomatis.
  29. 293 axes flag: The standard treatment for suspected chlamydial infection often presumes a Neisseria gonorrhoeae co-infection; ceftriaxone targets Neisseria gonorrhoeae, while azithromycin or doxycycline targets Chlamydia trachomatis.

18. Clostridioides difficile

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: hospital-acquired watery diarrhea after antibiotics (clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, cephalosporins, penicillins) disrupting normal flora.
  • Gram-positive, spore-forming, obligate anaerobic rod; spores resist heat, alcohol-based sanitizers, and drying.
  • Toxin A (enterotoxin) and Toxin B (cytotoxin; depolymerizes actin) cause pseudomembranous colitis with yellow-grey plaques.
  • Diagnosis: stool NAAT/PCR for toxin genes or EIA for toxins A/B (often GDH + toxin algorithm).
  • Treatment: oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin first-line; IV metronidazole only adjunctive in fulminant disease (with PO/PR vancomycin).
  • Prevention: soap-and-water handwashing (not alcohol gel), contact precautions, stop the offending antibiotic.
  1. 1Walnuts: Spores are metabolically inactive and highly resistant to heat, radiation, and chemical agents (including alcohol-based hand sanitizers). They persist on hospital surfaces and drive transmission.⭐ Alcohol gel does not kill spores; use soap and water.
  2. 2"Clean" sign with washing hands over chocolate machine: C. difficile is primarily nosocomial; spores spread patient to patient via contaminated hands and surfaces (fecal-oral). Antibiotics, especially clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, cephalosporins, and penicillins, disrupt normal gut flora, letting C. difficile overgrow and produce toxins that cause diarrhea.
  3. 3Workstation A and workstation B: C. difficile produces two major exotoxins, Toxin A (TcdA) and Toxin B (TcdB), which are responsible for disease. Both are glucosyltransferases that inactivate Rho GTPases in colonocytes.
  4. 4"A - Apples" sign: Toxin A is the enterotoxin: it binds the brush border of intestinal epithelial cells, causing fluid secretion, inflammation, and mucosal damage with watery diarrhea.
  5. 5Employee picking up the apple and brushing chocolate on them: Toxin A acts on the intestinal lining (apples brushed with chocolate): epithelial cell damage and inflammation lead to cell death and secretory watery diarrhea.
  6. 6Chocolate filled machine: Toxin A is mainly enterotoxic: it drives fluid secretion and mucosal injury, producing the watery diarrhea of early disease.
  7. 7"B - Black Licorice" sign: Toxin B is the cytotoxin: it depolymerizes actin filaments in enterocytes, causing cell rounding and death. Mucosal necrosis forms a yellowish-grey pseudomembrane of fibrin, mucus, and dead cells, characteristic of pseudomembranous colitis.⭐ Pseudomembranes seen on colonoscopy are the classic finding.
  8. 8Black Licorice: Toxin B (black licorice) disrupts the enterocyte actin cytoskeleton by depolymerizing actin, causing cell rounding and death.
  9. 9Little kid chewing them apart: Toxin B damage to the cytoskeleton (kid chewing licorice apart) causes enterocyte rounding, loss of the cell layer, and mucosal necrosis.
  10. 10Worker packing the licorice in sheets of yellowish plastic: Mucosal necrosis from toxin B yields the yellowish-grey pseudomembrane (yellow plastic sheets) of fibrin, mucus, and dead cells that defines pseudomembranous colitis. Severe disease can progress to toxic megacolon.
  11. 11Worker holding up chocolate covered shoe: Diagnose by stool testing for toxigenic strains: NAAT/PCR (detects toxin-encoding genes) or EIA for toxins A and B. Test only symptomatic patients with unformed stools.
  12. 12Gas mask: C. difficile is an obligate anaerobe (cannot grow in oxygen-rich environments), consistent with infection in the anaerobic colon.
  13. 13Violet: C. difficile is a gram-positive bacillus (violet on Gram stain) and a spore former.
  14. 14Van with the mouth like opening: Oral vancomycin is first-line therapy (fidaxomicin is an equally accepted first-line option). It stays in the gut lumen with minimal systemic absorption, directly reaching bacteria and toxins.⭐ IV vancomycin does not reach the colonic lumen and is useless for C. difficile.
  15. 15Train: IV metronidazole is used for severe or fulminant infection alongside vancomycin, as it achieves systemic levels that reach inflamed colonic tissue, complementing local gut-directed therapy.
