19. Clostridium botulinum

  1. 1Robots: Clostridium botulinum is a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple hues: Clostridium botulinum is a gram-positive bacillus.
  3. 3Tin can: The anaerobic conditions in improperly canned food can promote the germination and growth of Clostridium botulinum spores.
  4. 4Nuts: Clostridium botulinum can form metabolically inactive spores, which are resistant to various environmental stresses, including heat, radiation, and chemicals.
  5. 5Anaerobic gas mask: Clostridium botulinum is an obligately anaerobic bacterium.
  6. 6Limp neck: Botulism typically starts with cranial nerve dysfunction, often referred to as "bulbar symptoms," and progresses to symmetrical descending weakness affecting the trunk, limbs, and smooth muscles, culminating in flaccid paralysis.
  7. 7Droopy eyelids: Botulism typically starts with cranial nerve dysfunction, manifesting as "bulbar symptoms" such as ptosis (drooping of the eyelids) and diplopia (double vision).
  8. 8Cutting ACh current: Botulinum toxin is a protease that targets SNARE proteins, leading to inhibition of acetylcholine release from presynaptic motor neurons, which causes flaccid paralysis.
  9. 9Floppy baby robot: Infant botulism, often referred to as "floppy baby syndrome," results from ingestion of spores that colonize the underdeveloped GI tract of infants, leading to toxin production in vivo; in contrast, adults usually contract botulism from ingestion of preformed toxin.
  10. 10Nuts in honey: Honey can contain Clostridium botulinum spores, which is why it is recommended to avoid giving honey to infants younger than 12 months of age.

20. Clostridium perfringens

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: dirty wound (trauma, war injury) with severe pain, crepitus, and rapidly spreading myonecrosis (gas gangrene)
  • Large gram-positive rod, spore-forming, anaerobic; box-car shaped; alpha toxin (lecithinase/phospholipase C) is the key virulence factor
  • Blood agar shows a double zone of hemolysis (inner theta toxin, outer alpha toxin)
  • Treatment: surgical debridement plus penicillin G (often with clindamycin)
  • Also causes food poisoning (meat/gravy; watery diarrhea, self-limited)
  1. 1Private Ringen: Clostridium perfringens is a large gram-positive rod, spore-forming and non-motile.
  2. 2Purple hues: C. perfringens is gram-positive (purple on Gram stain); it is a bacillus.
  3. 3Wounded soldier: C. perfringens infection occurs in wounds contaminated with soil or fecal matter, historically in battlefield injuries, and also with trauma, surgical wounds, or ischemic limbs.
  4. 4Spored walnuts: C. perfringens forms endospores, which are metabolically inactive and resistant to heat, radiation, and chemicals, allowing survival in harsh environments and in poorly cooked food.
  5. 5Soil: Spores persist in soil (and in the human and animal GI tract), resisting heat, radiation, desiccation, and disinfectants, which is how wounds and food get contaminated.
  6. 6Anaerobic gas mask: C. perfringens is an obligate (aerotolerant) anaerobe, so it grows in devitalized, poorly oxygenated tissue such as necrotic wounds.
  7. 7Gas vapor: C. perfringens causes necrotizing soft tissue infection with myonecrosis and gas formation (gas gangrene): severe pain, crepitus on palpation, foul discharge, and rapid spread with systemic toxicity.⭐ Crepitus + dirty wound = gas gangrene.
  8. 8Alpha flag + phospholipid clothespins: Alpha toxin is a lecithinase (phospholipase C) that cleaves lecithin (phosphatidylcholine) in cell membranes, causing myonecrosis and red cell hemolysis. Hemolysis and tissue destruction impair oxygen delivery.
  9. 9Lysed tomatoes: Alpha toxin (lecithinase) lyses red cells, causing intravascular hemolysis that impairs oxygen delivery to tissues; this contributes to anemia, jaundice, and renal failure in severe cases.
  10. 10"DOUBLE fine zone": On blood agar, C. perfringens produces a characteristic double zone of hemolysis: complete hemolysis in the inner zone (theta toxin) and incomplete hemolysis in the outer zone (alpha toxin).⭐ Double zone of hemolysis = C. perfringens.
  11. 11Purple pencil: Treatment is urgent surgical debridement (amputation if needed) plus antibiotics; penicillin G is preferred, usually with clindamycin (to suppress toxin production).
  12. 12"Slow" Sign in Muddy water: C. perfringens enterotoxin causes food poisoning from improperly cooked or stored foods (meats, gravy), with watery diarrhea and cramps and little vomiting or fever. Sketchy says onset is slow, within 2-12 h (classically 8-16 h); it is self-limited.⭐ Spores survive cooking, and enterotoxin is released in the gut during sporulation.
Show the original legend picture

21. Clostridium tetani

  1. 1Monkey testing lab: Clostridium tetani is a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple hues: Clostridium tetani is a gram-positive bacillus.
  3. 3Anaerobic gas mask: Clostridium tetani is an obligately anaerobic bacterium.
  4. 4Spored walnuts: Clostridium tetani can form metabolically inactive spores, which are resistant to various environmental stresses, including heat, radiation, and chemicals.
  5. 5Barbed wire: Clostridium tetani infection is classically associated with puncture wounds from rusty nails and barbed wire.
  6. 6Rusty nails: Clostridium tetani infection is classically associated with puncture wounds from rusty nails and barbed wire.
  7. 7Soil: Clostridium tetani spores are widely distributed in soil.
  8. 8Evil grin: The classic clinical manifestations of tetanus include trismus, a spastic paralysis of the jaw muscles (lockjaw), and risus sardonicus, a sustained contraction of the facial muscles that results in an "evil smile" appearance.
  9. 9Arched back: Spasmodic contractions in tetanus can involve the neck and back, leading to opisthotonos, characterized by backward arching of the head, neck, and spine.
  10. 10Retrograde pulley: The tetanus toxin, tetanospasmin, is transported retrogradely along axons to the spinal cord and the brainstem.
  11. 11Cutting SNARE trap: Tetanospasmin functions as a protease that targets SNARE proteins, which are essential for the fusion of synaptic vesicles with the neuronal membrane and for neurotransmitter release.
  12. 12Trapped "G&G" researcher: Tetanospasmin cleaves SNARE proteins in Renshaw cells (inhibitory interneurons in the spinal cord), leading to reduced release of GABA and glycine (inhibitory neurotransmitters), which causes muscle spasms, spastic paralysis, and hyperreflexia.
  13. 13Wrench and saw: Tetanospasmin cleaves SNARE proteins in Renshaw cells (inhibitory interneurons in the spinal cord), leading to reduced release of GABA and glycine (inhibitory neurotransmitters), which causes muscle spasms, spastic paralysis, and hyperreflexia.
  14. 14Orange toxoid syringe: Tetanus prevention involves administration of the tetanus vaccine, which is composed of an inactivated form of the tetanus toxin (toxoid); this vaccine is often co-administered with diphtheria toxoid and acellular pertussis vaccine as DTaP for infants and young children and Tdap for older children and adults.

22. Coccidioides immitis

  1. 1"Presidio San Joaquin": Coccidioides immitis is a dimorphic fungus that causes San Joaquin Valley fever.
  2. 2Southwest US map: Coccidioides immitis is endemic to the arid regions of the southwestern United States and northern Mexico, thriving in desert soils.
  3. 3Breathing in dust: Coccidioides immitis infection occurs through the inhalation of airborne arthroconidia (mold spores).
  4. 4Cracks in earth: Coccidioides immitis arthroconidia (mold spores) can become aerosolized when soil is disturbed by activities such as construction, farming, natural events (e.g., earthquakes and dust storms).
  5. 5Dimorphic butterfly: Coccidioides immitis exhibits dimorphism, growing as mold in cooler environments (25–30°C) and as yeast at human body temperature (35–37°C).
  6. 6Tumbleweeds: In tissue, Coccidioides immitis appears as large, yeast-like spherules containing endospores, which are significantly larger than red blood cells.
  7. 7Small red hat next to tumbleweed: In tissue, Coccidioides immitis appears as large, yeast-like spherules containing endospores, which are significantly larger than red blood cells.
  8. 8Coughing + sweating: The presentation of coccidioidomycosis ranges from asymptomatic to acute pneumonia, with cough, fever, chest pain, and arthralgia; diagnosis can be supported by KOH stain or culture and serologic testing (IgM for acute infection).
  9. 9Knee pain: The presentation of coccidioidomycosis ranges from asymptomatic to acute pneumonia, with cough, fever, chest pain, and arthralgia; diagnosis can be supported by KOH stain or culture and serologic testing (IgM for acute infection).
  10. 10Holes in lung wall: CXR in patients with primary coccidioidomycosis might reveal unilateral infiltrates, hilar lymphadenopathy, cavities or nodules; however, imaging can be normal in many cases.
  11. 11Protruding bricks in lung wall: CXR in patients with primary coccidioidomycosis might reveal unilateral infiltrates, hilar lymphadenopathy, cavities or nodules; however, imaging can be normal in many cases.
  12. 12Spotted leg wall: Erythema nodosum in coccidioidomycosis reflects a robust immune response to fungal antigens, causing inflammation of subcutaneous fat (panniculitis) and producing painful, red nodules typically distributed symmetrically on the shins.
  13. 13Immunocompromised cane: Extrapulmonary coccidioidomycosis (most commonly skin, bone, joint, and CNS involvement) primarily occurs in patients who are immunocompromised.
  14. 14Statue erosion over chest: Dissemination spreads from the lungs and may present with skin lesions, osteolytic bone lesions, and meningitis.
  15. 15Statue erosion over skin: Dissemination spreads from the lungs and may present with skin lesions (papules, pustules, or ulcers), osteolytic bone lesions, and meningitis.
  16. 16Exposed bone: Dissemination spreads from the lungs and may present with skin lesions, osteolytic bone lesions (dissemination to bone may present with localized pain, swelling, and sometimes tenderness over the affected bones), and meningitis.
  17. 17Neck brace: Dissemination spreads from the lungs and may present with skin lesions, osteolytic bone lesions, and meningitis (dissemination to the CNS and meninges may present with headache, fever, neck stiffness, photophobia, and altered mental status).
  18. 18Pinecones: Azoles like itraconazole or fluconazole are recommended for mild to moderate coccidioidomycosis.
  19. 19Amphibians: Amphotericin B is the treatment of choice for severe or disseminated coccidioidomycosis.

23. Coronavirus

  1. 1Crown: Coronaviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses in the Coronaviridae family.
  2. 2Warm colors: Coronaviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  3. 3Positive sun: Coronaviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 4Enveloped by robe: Coronaviruses are enveloped viruses (a lipid bilayer studded with glycoproteins).
  5. 5Spiraling road + helical trees: Coronaviruses have helical nucleocapsids, in which the N protein binds the RNA genome.
  6. 6King outside the castle: After entering the host cell, the coronavirus releases its RNA genome into the cytoplasm, where viral replication and protein synthesis occur.
  7. 7Sneezing and blowing nose: Low-pathogenicity coronaviruses usually cause mild upper respiratory infections like the common cold.
  8. 8Respiratory tract design pattern on chest: High-pathogenicity coronaviruses—Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus (SARS-CoV, the cause of SARS), Middle East Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus (MERS-CoV, the cause of MERS), and SARS-CoV-2 (the cause of COVID-19)—more often cause pneumonia involving the alveoli rather than just bronchial inflammation, though mild bronchitis-like symptoms may occur early, and in severe cases, the infection can progress to acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS).

24. Corynebacterium diphtheriae

  1. 1Bull: Corynebacterium diphtheriae (a gram-positive bacillus).
  2. 2Purple hues: Corynebacterium diphtheriae is a gram-positive bacillus.
  3. 3V + Y design on maracas: Corynebacterium diphtheriae typically forms a club shape in a V- or Y-shaped chains due to its unique snapping division during cellular replication.
  4. 4Red and blue maracas: The cytoplasm of Corynebacterium diphtheriae contains metachromatic granules that demonstrate a characteristic staining pattern with aniline dyes: the granules appear red, the remainder of the bacillus stains blue.
  5. 5Bowtie + elongated accordion: The diphtheria toxin produced by Corynebacterium diphtheriae employs a mechanism of ADP ribosylation to inhibit elongation factor-2 (EF-2), inhibiting protein synthesis; this mechanism is shared by exotoxin A produced by Pseudomonas aeruginosa.
  6. 6Grey cotton candy + plastic wrap: Exposure to the diphtheria toxin leads to cell necrosis and the formation of a dense, grey pseudomembrane over the tonsils and pharyngeal wall.
  7. 7Bull's neck: Severe laryngeal diphtheria can result in cervical edema and cervical lymphadenopathy, giving rise to the characteristic “bull neck” appearance.
  8. 8Water droplets: Corynebacterium diphtheriae is primarily transmitted via respiratory droplets.
  9. 9Heart cape: Systemic absorption of the diphtheria toxin can result in significant cardiac toxicity, most commonly manifesting as myocarditis and arrhythmias.
  10. 10Myelin sausages + back of throat: The diphtheria toxin can induce nerve damage, leading to demyelination and a paralysis that generally begins in the posterior pharyngeal wall and soft palate.
  11. 11Black “TELE”: Tellurite agar is a selective medium that allows the growth of Corynebacterium diphtheriae, which forms gray/black colonies due to the reduction of tellurite.
  12. 12Laughing: Corynebacterium diphtheriae can be cultivated on Loeffler's medium, which is used primarily to enhance the bacterium's morphological features, facilitating easier identification.
  13. 13Tongue lick: The ELEK test is utilized to differentiate toxigenic from non-toxigenic strains of Corynebacterium diphtheriae.
  14. 14Toxoid syringes: The vaccine against diphtheria consists of an inactivated form of the diphtheria exotoxin and is often given in combination with the tetanus toxoid and acellular pertussis vaccine (DTaP, DT, Tdap, and Td vaccines provide varying levels of protection against diphtheria, tetanus, and pertussis, catering to different age groups and needs).

25. Coxiella burnetii

  1. 1The curly Q on the ram represents Coxiella burnetii, a gram-negative, gram-variable bacterium that causes Q fever.
  2. 2The red barn represents the fact that Coxiella burnetii can exhibit atypical staining due to its unusual cell wall properties, leading to variability in Gram-staining results; despite this, it is generally classified as a gram-negative organism.
  3. 3The white fur represents that, unlike other similar zoonotic genera (e.g., Rickettsia, Ehrlichia, Orientia), Q fever typically does NOT present with a rash.
  4. 4The "Q" mark represents that Coxiella burnetii is the causative agent of Q fever, which typically presents with mild flu-like symptoms such as fever, headache, myalgia, and malaise, and can occasionally progress to atypical pneumonia or hepatitis.
  5. 5The locked indoors imagery represents that Coxiella burnetii is an obligate intracellular organism, surviving and multiplying within host cells, notably macrophages.
  6. 6The walnuts represent that Coxiella burnetii forms spore-like structures that are highly resistant to environmental stress, allowing it to survive in animal waste-including birth products, urine, feces, and milk-which can become aerosolized; inhalation of these contaminated particles is the primary route of human infection.
  7. 7The animal droppings represent that Coxiella burnetii forms spore-like structures that are highly resistant to environmental stress, allowing it to survive in animal waste-including birth products, urine, feces, and milk-which can become aerosolized; inhalation of these contaminated particles is the primary route of human infection.
  8. 8The inhaling of dust clouds represents that Coxiella burnetii forms spore-like structures that are highly resistant to environmental stress, allowing it to survive in animal waste-including birth products, urine, feces, and milk-which can become aerosolized; inhalation of these contaminated particles is the primary route of human infection.
  9. 9Hitting the head represents that Q fever commonly presents with nonspecific symptoms, including headache.
  10. 10Coughing represents that Q fever commonly presents with nonspecific symptoms, including a dry cough, high fever, and myalgia.
  11. 11Sweating is a consistent and defining symptom in the clinical presentation of Q fever.
  12. 12The cow represents that domestic farm animals, particularly cattle, sheep, and goats, are significant reservoirs for Coxiella burnetii; Q fever can be prevented by avoiding exposure to animal birth products and waste, using protective gear, pasteurizing milk, and vaccinating high-risk individuals (e.g., veterinarians).
  13. 13The liver-shaped spot represents that in chronic cases, Q fever can lead to granulomatous hepatitis, characterized by granulomatous inflammation in the liver, often presenting with mild elevation of liver enzymes and nonspecific systemic symptoms.
  14. 14The red fingers represent that in chronic cases, Q fever can lead to culture-negative endocarditis, which may present with nonspecific symptoms and peripheral signs of infective endocarditis, including splinter hemorrhages under the fingernails.

26. Coxsackievirus A & B

  1. 1Cockatoos: Coxsackievirus A and B are positive-sense RNA viruses in the Picornaviridae family.
  2. 2Warm colors: Coxsackieviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  3. 3Positive sun: Coxsackieviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 4Baby cockatoo: Coxsackieviruses are part of the Picornaviridae family.
  5. 5Statue of David: Coxsackieviruses lack envelopes, classifying them as naked viruses.
  6. 6"A" cage: Coxsackievirus A is a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family.
  7. 7Hand, foot, mouth red birdseed: Coxsackievirus A causes hand, foot, and mouth disease, with small (2–7 mm), red, vesicular, macular, or maculopapular lesions on the mouth, hands, and feet, sometimes ulcerative and symmetrical, non-itchy; it also causes herpangina (small vesicles or ulcers on the soft palate, tonsillar pillars, and uvula, often with sudden fever and sore throat); both are usually self-limited with treatment primarily supportive.
  8. 8Meningitis helmet: Coxsackievirus A (and B, less commonly) can cause aseptic meningitis—a non-bacterial inflammation of the meninges, especially in children—by crossing the blood–brain barrier to infect the meninges or brain tissue.
  9. 9Kid + bathing suit: Coxsackieviruses predominantly affect children, with a rise in cases during the warmer summer months.
  10. 10"B" cage: Coxsackievirus B is a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family.
  11. 11Floppy heart bags: Coxsackievirus B can cause myopericarditis—viral inflammation of the myocardium and pericardium—by directly infecting cardiomyocytes and triggering an immune response, leading to chest pain, fatigue, dyspnea, or arrhythmias; severe or chronic cases may progress to dilated cardiomyopathy and reduce cardiac output and potentially cause heart failure.
  12. 12Tight grip around chest: Coxsackievirus B causes Bornholm disease (epidemic pleurodynia or "devil's grip"), which is characterized by sudden, severe, often unilateral pleuritic chest or upper abdominal pain, sometimes with fever and muscle tenderness; it is usually self-limited, with treatment primarily supportive.

27. Cryptococcus neoformans

  1. 1The crypt represents Cryptococcus neoformans, an opportunistic fungus.
  2. 2The thick-walled sarcophagus represents that Cryptococcus neoformans has a thick polysaccharide capsule that inhibits phagocytosis; this repeating capsular antigen enhances virulence, provides antiphagocytic properties, and serves as a key diagnostic marker.
  3. 3The hexagon design represents that Cryptococcus neoformans has a thick polysaccharide capsule that inhibits phagocytosis; this repeating capsular antigen enhances virulence, provides antiphagocytic properties, and serves as a key diagnostic marker.
  4. 4The pigeon poop and archeologist coughing represent that Cryptococcus neoformans is commonly associated with pigeon droppings, and humans can become infected by inhaling dust contaminated with the fungus, which initially settles in the lungs, causing pulmonary infection, and may potentially disseminate to other organs, including the central nervous system.
  5. 5The ammonia bottle represents that Cryptococcus neoformans produces urease (i.e., urease positive), an enzyme that hydrolyzes urea into carbon dioxide and ammonia.
  6. 6The immunocompromised cane represents that Cryptococcus neoformans is an opportunistic fungus and predominantly infects immunocompromised individuals, such as those with HIV/AIDS.
  7. 7The archeologist coughing represents that Cryptococcus pulmonary infections can be asymptomatic; however, approximately half of patients have cough or chest pain. Symptoms may also include dyspnea and other pulmonary complications, and in immunocompromised individuals, the onset may be more severe.
  8. 8The neck brace and sweat represent that Cryptococcus can disseminate to the central nervous system, infecting the meninges and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), with meningoencephalitis as the most common manifestation, typically presenting with fever, malaise, headache, neck stiffness, nausea, vomiting, and photophobia, and cerebrospinal fluid analysis showing elevated opening pressure, lymphocytic pleocytosis, elevated protein, and low to normal glucose.
  9. 9The soap bubbles on head represent that cryptococcal meningoencephalitis may present as 'soap bubble' lesions on magnetic resonance imaging (MRI)-multiple, small, non-enhancing cystic lesions in the brain's gray matter, typically in the basal ganglia and periventricular regions, representing yeast-filled dilated perivascular (Virchow-Robin) spaces and indicating the cerebral impact of the disease.
  10. 10The red and silver staining represents that bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid, collected from the lower respiratory tract during bronchoscopy, can be stained with mucicarmine (red) or with methenamine (silver) to visualize the polysaccharide capsule and the yeast cell wall, allowing direct detection of the organism to diagnose pulmonary cryptococcosis.
  11. 11The light circles in black tar represent that India ink staining of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) obtained via lumbar puncture (LP) is a negative staining technique used to visualize encapsulated fungi such as Cryptococcus neoformans, in which the capsule appears as a clear halo around the yeast cell against a dark background, aiding in the diagnosis of cryptococcal meningitis.
  12. 12The latex gloves represent that the latex agglutination test detects the polysaccharide capsule antigen of Cryptococcus neoformans in serum and cerebrospinal fluid by using antibody-coated latex beads that clump (agglutinate) when the antigen is present, confirming infection.
  13. 13The soap bubbles on head represent that cryptococcal meningoencephalitis may present as 'soap bubble' lesions on magnetic resonance imaging (MRI)-multiple, small, non-enhancing cystic lesions in the brain's gray matter, typically in the basal ganglia and periventricular regions, representing yeast-filled dilated perivascular (Virchow-Robin) spaces and indicating the cerebral impact of the disease.
  14. 14The frogs and flute represent that initial treatment of cryptococcal meningitis involves a combination of amphotericin B and flucytosine, followed by long-term therapy with fluconazole.
  15. 15The pinecones represent that initial treatment of cryptococcal meningitis involves a combination of amphotericin B and flucytosine, followed by long-term therapy with fluconazole.

28. Cryptosporidium spp

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: watery, non-bloody diarrhea; self-limited in normal hosts but severe and chronic in AIDS (CD4 <100).
  • Diagnosis: oocysts in stool, acid-fast (modified Ziehl-Neelsen) pink-red on blue; antigen or PCR also used.
  • Transmission: fecal-oral; contaminated water (pools, lakes, municipal supply) or animal contact; oocysts resist chlorine.
  • Treatment: nitazoxanide for immunocompetent patients; in HIV, restore immunity with ART plus supportive care.
  • Classic association: outbreaks at swimming pools/daycares and in HIV patients with chronic watery diarrhea.
  1. 1Flooded crypt: Cryptosporidium spp. are intestinal protozoa that infect the small-bowel epithelium and cause watery diarrhea. Mnemonic: 'crypt' for Cryptosporidium, flooded because of the profuse watery diarrhea.
  2. 2Bubbles: Infectious oocysts are shed in stool and spread fecal-orally, typically by oocyst-contaminated water or food. They are environmentally hardy and survive for long periods.
  3. 3Multifaceted amethyst gems scattered in water: Oocysts contain four sporozoites and are small (about 4-6 micrometers). They are acid-fast or partially acid-fast on modified Ziehl-Neelsen stain (pink-red on a blue background), which allows stool microscopy diagnosis.
  4. 4Pink acid-fast poncho: Modified acid-fast staining of stool is the classic diagnostic test: pink-red oocysts stand out against the blue counterstain. Antigen detection or PCR can also be used.⭐ Acid-fast oocyst in stool + watery diarrhea = Cryptosporidium.
  5. 5Cowboy hat + wading through dirty water: Risk factors include drinking or swimming in contaminated water (pools, lakes, untreated water) and occupational or recreational contact with animals, especially cattle and calves. Daycare and swimming pool outbreaks are classic.
  6. 6Brown water: Cryptosporidium causes watery, non-bloody diarrhea. In immunocompetent hosts it is self-limited, usually resolving in 1-2 weeks.
  7. 7Immunocompromised cane: In immunocompromised patients, particularly advanced HIV (CD4 below about 100), it causes severe, chronic, profuse watery diarrhea with dehydration and weight loss, and may involve the biliary tract.
  8. 8Broken pipe + leaking brown water: After ingestion, sporozoites invade the epithelial cells of the GI tract (the apical enterocyte, within the brush border), leading to diarrhea through direct cellular damage and malabsorption/secretory changes. The infection is non-invasive beyond the epithelium, so stool is not bloody.
  9. 9Knitted sock: Nitazoxanide is approved for cryptosporidiosis in immunocompetent patients and offers variable benefit in immunocompromised patients.
  10. 10Sock filter: Prevention depends on water filtration, because oocysts are resistant to chlorine and standard chemical disinfection. Hand hygiene and avoiding swimming while having diarrhea also reduce spread.⭐ Chlorine-resistant oocysts explain pool and municipal water outbreaks.
  11. 11Spirit crows: Spiramycin (a macrolide) may relieve diarrhea in some immunocompromised patients, but the key to resolution is immune restoration (ART in HIV) plus supportive care and rehydration. Paromomycin is another agent sometimes used.check original

29. Cytomegalovirus (CMV)

No Sketchy video/scene available for this organism in the current deck — notes below are compiled directly from your Anki cards.

ID / Micro
  • Herpesviridae (HHV-5); dsDNA, linear, icosahedral, enveloped
  • Latent in mononuclear cells (e.g., macrophages); reactivates with immunosuppression
  • Binds cellular integrins (heparan sulfate); infects WBCs and blocks MHC I expression, inhibiting cytotoxic T-cell killing
  • Owl's eye intranuclear inclusion bodies on histology
Clinical — Immunocompromised
  • Mononucleosis with a negative monospot test
  • Retinitis (cotton-wool spots/"pizza pie" fundoscopy) in HIV patients with CD4+ < 50/mm³; also esophagitis (linear ulcerations) and colitis
  • Interstitial pneumonia, especially in HIV and organ transplant patients; detected via buffy coat culture; can cause cauda equina syndrome in HIV/AIDS
Congenital CMV (ToRCHHeS VP)
  • Most common congenital infection in the developed world; highest risk in the 1st trimester (can cause hydrops fetalis)
  • 80–90% of congenitally infected neonates are asymptomatic
  • Blueberry muffin rash (shared with congenital rubella), hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice, periventricular calcifications, sensorineural deafness (most common viral cause)
  • Severe/permanent findings mnemonic: MR DICS (Microcephaly, Restricted growth, Deafness, Intellectual disability, Chorioretinitis/seizures, Sensorineural hearing loss)
Treatment
  • Ganciclovir or valganciclovir (first-line, including for congenital CMV and transplant prophylaxis); foscarnet/cidofovir for ganciclovir-resistant CMV (resistance via UL-97 gene mutation).

30. Dermatophytes (Epidermophyton, Trichophyton, Microsporum)

Exam snapshot
  • Tinea (ringworm): red, scaly, itchy, annular lesions with raised edges and central clearing on skin, scalp, groin, feet, or nails.
  • Filamentous fungi that infect keratinized tissue (skin, hair, nails): Epidermophyton, Trichophyton, Microsporum; not normal flora.
  • KOH microscopy shows septate hyphae; Microsporum fluoresces under Wood's lamp; culture on Sabouraud agar.
  • Topical azoles or terbinafine for skin; oral terbinafine, griseofulvin, or azoles for nails, scalp, or severe disease.
  • Ringworm without worms, tinea capitis/corporis/cruris/pedis/unguium, Wood's lamp, griseofulvin binds keratin.
  1. 1Tin man: Dermatophytes are fungi that infect keratinized tissues (skin, hair, nails), causing tinea, or ringworm, so named for the red circular lesions, although no worm is involved.
  2. 2Rings of rust on tin man: Same point: tinea lesions are red, annular (ring-shaped) plaques, hence 'ringworm'.
  3. 3"E," "T," and "M" munchkins: The main dermatophyte genera are Epidermophyton, Trichophyton, and Microsporum.⭐ Trichophyton is the most common cause of tinea.
  4. 4Naked munchkins: Dermatophytes are pathogens, not part of the normal skin flora.
  5. 5Dog: Transmission is by direct contact with infected animals or humans, or indirectly through shared environments and fomites.⭐ Microsporum canis is acquired from pets.
  6. 6Rings on hat: Tinea capitis: infection of the scalp and hair, mainly in children, with patchy hair loss, scaling, broken hairs, and sometimes inflammation.⭐ Treat with oral therapy (griseofulvin or terbinafine); topicals are not enough.
  7. 7Rings on groin: Tinea cruris (jock itch): red, itchy, often ring-shaped rash of the groin and inner thighs; more common with warmth, moisture, sweating, or tight clothing.
  8. 8Rings on feet: Tinea pedis (athlete's foot): scaling, redness, itching, and sometimes cracking or blisters, especially between the toes; common in locker rooms, showers, and tight shoes.
  9. 9Rings on body: Tinea corporis: red, scaly, ring-shaped patches with raised edges and central clearing, on any part of the body; seen in wrestlers (tinea gladiatorum).
  10. 10Athletic head band: Athletes are at higher risk because of close contact, shared equipment, and warm, moist environments (swimmers, wrestlers); common infections include tinea corporis, pedis, and cruris.
  11. 11Scratching: Dermatophyte infections typically cause a red, itchy, scaly rash, often with raised edges and central clearing.
  12. 12KOH salt: KOH microscopy of scrapings reveals septate hyphae and confirms the diagnosis.⭐ KOH dissolves keratinocytes so hyphae are seen.
  13. 13Lamps + "M" munchkin: A Wood's (UV) lamp can detect Microsporum species, which fluoresce.⭐ Microsporum fluoresces green; Trichophyton generally does not (except T. schoenleinii).
  14. 14Pinecones: Topical azoles are commonly used to treat dermatophyte skin infections.⭐ Topical terbinafine is also effective.
  15. 15Biting nails: Onychomycosis (tinea unguium) is a fungal nail infection causing thickening, discoloration, brittleness, and separation from the nail bed; it can affect fingernails or toenails.
  16. 16Turban + biting nails: Oral antifungals (terbinafine, griseofulvin, or azoles) are used for onychomycosis, tinea capitis (turban), and severe or resistant skin infections.⭐ Terbinafine is first-line for onychomycosis; monitor liver enzymes.
  17. 17Grease + biting nails: Oral griseofulvin is used for more severe dermatophyte infections; it deposits in keratin-rich tissues (nails, hair, skin) but is often poorly tolerated due to GI side effects.⭐ Griseofulvin: microtubule inhibitor; hepatotoxic, teratogenic, CYP450 inducer.

31. Ebolavirus & Marburgvirus (Filoviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: abrupt fever, headache, myalgias after exposure to bats or an outbreak region, then GI symptoms, rash and hemorrhage.
  • Micro: enveloped, helical, nonsegmented negative-sense ssRNA virus (filamentous); Ebola and Marburg.
  • Diagnosis: RT-PCR on blood; handle under BSL-4 precautions.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; Ebola Zaire has monoclonal antibodies (e.g. ansuvimab, atoltivimab/maftivimab/odesivimab) and a vaccine; strict PPE and isolation.
  • Buzzwords: hemorrhagic fever, DIC, shock, fruit bats, health care workers, body fluids, funeral transmission.
  1. 1Soccer field: Filoviruses (Ebola, Marburg) are RNA viruses.
  2. 2Warm colors: Filoviruses (Ebola, Marburg) are RNA viruses. (warm colors = RNA virus in the Sketchy color code).
  3. 3Moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Filoviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses (the genome must be transcribed by a viral RNA-dependent RNA polymerase carried in the virion).⭐ Negative-sense RNA viruses carry their own RdRp in the virion.
  4. 4Orange spiral: The nucleocapsids of filoviruses have a helical structure (long filamentous virions).
  5. 5Soccer jerseys: Filoviruses are enveloped viruses.
  6. 6"Marburg" jersey: Marburg virus is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Filoviridae family.
  7. 7Gooooal!: Ebola virus is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Filoviridae family.
  8. 8Spotted jersey + red face: Marburg and Ebola infections begin abruptly with fever, headache, malaise and myalgias (flu-like), followed by nausea, vomiting, diarrhea and a maculopapular or petechial rash around day 5 as the disease progresses toward hemorrhagic features.
  9. 9Pool of blood: Filoviruses may progress to severe viral hemorrhagic fever with bleeding due to vascular leakage, DIC, mucosal and GI bleeding, multiorgan failure and shock; without supportive care mortality is extremely high.⭐ DIC with hemorrhagic fever is the classic cause of death.
  10. 10Kidney + liver blood spots: Same severe hemorrhagic fever picture: vascular leakage, DIC, bleeding, multiorgan (kidney and liver) failure and shock; mortality is extremely high without supportive care.
  11. 11Lightning bolt: Filoviruses may present with shock, typically mixed hypovolemic (fluid loss from vomiting, diarrhea, vascular leakage) and distributive (cytokine-mediated vasodilation, endothelial dysfunction), causing hypotension, tachycardia, oliguria and end-organ hypoperfusion.
  12. 12Monkeys: Fruit bats (Rousettus species for Marburg; several species for Ebola) are believed to be the natural reservoir; humans are infected through contact with infected bats, primates or other animals, followed by human-to-human spread via direct contact with blood or body fluids.⭐ Spread by direct contact with body fluids, not by aerosol.
  13. 13Bats: Fruit bats are the presumed natural reservoir for both viruses (Rousettus for Marburg), the source of spillover to humans and primates.
  14. 14Medics: Health care workers are at high risk without adequate PPE because the viruses are highly transmissible through direct contact with blood or body fluids.⭐ Strict isolation and full PPE prevent nosocomial spread.

32. Echovirus (Enterovirus)

  1. 1The mountain peak represents the Picornaviridae family, which includes hepatitis A virus, poliovirus, coxsackievirus A and B, echovirus, and rhinovirus.
  2. 2The peak animal nursery and baby animals represent that picornaviruses are very small, with a 20-30 nm icosahedral capsid; the "pico" in their name means "small."
  3. 3The warm colors represent that picornaviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 4The positive sun represents that picornaviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  5. 5The Statue of David represents that picornaviruses lack an envelope and consist of only a protein capsid surrounding their RNA, classifying them as naked viruses.
  6. 6The poop represents that hepatitis A virus, poliovirus, coxsackievirus A and B, and echovirus are transmitted via the fecal-oral route (rhinovirus is transmitted via respiratory droplets).
  7. 7The coin stamp machine represents that the genomic RNA of positive-sense picornaviruses, like picornaviruses, resembles mRNA and can be directly translated by host cell ribosomes.
  8. 8The roll of tickets represents that the RNA genome of picornaviruses is translated into a single, extended polyprotein, which is then cleaved by proteases.
  9. 9The admissions booth in the open area represents that positive-sense RNA viruses replicate in the cytoplasm, with their genomic RNA ready for immediate translation.
  10. 10The "A"-tagged hippos represent hepatitis A virus, a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family.
  11. 11The "ENTER" aviary represents that the Enterovirus genus of Picornaviridae includes poliovirus, coxsackievirus A and B, and echovirus.
  12. 12The flamingos represent poliovirus, a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family.
  13. 13The cockatoos represent coxsackievirus A and B, positive-sense RNA viruses in the Picornaviridae family.
  14. 14The mockingbirds echoing each other represent echovirus, a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family.
  15. 15The rhinos represent rhinovirus, a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family.
  16. 16The enlarged liver spot represents that hepatitis A virus causes hepatitis (inflammation of the liver) and may result in moderate enlargement of the liver (hepatomegaly) and, less frequently, an enlargement of the spleen (splenomegaly).
  17. 17The head-shaped aviary represents that aseptic meningitis, usually caused by enteroviruses, is a self-limited inflammation of the meninges in which the causative agent is a virus; therefore, CSF bacterial cultures are negative because viruses cannot grow on standard media, and no organisms are seen, hence it is called "aseptic."
  18. 18The "100% aseptic zone" sign represents that aseptic meningitis, usually caused by enteroviruses, is a self-limited inflammation of the meninges in which the causative agent is a virus; therefore, CSF bacterial cultures are negative because viruses cannot grow on standard media, and no organisms are seen, hence it is called "aseptic."
  19. 19The "no organisms" feed represents that aseptic meningitis, usually caused by enteroviruses, is a self-limited inflammation of the meninges in which the causative agent is a virus; therefore, CSF bacterial cultures are negative because viruses cannot grow on standard media, and no organisms are seen, hence it is called "aseptic."
  20. 20The "no sugar added" sign represents that aseptic meningitis typically manifests with a CSF glucose within the normal range (about 2/3 of the serum glucose) because viruses do not consume significant glucose and the inflammatory response is primarily lymphocytic rather than neutrophilic.
  21. 21The "source of protein" feed represents that aseptic meningitis often manifests with an elevated CSF protein level because inflammation increases protein permeability in the meninges, but the increase is usually milder than in bacterial meningitis, where protein is often markedly elevated.
  22. 22The child in a helmet represents that aseptic meningitis most often affects children and young adults.
  23. 23The mud on nose and mouth represents that rhinovirus, the main cause of the common cold, infects the upper respiratory tract and spreads via respiratory droplets, unlike other picornaviruses, which are primarily transmitted via the fecal-oral route.

33. Entamoeba histolytica

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: bloody diarrhea (dysentery) after travel or fecal-oral exposure; RUQ pain/fever from liver abscess.
  • Diagnosis: stool O&P with trophozoites containing ingested RBCs; serology/antigen/PCR help, and serology is key for liver abscess.
  • Histology: flask-shaped colonic ulcers; liver abscess aspirate looks like anchovy paste (usually right lobe, solitary).
  • Treatment: metronidazole or tinidazole for invasive disease, THEN a luminal agent (paromomycin or iodoquinol) to clear cysts.
  • Transmission: fecal-oral via cyst-contaminated food/water; also sexual (oral-anal) contact. Cysts have 4 nuclei, trophozoites eat RBCs.
  1. 1"Do not ENTer - HiSTorical site": Entamoeba histolytica is an intestinal protozoan that causes amebiasis (amebic dysentery and liver abscess). Mnemonic: the 'histolytica' name means tissue-lysing, so it invades tissue.
  2. 2Bubbles in water: The trophozoite is the motile, invasive, feeding stage; the cyst is the infectious, environmentally resistant form passed in stool. Cysts survive in water, which is why contaminated water is the main source.
  3. 3Drinking bubbly water: Amebiasis is acquired mainly by ingesting food or water contaminated with E. histolytica cysts (fecal-oral route). It is most common in areas with poor sanitation and in travelers returning from endemic regions.
  4. 4Holding hands: Transmission can also be sexual through oral-anal contact, since spread is fecal-oral. This makes it a consideration in men who have sex with men.
  5. 5Right lobe dig site: Hepatic amebiasis, a liver abscess, is the most common extraintestinal manifestation. Trophozoites reach the liver via the portal vein, and the abscess is typically solitary and in the right lobe.
  6. 6Clutching right side: Liver abscess presents with RUQ pain, fever, hepatomegaly, malaise and nausea; labs may show leukocytosis and elevated liver enzymes. Diarrhea is often absent at the time the abscess presents.
  7. 7Anchovy paste truck: Abscess aspirate is thick, reddish-brown 'anchovy paste' from necrotic hepatocytes (liquefactive necrosis). Trophozoites are usually absent from the aspirate and sit at the abscess periphery.⭐ Anchovy paste = amebic liver abscess. Treat with metronidazole; drainage is rarely needed.
  8. 8Colon drainage pipe with rust spots: Colonic infection produces ulcers with a narrow neck at the mucosal surface and a broad base in the submucosa (flask shape), most often in the cecum and ascending colon but anywhere in the large bowel.
  9. 9Flask water bottles: Flask-shaped ulcers on histology are the hallmark of invasive intestinal amebiasis. The trophozoites lyse the mucosa and undermine the submucosa.
  10. 10Flask in colon pipe: Colon biopsy showing flask-shaped ulcers with trophozoites confirms intestinal amebiasis when stool studies are unrevealing. Colonoscopy/biopsy is used when the diagnosis is uncertain.
  11. 11Red stools: Bloody diarrhea (dysentery) results from trophozoite invasion of the mucosa and submucosa with tissue necrosis and ulceration. Stool is typically bloody and mucoid with relatively little fever, unlike bacterial dysentery.
  12. 12"O&P" pipe: Diagnosis uses stool O&P: cysts (round, up to 4 nuclei, mostly in formed stool) or trophozoites (motile, mostly in liquid stool of acute infection). Multiple samples may be needed; antigen tests or PCR can distinguish E. histolytica from non-pathogenic E. dispar.
  13. 13Trophozoite puddle + red cups: Trophozoites containing ingested RBCs (erythrophagocytosis) in stool or tissue are diagnostic of invasive amebiasis, and this separates E. histolytica from non-pathogenic amebae.⭐ Ingested RBCs inside the trophozoite = invasive E. histolytica.
  14. 14Metro: Metronidazole (or tinidazole) is first-line for invasive disease and liver abscess because it kills tissue-invading trophozoites. It does not reliably clear luminal cysts, so a luminal agent must follow.
  15. 15Pair of mice: A luminal agent (paromomycin or iodoquinol) is given after metronidazole/tinidazole to eradicate residual cysts in the gut lumen and prevent relapse and spread. Paromomycin is a poorly absorbed aminoglycoside.
  16. 16"Queen Iodo's Tomb": Iodoquinol is the other luminal amebicide used to clear cysts after tissue-active therapy. Either paromomycin or iodoquinol completes the treatment course.

34. Enterococcus faecium & faecalis

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: UTI, endocarditis (after GU/GI procedures), biliary tree infection, often hospital-acquired
  • Gram-positive cocci in pairs/short chains; group D; non-hemolytic (gamma) usually; PYR positive; catalase negative
  • Grows in 6.5% NaCl and in bile (hydrolyzes esculin: bile esculin agar turns black)
  • Treatment: ampicillin (+/- gentamicin for endocarditis); VRE: linezolid or daptomycin
  • Buzzwords: 6.5% NaCl, bile-esculin, VRE, E. faecalis most common
  1. 1"Caucus": Enterococcus species are gram-positive cocci (in pairs and short chains; group D by Lancefield).
  2. 2Purple hues: Enterococcus faecium and Enterococcus faecalis are gram-positive.
  3. 3"Resist the 6.5% N.Ca": E. faecium and E. faecalis grow in 6.5% NaCl (hypertonic saline), distinguishing them from most other gram-positive cocci such as nonenterococcal group D strep (S. gallolyticus/bovis).⭐ Enterococci survive 6.5% NaCl and bile; group D Streptococcus bovis survives bile only.
  4. 4Green bile boots: E. faecium and E. faecalis hydrolyze esculin in the presence of bile (bile-esculin positive, i.e. bile-resistant), turning the medium black.
  5. 5"Stop the FEES" + less people: Enterococcus faecium accounts for the minority of enterococcal infections but is more often vancomycin-resistant.⭐ E. faecium = fewer infections but more resistance.
  6. 6"CALIfornia caucus" + more people: Enterococcus faecalis is responsible for the majority of enterococcal infections.
  7. 7"Do U ¥ trees?": Enterococcal infections include UTIs (cystitis, pyelonephritis), endocarditis and biliary tree infections (also intra-abdominal and wound infections).⭐ Enterococcus is a classic cause of subacute endocarditis after GU/GI procedures.
  8. 8Resisting entering van: Some E. faecium and E. faecalis strains are resistant to many antibiotics; vancomycin-resistant enterococci (VRE) are particularly important.⭐ VRE: vanA gene alters D-Ala-D-Ala to D-Ala-D-Lac.
  9. 9Police line: For VRE infections, linezolid is an effective treatment.⭐ Daptomycin is another option for VRE.
  10. 10Tiger stripes: Tigecycline has activity against some VRE strains and may be considered in specific clinical scenarios.

35. Epstein-Barr Virus

  1. 1“Epstein's Barr 21”: Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) is a DNA virus in the Herpesviridae family.
  2. 2Cool colors: EBV is a DNA virus.
  3. 3Saliva drips + trying to kiss: EBV is primarily transmitted via saliva, making close personal contact a common route of spread, especially among young adults and college students; nicknamed the “kissing disease.”
  4. 4Sweat drops: A hallmark of EBV-induced infectious mononucleosis is the presentation of fever, which, in combination with lymphadenopathy and pharyngitis, forms the classic triad of symptoms frequently observed in affected individuals.
  5. 5Grabbing back of shirt: Patients with EBV-induced mononucleosis frequently manifest tender lymphadenopathy, particularly in the posterior cervical region, but it can be generalized.
  6. 6Reactive “8” T-knight + atypical shapes: EBV infection causes peripheral lymphocytosis and the presence of atypical lymphocytes (also known as Downey cells), abnormally large, reactive CD8+ T-cells with strongly basophilic cytoplasm that respond to EBV-infected B-cells; these atypical lymphocytes can be seen on peripheral blood smear.
  7. 7Enlarged spleen and liver spot: EBV infection typically leads to a proliferation of mononuclear cells that accumulate within the lymphoid tissue, including the spleen, which may result in splenomegaly (less commonly hepatomegaly).
  8. 8Sleeping B-cell archer: EBV maintains latency within B-cells, persisting indefinitely within the host, potentially leading to reactivated EBV under certain conditions or when exposed to certain triggers.
  9. 9Entrance + “Must B 21”: To enter host cells, the EBV envelope glycoprotein gp350 binds to CD21 (also known as CR2, the complement receptor type 2) on the surface of B cells.
  10. 10White drool: A common clinical feature of EBV-induced mononucleosis is severe pharyngitis with copious gray-white tonsillar exudate and marked tonsillar enlargement; EBV pharyngitis typically affects late teens and young adults, unlike streptococcal pharyngitis, which is more common in children.
  11. 11Red pencil spots: When patients with an active EBV infection are inadvertently administered penicillin or aminopenicillins (especially amoxicillin or ampicillin) due to suspicion of a bacterial infection, they may develop a characteristic maculopapular rash; this reaction is immune-mediated (Type IV hypersensitivity), T-cell–driven, and not a true IgE-mediated allergic reaction.
  12. 12Crab meals: EBV infection is associated with a higher risk for development of some cancers, including Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphoma (particularly Burkitt lymphoma).
  13. 13Stern owl's eyes + sitting in reeds: Hodgkin lymphoma, which is strongly associated with EBV infection, is characterized by Reed-Sternberg cells, large, abnormal B-cells with a distinctive bilobed nucleus resembling “owl eyes.”
  14. 14Bar kid: EBV is associated with a higher risk for development of Burkitt lymphoma, a rare but highly aggressive B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma characterized by a t(8;14) translocation.
  15. 15Jaw lump + African clothing: Endemic Burkitt lymphoma, a specific subtype of Burkitt lymphoma that occurs predominantly in children in certain regions of equatorial Africa, often presents as a rapidly enlarging jaw or facial bone mass and swelling vs. sporadic Burkitt lymphoma typically occurring in children or young adults worldwide, often presenting as an abdominal (ileocecal) mass.
  16. 16Crab pinching nose + adult man in kimono: Nasopharyngeal carcinoma, a malignancy with a tendency to metastasize, has a strong association with EBV, most commonly presenting with a painless neck mass, nasal obstruction or bleeding, and sometimes ear symptoms or cranial nerve involvement, classically seen in adult males of Southern Chinese (Asian) descent.
  17. 17Bearded man + immunocompromised case: Oral hairy leukoplakia, benign, painless white lesions with a corrugated or “hairy” appearance on lateral borders of the tongue, which cannot be scraped off (unlike candidal thrush); it is triggered by EBV in immunocompromised individuals (HIV/AIDS).
  18. 18Bullseye spot + dart stars: The monospot test can be used to provide a quick diagnosis for acute EBV infection by detecting heterophile IgM antibodies, which induce agglutination of horse or sheep red blood cells; however, the CDC no longer recommends the monospot test due to low sensitivity and specificity (currently, serum testing for EBV-specific antibodies is considered the gold standard for diagnostic testing).
  19. 19“No contact jousting”: Patients with infectious mononucleosis are advised to avoid contact sports for several weeks or until a physician clears them due to the elevated risk of splenic rupture.

36. Eq. Encephalitis / Chikungunya virus

  1. 1The warm colors represent that Rubella virus and Togaviridae are RNA viruses with a single positive-sense strand.
  2. 2The positive sun represents that Rubella virus and Togaviridae are RNA viruses with a single positive-sense strand.
  3. 3The man in a toga carrying a robe represents that Togaviridae are enveloped viruses.
  4. 4The arbor represents that members of Togaviridae (genus Alphavirus) are arthropod-borne viruses (or arboviruses) transmitted to humans by insect vectors (e.g., mosquitoes).
  5. 5The mosquitoes swarming around the man represent that members of Togaviridae (genus Alphavirus) are arthropod-borne viruses (or arboviruses) transmitted to humans by insect vectors (e.g., mosquitoes).
  6. 6The compass on horse represents that the equine encephalitis viruses-Eastern Equine Encephalitis Virus (EEEV, eastern North America), Western Equine Encephalitis Virus (WEEV, western North America), and Venezuelan Equine Encephalitis Virus (VEEV, Central and South America)-are Alphaviruses (family Togaviridae).
  7. 7The neck brace represents that neuroinvasive manifestations of the equine encephalitides include meningitis (fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, nausea/vomiting) and encephalitis (fever, altered mental status, seizures, focal neurologic deficits, movement disorders); Eastern Equine Encephalitis Virus (EEEV) is the most severe, carrying the highest risk of death and long-term neurologic morbidity.
  8. 8The red turban and hitting head represent that neuroinvasive manifestations of the equine encephalitides include meningitis (fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, nausea/vomiting) and encephalitis (fever, altered mental status, seizures, focal neurologic deficits, movement disorders); Eastern Equine Encephalitis Virus (EEEV) is the most severe, carrying the highest risk of death and long-term neurologic morbidity.
  9. 9The rubies represent that Rubella virus is the only member of the Rubivirus genus (within the Matonaviridae family) and presents in three distinct clinical forms depending on patient age: congenital rubella, childhood rubella, and adult rubella.
  10. 10The child emperor wearing a robe represents that Rubella virus is enveloped.
  11. 11The rolling of tickets represents that the RNA genome of Rubella virus is translated into a single, extended polyprotein, which is then cleaved by proteases.
  12. 12The nucleus-shaped dome in the background represents that positive-sense RNA viruses replicate in the cytoplasm, outside the nucleus, with their genomic RNA ready for immediate translation by host ribosomes.
  13. 13The child emperor represents that Rubella virus is an important cause of a childhood exanthem (widespread skin rash).
  14. 14The servants fanning represent that Rubella virus spreads via respiratory droplets, replicates in the nasopharynx during an incubation period of 2-3 weeks (asymptomatic), spreads to lymph nodes, and then enters the bloodstream (viremia, meaning virus in the blood), often causing a mild prodrome of low-grade fever, malaise, mild URI symptoms, and tender lymphadenopathy that precedes the characteristic rash.
  15. 15The spreading rubies represent that Rubella virus causes a childhood exanthem-a spreading maculopapular rash that begins on the face and extends to the trunk and extremities-occurring after viremia as the immune response begins to react to viral antigens in small skin blood vessels; the rash spreads more rapidly than with measles, and unlike measles, rubella typically does not darken or coalesce, resolving within about three days ("3-day measles").
  16. 16The beaded necklace behind ears represents that Rubella virus is often associated with tender lymphadenopathy, particularly in the posterior auricular, suboccipital, and posterior cervical regions, from immune response to viral replication.
  17. 17The kneeling adult in pain represents that adults infected with rubella virus may experience arthralgias (joint pain), often affecting small joints, sometimes accompanied by mild fever and malaise.
  18. 18The baby sculptures on walls represent that congenital rubella infection encompasses negative pregnancy outcomes (e.g., miscarriage, stillbirth) and the congenital rubella syndrome seen in the neonate.
  19. 19The TORCHeS group of infections is represented as: T: Toxoplasma; O: Other (varicella-zoster virus, parvovirus B19, Zika virus, HIV); R: Rubella; C: Cytomegalovirus (CMV); He: Herpes simplex virus (HSV); S: Syphilis-a group of infections that are transmitted vertically through the placenta.
  20. 20The blueberry gemmed babies represent that congenital rubella syndrome (CRS) presents with purpuric skin lesions known as the 'blueberry muffin rash', consisting of purplish, non-blanching nodules caused by extramedullary hematopoiesis in response to fetal anemia.
  21. 21The golden babies represent that CRS can present with hyperbilirubinemia and jaundice due to fetal hepatocellular injury and cholestasis caused by in utero rubella infection.
  22. 22The empty eyes represent that CRS presents with congenital cataracts (other ocular findings include corneal clouding, glaucoma, and retinopathy) because in utero rubella infection disrupts normal development of the lens, cornea, anterior chamber, and retina.
  23. 23The covering of ears represents that CRS presents with congenital bilateral sensorineural hearing loss due to rubella virus-induced damage to the developing cochlea and auditory neurons during fetal development.
  24. 24The tiny head represents that CRS presents with microcephaly (other neurological findings include meningoencephalitis, and developmental delays) due to rubella virus-mediated disruption of fetal brain development during early gestation.
  25. 25The aqueductus arteriosus represents that CRS presents with patent ductus arteriosus (other cardiac findings include branch pulmonary artery stenosis, aortic stenosis, ASD and VSD) due to rubella virus-mediated disruption of cardiac morphogenesis during early gestation.
  26. 26The LIVE puppet show represents that active immunity against rubella is achieved with a live, attenuated vaccine eliciting humoral and cell-mediated immune responses; rubella cases and CRS are rare in countries with routine MMR vaccination, but cases can occur in immigrants or travelers from regions with low vaccination coverage or in individuals who have not completed the vaccine series.
  27. 27The 3 puppets plus syringe sword represent that measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) comprise a trivalent vaccine, while the quadrivalent version (MMRV) adds varicella; it is recommended for children aged 12 months and older, except in pregnant women and immunocompromised patients.
  28. 28The pregnant puppet running away from syringe sword represents that MMR is contraindicated in pregnancy because it is a live attenuated vaccine that could theoretically infect the fetus and cause congenital rubella syndrome.
  29. 29The only 200 tickets represent that MMR is contraindicated in patients with significant immunodeficiency (e.g., HIV with CD4 <200) because the live attenuated vaccine virus could replicate uncontrolled, causing serious systemic infection.

37. Escherichia coli (ETEC & EHEC)

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: UTI (cystitis/pyelonephritis), neonatal meningitis (K1), gram-negative sepsis; EHEC = bloody diarrhea + HUS; ETEC = traveler's watery diarrhea.
  • Lactose fermenter: pink on MacConkey, metallic green sheen on EMB; oxidase negative, indole positive.
  • EHEC O157:H7: sorbitol-negative (colorless on sorbitol-MacConkey), Shiga-like toxin (inactivates 60S ribosome), undercooked beef.
  • HUS triad: thrombocytopenia, MAHA with schistocytes, acute kidney injury; do NOT give antibiotics for EHEC.
  • ETEC: LT (like cholera, increases cAMP) and ST (increases cGMP) cause watery diarrhea; treat with fluids.
  1. 1E. cola: Escherichia coli is a gram-negative bacillus (pink rod on Gram stain) and a normal member of gut flora, but it is also the leading cause of several infections. It is a facultative anaerobe in the Enterobacteriaceae family.
  2. 2Red hues: Red/pink coloring reminds you that E. coli is gram-negative: it has a thin peptidoglycan layer with an outer membrane containing LPS (endotoxin), which stains pink with safranin counterstain.
  3. 3Pink milk carton: E. coli ferments lactose, forming pink colonies on MacConkey agar (acid from lactose fermentation turns the pH indicator pink). Other lactose fermenters include Klebsiella and Enterobacter.
  4. 4Glass capsule: Many strains of E. coli are surrounded by a polysaccharide capsule, which helps them evade phagocytosis.
  5. 5K cake + glass capsule: Some strains express K capsule (K antigen), a key virulence factor in extraintestinal infections (e.g., sepsis, meningitis, UTI) because it resists phagocytosis.
  6. 6Green coaster: E. coli typically forms distinctive metallic green colonies on eosin methylene blue (EMB) agar; the metallic sheen is a classic buzzword for strong lactose fermentation.
  7. 7Catalase cat: E. coli is catalase positive: catalase converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen, protecting the bacterium from oxidative killing. Catalase-positive organisms are particularly a problem in chronic granulomatous disease.
  8. 8Fimbriae ribbons: Fimbriae (pili) of E. coli mediate adhesion to host cells, an important virulence factor. P fimbriae bind uroepithelium and are linked to pyelonephritis, preventing the bacteria from being washed out by urine flow.
  9. 9"1" bladder cup: E. coli is the most common cause of urinary tract infections (cystitis and pyelonephritis), usually by ascending spread from the gut flora. It is the most common cause of uncomplicated UTI.
  10. 10Blood red shake: E. coli is a major cause of gram-negative sepsis. LPS (endotoxin, lipid A) triggers release of TNF-α and IL-1, leading to fever, hypotension and septic shock; urinary tract and GI/biliary sources are common.
  11. 11K cake + baby + meningitis helmet: Strains with the K1 capsular antigen cause neonatal meningitis, acquired from the maternal birth canal.⭐ Neonatal meningitis: think group B Strep, E. coli (K1), and Listeria.
  12. 12Hamburger: Enterohemorrhagic E. coli (EHEC) is classically transmitted through undercooked ground beef (also unpasteurized juice and raw produce). The infectious dose is very low.⭐ Undercooked burger + bloody diarrhea = EHEC (O157:H7).
  13. 13Red stool: EHEC causes bloody diarrhea (hemorrhagic colitis) by damaging the intestinal lining through Shiga-like toxin production. Fever is typically absent or low-grade, and fecal leukocytes are scant.⭐ Antibiotics and antimotility agents are avoided in EHEC because they can raise the risk of HUS.
  14. 14Sorbitol free soda: O157:H7 does not ferment sorbitol, so it forms colorless colonies on sorbitol-MacConkey agar, distinguishing it from most other E. coli strains, which are sorbitol fermenters.
  15. 15She-gorilla toy: EHEC produces Shiga-like toxin (Shiga toxin 1/2, phage-encoded), nearly identical to the Shiga toxin of Shigella dysenteriae. It inactivates the 60S ribosomal subunit (cleaves 28S rRNA) in host cells, disrupting protein synthesis and killing the cell.
  16. 16Glomerular straw: Shiga-like toxin damages endothelial cells of glomerular capillaries and causes hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS). This endothelial injury leads to acute kidney injury; HUS usually follows the diarrheal illness by about a week, mainly in children.
  17. 17Plate aggregation: Endothelial damage exposes the surface to platelets, triggering platelet aggregation and microthrombi. Platelet consumption causes thrombocytopenia (the classic HUS triad is thrombocytopenia, MAHA and acute kidney injury).⭐ HUS: low platelets but normal PT/PTT (unlike DIC); schistocytes on smear.
  18. 18Popped red balloon: In microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), red cells are mechanically sheared into schistocytes as they pass through small vessels filled with fibrin/platelet clots, lowering the red cell count. Labs show elevated LDH and indirect bilirubin, low haptoglobin, and a negative Coombs test.
  19. 19"Only $1.57": E. coli O157:H7 is the classic enterohemorrhagic serotype: Shiga-like toxin causes bloody diarrhea and HUS. The "$1.57" in the picture is a mnemonic for O157. Management is supportive (fluids); avoid antibiotics.
  20. 20Water truck: Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC) is a common cause of traveler's diarrhea, acquired by ingesting contaminated water or food. It causes watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, nausea and vomiting; it colonizes the small bowel via fimbriae (colonization factors) and does not invade.
  21. 21eL Agua: Heat-labile toxin (LT, mnemonic: eL = Labile) ADP-ribosylates Gs, increasing adenylyl cyclase activity, so cAMP rises, inhibiting sodium/chloride absorption and promoting chloride and water secretion, causing secretory (watery) diarrhea. It works like cholera toxin.
  22. 22San Gabriel: Heat-stable toxin (ST, mnemonic: San Gabriel = Stable, Guanylate) activates guanylate cyclase, raising cGMP, increasing chloride secretion and reducing sodium absorption, causing secretory (watery) diarrhea.⭐ LT = cAMP (like cholera); ST = cGMP. Remember: LT is Labile (heat-inactivated), ST is Stable.
  23. 23Spilling water: ETEC causes traveler's diarrhea: watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, nausea and vomiting, with no blood and no significant inflammation. It is usually self-limited and treated with oral rehydration; antibiotics (e.g., rifaximin, azithromycin, fluoroquinolone) are used in more severe cases.

38. Flaviviridae/Yellow Fever

Exam snapshot
  • Flaviviridae: enveloped, (+)ssRNA, non-segmented viruses; flaviviruses (dengue, yellow fever, Zika, West Nile) are arboviruses; HCV (Hepacivirus) is bloodborne.
  • Dengue: Aedes; 'breakbone fever', retro-orbital pain, thrombocytopenia; severe dengue with plasma leakage on secondary infection by a different serotype (antibody-dependent enhancement).
  • Yellow fever: Aedes; fever, jaundice, hemorrhage, black vomit; Councilman bodies and Faget sign; live attenuated 17D vaccine for travelers to Africa/South America.
  • Zika: Aedes; mild febrile illness; congenital microcephaly, sexual transmission, and Guillain-Barre syndrome.
  • West Nile: Culex mosquito, birds are reservoir; mostly asymptomatic; can cause meningitis, encephalitis, and acute flaccid paralysis; all treatment is supportive.
  1. 1Warm colors: Flaviviridae are RNA viruses (the warm color theme represents 'flavi' = yellow, from yellow fever).
  2. 2Positive sun: Flaviviridae are positive-sense RNA viruses, so the genome can serve directly as mRNA.
  3. 35 Flavors!: The Flaviviridae family includes the genera Flavivirus and Hepacivirus; clinically important members are dengue, yellow fever, Zika, West Nile, and hepatitis C. Most flaviviruses are arboviruses (arthropod-borne), spread by mosquitoes (Aedes or Culex), whereas hepatitis C is bloodborne and not vector-transmitted.
  4. 4Single straw: Flaviviridae have a non-segmented, single-stranded RNA genome, so they cannot undergo reassortment.
  5. 5Hep C Hippo: Hepatitis C virus is a member of Flaviviridae (genus Hepacivirus). It is the only one commonly spread by blood and it causes chronic liver disease.
  6. 6Robe: Flaviviridae are enveloped viruses, so they are inactivated by detergents and disinfectants.
  7. 7Dinghy with a donkey: Dengue virus (Flavivirus) is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito and causes dengue fever, or 'breakbone fever'. It presents with high fever, severe myalgias and arthralgias, headache, retro-orbital pain, and rash.
  8. 8Eighties clothes on donkey: Dengue fever (the colorful 80s clothes represent the rash) presents with high fever, severe myalgias and arthralgias, headache, and a macular or maculopapular rash. Treatment is supportive (fluids, acetaminophen); avoid NSAIDs and aspirin because of bleeding risk.
  9. 9Broken plates: Dengue causes bone marrow suppression and immune-mediated platelet destruction, leading to thrombocytopenia and increased bleeding risk (petechiae, positive tourniquet test).⭐ Thrombocytopenia is a lab hallmark of dengue.
  10. 10Spilled strawberry milkshake: Dengue hemorrhagic fever is dengue with thrombocytopenia, plasma leakage, and hemorrhagic manifestations. It often occurs on secondary infection with a different serotype, when non-neutralizing antibodies cause antibody-dependent enhancement.⭐ Second infection with a different serotype = worse disease (ADE).
  11. 11Deflated kidney: Severe dengue can cause acute renal failure and hypovolemic shock (dengue shock syndrome) because of plasma leakage, dehydration, and multiorgan dysfunction.
  12. 12Syringe-shaped oar: A tetravalent live attenuated dengue vaccine is available, but it is given only to people with confirmed prior dengue infection. In seronegative people it can predispose to antibody-dependent enhancement and severe disease upon natural infection.
  13. 13Yellow water buffalo: Yellow fever virus (a positive-sense RNA flavivirus) is transmitted by Aedes aegypti and causes fever, jaundice, myalgia, and hemorrhagic manifestations. Treatment is supportive. Its reservoirs are monkeys (sylvatic cycle) and humans (urban cycle).
  14. 14Eighties clothes on buffalo: Yellow fever (the 80s clothes mark the Aedes-borne flavivirus) causes fever, jaundice, myalgia, and hemorrhage. Classically, a patient may improve briefly and then relapse with the toxic phase of jaundice and bleeding. The Faget sign is a high fever with a relatively slow pulse.⭐ Faget sign = fever with relative bradycardia.
  15. 15Liver spot + yellow buffalo: Yellow fever damages hepatocytes and Kupffer cells, causing hepatocellular necrosis and inflammation that lead to hepatomegaly and impaired bilirubin metabolism, hence jaundice. Histology shows midzonal necrosis with eosinophilic Councilman bodies (apoptotic hepatocytes).⭐ Councilman bodies are classic for yellow fever.
  16. 16Back hump: Yellow fever causes severe myalgia, especially in the lumbosacral region, from viral-induced myositis and cytokine-mediated muscle inflammation.
  17. 17Red stool: Yellow fever is a hemorrhagic fever with bloody diarrhea, melena, hematemesis ('black vomit'), epistaxis, and petechiae. It results from impaired clotting factor synthesis by the injured liver, thrombocytopenia, and endothelial damage, giving elevated PT and PTT and a low platelet count.
  18. 18Syringe-shaped horns: A live attenuated vaccine (17D strain) prevents yellow fever and is recommended for travelers to endemic regions of Africa and South America. It is contraindicated in immunocompromised patients and used with caution in egg allergy.
  19. 19Zebra: Zika virus (Flavivirus) is transmitted by Aedes aegypti and usually causes mild fever, rash, arthralgia, and conjunctivitis (red, irritated eyes). Most infections are asymptomatic or mild and treatment is supportive.
  20. 20Eighties clothes on zebra: Zika (the 80s clothes mark the Aedes-borne flavivirus) causes mild fever, rash, arthralgia, and nonpurulent conjunctivitis. Treatment is supportive.
  21. 21Pregnant zebra: Zika is shed in blood, semen, and other body fluids, so it spreads through Aedes bites and also vertically (mother to fetus), sexually, and via blood transfusion or organ transplant.⭐ Zika is notable among flaviviruses for sexual transmission.
  22. 22Tiny hat: Congenital Zika syndrome is marked by microcephaly with deficient brain tissue (e.g., calcifications, ventriculomegaly) and neurologic dysfunction.⭐ Zika = microcephaly.
  23. 23Guillain the bear: Zika infection is associated with Guillain-Barre syndrome, an ascending symmetric weakness with areflexia, usually 1-2 weeks after the acute infection. It is an autoimmune demyelinating polyneuropathy.
  24. 24Nile bird: West Nile virus is transmitted by Culex mosquitoes, with birds as the reservoir host. It is mostly asymptomatic, but can cause West Nile fever (fever, headache, myalgia, rash) or progress to neuroinvasive disease. Treatment is supportive.
  25. 25Cool-X + mosquito: Culex mosquitoes (the 'X' in Culex) spread West Nile virus between birds and then to humans, who are dead-end hosts. Fewer than 1% of infections become neuroinvasive, and risk is higher in the elderly.
  26. 26Red feather on head: West Nile neuroinvasive disease can present as encephalitis (inflammation of brain parenchyma) with fever, headache, altered mental status, seizures, and focal neurologic deficits.
  27. 27Neck brace: West Nile virus can cause meningitis (inflammation of the meninges), presenting with fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, and nausea. CSF typically shows lymphocytic pleocytosis with elevated protein and normal glucose.
  28. 28Floppy neck: West Nile virus can cause asymmetric acute flaccid paralysis from damage to anterior horn cells, similar to poliomyelitis. It presents with weakness, decreased tone, and areflexia without sensory loss.
  29. 29Flapping wings: West Nile neuroinvasive disease may also cause tremor and Parkinson-like movement disorders (flapping wings = tremor/abnormal movements), along with seizures, altered mental status, and focal deficits.
  30. 30Comatose bird: Severe West Nile neuroinvasive disease can progress to coma and death, especially in elderly or immunocompromised patients. Diagnosis is by IgM antibody in serum or CSF, and treatment is supportive.

39. Francisella tularensis (Tularemia)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: hunter or rabbit/rodent handler or tick bite victim with an ulcer at the site and painful regional lymphadenopathy, plus fever.
  • Gram-negative coccobacillus, facultative intracellular (macrophages); needs cysteine for growth; bioterrorism agent.
  • Reservoirs: rabbits, rodents; vectors: Dermacentor ticks, deer flies, mosquitoes.
  • Treatment: streptomycin (or gentamicin); report to the CDC.
  • Buzzwords: rabbits, ulceroglandular, Dermacentor tick, granulomas, bioterrorism.
  1. 1Francis the rabbit: Francisella tularensis is a gram-negative coccobacillus.
  2. 2Rabbit: F. tularensis has a broad animal reservoir, including rabbits, rodents, and other mammals.⭐ Rabbit skinning or hunting is a classic exposure.
  3. 3Ticks: F. tularensis is transmitted by arthropod vectors such as ticks (especially Dermacentor), deer flies, horse flies, and mosquitoes. It can be aerosolized, so it is a potential bioterrorism agent and cases must be reported to the CDC.⭐ Any case of F. tularensis must be reported to the CDC.
  4. 4Red radishes: F. tularensis is a gram-negative coccobacillus (red on Gram stain).
  5. 5Open cage: F. tularensis is a facultative intracellular pathogen that survives and replicates in macrophages.⭐ This allows it to evade immune clearance.
  6. 6Rabbit holes: Tularemia can cause a painful skin ulcer with necrosis at the site of infection (e.g., a tick bite), the ulceroglandular form.⭐ Ulceroglandular is the most common form.
  7. 7Tunnels to rotting radishes: The organism spreads through lymphatics to reticuloendothelial organs (lymph nodes, liver, spleen), causing granulomatous inflammation with central necrosis, painful regional lymphadenopathy, fever, and systemic symptoms.
  8. 8Mounds around rabbit: Tularemia classically causes painful regional lymphadenopathy (lymph node enlargement).
  9. 9Sai: Aminoglycosides, particularly streptomycin and gentamicin, are effective against tularemia.⭐ Streptomycin is the classic drug of choice.
