56. Influenzavirus

  1. 1“FLU-oride”: Influenzaviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses in the Orthomyxoviridae family.
  2. 2Orthodontist: Influenzaviruses (negative-sense RNA family) belong to the Orthomyxoviridae family.
  3. 3Warm colors: Influenzaviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 4Moon with negative-sign-shaped clouds: Influenzaviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses.
  5. 5Enveloping coat: Influenzaviruses are enveloped viruses.
  6. 6“FLU-oride...it's as easy as ABC!”: Orthomyxovirus family includes the influenza A, B, and C viruses.
  7. 7Octopus babies in helmet: Influenza viruses replicate within the nucleus of the host cells, a unique characteristic distinguishing them from many other RNA viruses, which replicate in the cytoplasm.
  8. 88 octopus tentacles: The genome of orthomyxoviruses is segmented, with influenza virus A and B having 8 RNA segments and influenza virus C having 7 RNA segments.
  9. 9“DoKtor Drift” + one letter altered: Antigenic drift in influenzaviruses is due to point mutations in the hemagglutinin (HA) or neuraminidase (NA) genes, leading to minor alterations in these proteins; minor changes help the virus evade prior immunity, necessitating annual vaccine updates and leading to seasonal epidemics usually limited to specific regions.
  10. 10“Night shift”: Antigenic shift of influenza virus A occurs through reassortment of genome segments from different influenza A viruses (or strains) come together to form a novel genome, resulting in major changes in virulence factors such as hemagglutinin (HA) and neuraminidase (NA); often involving significant genome exchange between species, can produce easily transmissible subtypes to which humans have little immunity, causing pandemics such as H1N1 the swine flu outbreak.
  11. 11Mixed H's and N's: Antigenic shift of influenza virus A occurs through reassortment of genome segments from different influenza A viruses (or strains), resulting in major changes in virulence factors such as hemagglutinin (HA) and neuraminidase (NA); often involving significant genome exchange between species, can produce easily transmissible subtypes to which humans have little immunity, causing pandemics such as the H1N1 swine flu outbreak.
  12. 12Multicolored curtains: Influenza virus exhibits antigenic variation due to antigenic drift and antigenic shift.
  13. 13Heme aquarium + RBC rocks: Influenza virus uses its glycoprotein hemagglutinin (HA) to facilitate entry into host cells by binding to sialic acid residues.
  14. 14Branched heme aquarium plant: Influenza virus uses its glycoprotein hemagglutinin (HA) to facilitate entry into host cells by binding to sialic acid residues.
  15. 15Multicolored suckers: A variety of hemagglutinin (HA) glycoproteins are expressed by influenzaviruses; H1, H2, and H3 are the most common subtypes in human infections; the HA glycoprotein determines viral cell tropism (what cells/influenzaviruses are able to infect).
  16. 162 “M” shell opening: The M2 proton channel of influenza virus A modulates the hydrogen ion concentration, facilitating an optimal pH environment for viral uncoating.
  17. 17Manta diving + hiding in 2 “M” shell: Amantadine and rimantadine inhibit the M2 proton channel of influenza A, blocking viral uncoating, but their use has declined due to resistance and side effects; amantadine also increases dopamine release and blocks certain glutamate receptors, helping reduce Parkinson's symptoms.
  18. 18Nurse Asst.'s scalpels: Influenza virus possesses the neuraminidase (NA) enzyme, which facilitates the release of progeny viruses from host cells by cleaving sialic acid residues.
  19. 19Scalpels cleaving branched plant: Influenza virus possesses the neuraminidase (NA) enzyme, which facilitates the release of progeny viruses from host cells by cleaving sialic acid residues.
  20. 20Nurse Asst. Tammy V: Oseltamivir (Tamiflu) and zanamivir are neuraminidase inhibitors that prevent influenza virus release and must be given within 72 hours, when viral replication is still active, to be most effective.
  21. 21Tammy V capping scalpels: Oseltamivir (Tamiflu) and zanamivir are neuraminidase inhibitors that prevent influenza virus release and must be given within 72 hours, when viral replication is still active, to be most effective.
  22. 22Water droplets: Influenza virus is primarily transmitted via respiratory droplets (coughing, sneezing, close contact) and affects humans most commonly during the colder months, typically December to February in the United States, with signs and symptoms including sudden onset fever, chills, myalgias, headache, sore throat, nonproductive cough, and fatigue.
  23. 23Syringe + skeleton: Vaccines against influenza virus include a killed injectable formulation and a live attenuated intranasal formulation.
  24. 24Nose bubbler: Vaccines against influenza virus include a killed injectable formulation and a live attenuated intranasal formulation.
  25. 25Lung bib: Pneumonia can manifest as a complication following influenza infection, either as a direct consequence of influenza virus or due to a secondary bacterial or viral infection; primary viral pneumonia is usually simultaneous with the initial influenza illness, presenting with dyspnea, tachypnea, hypoxia, high fever, chills, and nonproductive cough, and can be life-threatening.
  26. 26Gold dental tool: Secondary bacterial pneumonia, particularly from pathogens like Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pneumoniae, is a notable complication post-influenza infection, commonly presenting with fever, productive cough, pleuritic chest pain, dyspnea, and sometimes purulent sputum, often occurring after initial improvement from the viral illness.
  27. 27Aspirin bottle + skull: For children with viral illnesses like influenza, aspirin is contraindicated due to the associated risk of Reye's syndrome (aspirin uncouples oxidative phosphorylation in hepatic mitochondria, causing ↓ATP with impaired β-oxidation (fatty liver), defective urea cycle (hyperammonemia → cerebral edema), and impaired gluconeogenesis (hypoglycemia)).
  28. 28Sun rays: For children with viral illnesses like influenza, aspirin is contraindicated due to the associated risk of Reye's syndrome (aspirin uncouples oxidative phosphorylation in hepatic mitochondria, causing ↓ATP with impaired β-oxidation (fatty liver), defective urea cycle (hyperammonemia → cerebral edema), and impaired gluconeogenesis (hypoglycemia)).
  29. 29Red turban: Reye's syndrome may present clinically as encephalopathy (vomiting, confusion, seizures), fatty liver (hepatic steatosis, hepatomegaly, ↑transaminases), and potential hepatic failure (hypoglycemia, coagulopathy, coma).
  30. 30Fat cow + liver spot: Reye's syndrome may present clinically as encephalopathy (vomiting, confusion, seizures), fatty liver (hepatic steatosis, hepatomegaly, ↑transaminases), and potential hepatic failure (hypoglycemia, coagulopathy, coma).
  31. 31Boar toy + grabbing leg: In rare cases, influenza virus infection can lead to Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS), which manifests as ascending symmetric weakness and paralysis (starting in the legs), areflexia, paresthesias, and sometimes respiratory muscle involvement, with cerebrospinal fluid showing albuminocytologic dissociation (high protein, normal white blood cell count).

57. Intestinal Nematodes Enterobius, Ancylostoma, Necator, Ascaris, Strongyloides, and Trichinella

Exam snapshot
  • Pinworm (Enterobius): nocturnal perianal itching in a child; Scotch tape test; pyrantel pamoate (treat household).
  • Hookworms (Ancylostoma, Necator): larvae penetrate bare feet, then lungs, then gut; iron deficiency anemia; eggs in stool; albendazole/pyrantel.
  • Ascaris: ingested eggs, lung migration (Loeffler), giant worm can cause intestinal obstruction; eggs in stool; albendazole.
  • Strongyloides: skin penetration plus autoinfection; larvae (not eggs) in stool; hyperinfection with steroids/HTLV-1; ivermectin.
  • Trichinella: undercooked pork or bear; periorbital edema, fever, myalgias, high CK; encysts in striated muscle; albendazole.
  • Eosinophilia is common in tissue-migrating nematodes; skin-penetrators (hookworm, Strongyloides) go blood to lungs to gut.
  1. 1Vermin lady entering wall: Enterobius vermicularis (pinworm) is an intestinal nematode (roundworm). It is the most common helminth infection in the US, especially in school-age children and daycare or household settings.
  2. 2Rocks outside hole: Gravid female worms migrate out of the anus at night to lay eggs on the perianal skin, causing intense perianal pruritus (worse at night). Pruritus leads to scratching and disrupted sleep.
  3. 3Rats eating by egg rocks: Transmission is fecal-oral: infective eggs on contaminated hands, bedding, toys, and surfaces are ingested. Scratching puts eggs under the fingernails, causing autoinfection and rapid spread within households.
  4. 4Scotch tape cape: Scotch tape test: press clear adhesive tape to the perianal skin (best done in the morning before bathing) and examine it microscopically for eggs. Stool O&P is usually negative because eggs are laid on the skin, not in the gut.⭐ Diagnose pinworm with the tape test, not a stool exam.
  5. 5"PAM!" + vermin lady: Pyrantel pamoate is the treatment for pinworm; it is a nicotinic agonist that causes depolarizing spastic paralysis of the worm, which is then expelled. Treat the whole household and repeat in about 2 weeks.
  6. 6Bendy metal bars: Albendazole is a broad-spectrum benzimidazole anthelmintic active against Enterobius, hookworms (Ancylostoma, Necator), Ascaris, Strongyloides, and Trichinella. It binds beta-tubulin and inhibits microtubule formation in the worm. It is contraindicated in the first trimester of pregnancy (teratogenic).
  7. 7America dude: Ancylostoma duodenale (Old World hookworm) and Necator americanus (New World hookworm) are the two hookworms. Both are intestinal nematodes.
  8. 8Rings around ankle: Ancylostoma duodenale is the Old World hookworm (Europe, Asia, Africa). It is an intestinal nematode with teeth-like cutting plates in its mouth.
  9. 9Rings around neck: Necator americanus is the New World hookworm (the Americas and sub-Saharan Africa). It is an intestinal nematode with cutting plates in its mouth.
  10. 10Hook: Hookworms are roundworms with a hook-like (curved) head and cutting mouthparts that attach to the small-intestinal wall and feed on blood.
  11. 11Red boots on America dude: Hookworm larvae in contaminated soil penetrate intact skin, classically of bare feet (going barefoot is the risk factor). The skin entry can cause a pruritic rash (ground itch).
  12. 12Arrow symbol on America dude: After skin penetration the larvae travel via the bloodstream to the lungs, ascend the trachea, and are swallowed at the pharynx. They mature in the small intestine, attach, suck blood, and release eggs in stool to continue the cycle.
  13. 13Iron + small pale steam holes: Chronic intestinal blood loss from attached worms causes iron deficiency anemia (microcytic, hypochromic). Think of it in a patient from an endemic area or with a barefoot-exposure history.
  14. 14Grenades in water: Diagnosis is by finding eggs in stool (O&P). Hookworm eggs are oval with a thin shell and a segmented (multi-cell) embryo inside.
  15. 15Bilobed slingshot kid + pink granules: Eosinophilia is common, especially during larval migration through the tissues. Eosinophils are bilobed with bright red-orange granules.
  16. 16"PAM!" + America dude: Pyrantel pamoate treats hookworm infection (nicotinic agonist causing spastic paralysis of the worm). Albendazole or mebendazole are alternatives, and iron is given for anemia.
  17. 17Lumbering tree man: Ascaris lumbricoides is the giant human roundworm, the largest intestinal nematode. Infection occurs by ingesting embryonated eggs from soil-contaminated hands, vegetables, or water (fecal-oral).
  18. 18Arrow symbol on lumbering tree man: Ingested eggs hatch in the small intestine, and larvae penetrate the gut wall and travel via the bloodstream to the lungs. They ascend the trachea, are swallowed at the pharynx, and mature into adults in the small intestine; eggs pass in stool.
  19. 19Respiratory leaf: During lung migration, larvae can cause cough, wheezing, and transient pulmonary infiltrates with eosinophilia (Loeffler syndrome).
  20. 20Obstructing rubble: A heavy worm burden can cause intestinal obstruction (classically at the ileocecal valve), and may also cause biliary obstruction or pancreatitis if worms migrate. Obstruction is most common in children.⭐ Large ball of worms -> small bowel obstruction in a child from an endemic area.
  21. 21Acorns in water: Diagnosis is by finding characteristic eggs in stool; fertilized eggs are round with a thick, bumpy (mammillated) shell. Adult worms may also be seen passed in stool or vomit.
  22. 22Pink granules by lumbering tree man: Eosinophilia is a common finding, especially during the tissue-migration phase. Treatment is albendazole or mebendazole (pyrantel is an alternative).
  23. 23Strong guy: Strongyloides stercoralis (threadworm) is an intestinal nematode. Unlike the other worms in this video, it can complete its life cycle within the host, which allows persistence for decades.
  24. 24Red boots on strong guy: Filariform (infective) larvae in soil penetrate intact skin, classically of the feet, just like hookworms.
  25. 25Arrow symbol on strong guy: After skin penetration, larvae go through the bloodstream to the lungs, up the trachea, and are swallowed. Adult females embed in the small-intestinal mucosa and lay eggs that hatch there, so rhabditiform larvae (not eggs) are passed in stool; some mature into filariform larvae and cause autoinfection.
  26. 26Rocks inside hole: Autoinfection: rhabditiform larvae molt into filariform larvae inside the gut, penetrate the intestinal wall or perianal skin, and re-enter the cycle. This lets infection persist for decades and, in immunosuppressed patients, causes hyperinfection and disseminated disease (including gram-negative sepsis).⭐ Corticosteroids / HTLV-1 -> Strongyloides hyperinfection; screen before starting steroids.
  27. 27Larval worms in water: Diagnosis is by finding rhabditiform larvae in stool (not eggs, in contrast to the other nematodes). Stool is often negative at first, so serology or repeated stool exams may be needed.⭐ Larvae, not eggs, in stool.
  28. 28Pink granules by strong guy: Eosinophilia is common in chronic strongyloidiasis. It may be absent in disseminated disease, a bad prognostic sign.
  29. 29"No dumping, drains to river": Ivermectin is the treatment of choice for Strongyloides; it activates glutamate-gated chloride channels, causing paralysis of the worm. Albendazole is an alternative, and treatment must also be given to carriers before immunosuppression.
  30. 30Porky trickster + spiral suit: Trichinella spiralis (pork worm) is a nematode that causes trichinosis. Its life cycle includes an intestinal phase and a muscle phase.
  31. 31Pig: Infection occurs by eating undercooked or raw meat containing encysted larvae. Classic sources are pork and wild game such as bear.
  32. 32Glasses: Larval migration causes a hypersensitivity/vasculitic reaction, producing periorbital edema (puffy eyes), often with conjunctival hemorrhages.
  33. 33Green drool: The intestinal phase (adult worms in the gut, days 1-2 after ingestion) causes nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and abdominal pain.
  34. 34Sweat drops: The invasive phase (larvae migrating) causes fever, along with periorbital edema and myalgias.
  35. 35Red pipes: Larvae released in the gut penetrate the intestinal wall, enter the bloodstream, and are carried to skeletal (striated) muscle. Larval spread in the blood is what triggers fever and edema.
  36. 36Striated brick: Larvae preferentially invade striated muscle (e.g., extraocular muscles, diaphragm, masseters, tongue), where they encyst. Muscle biopsy shows encysted larvae.
  37. 37Explosives in brick: Larvae encyst within striated muscle cells and can calcify later. Muscle cell damage causes elevated creatine kinase (CK).
  38. 38Inflammatory fire: Larval encystment causes myositis, with muscle pain (myalgia), tenderness, and inflammation. Treatment is albendazole or mebendazole (steroids for severe symptoms).
  39. 39Pink granules by pig: Marked eosinophilia is typical. Diagnosis rests on history, eosinophilia, elevated CK, serology, and muscle biopsy.⭐ Eosinophilia + myalgia + periorbital edema after undercooked pork or bear meat = Trichinella.

58. JC Polyomavirus and BK Polyomavirus (Polyomaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: JC virus causes PML (progressive focal deficits in HIV with low CD4 or natalizumab use); BK virus causes nephropathy in kidney transplant and hemorrhagic cystitis in HSCT.
  • Microbiology: naked, circular, double-stranded DNA viruses; ubiquitous latent infection in kidneys; reactivate with immunosuppression.
  • Diagnosis: MRI (non-enhancing white-matter lesions) and CSF JC virus PCR for PML; urine/blood BK PCR and kidney biopsy (viral inclusions, SV40 stain) for BK nephropathy.
  • Treatment: restore immunity (ART, stop immunosuppressant or natalizumab); for BK, reduce immunosuppression.
  • Buzzwords: 'PML', 'CD4 <200', 'demyelination of oligodendrocytes', 'natalizumab', 'transplant', 'hemorrhagic cystitis', 'polyoma = many tumors'.
  1. 1Cool colors: Polyomaviruses (JC virus, BK virus) are DNA viruses that are ubiquitous in the general population; clinical disease typically occurs only when the immune system is weakened.
  2. 2Circular room + double stranded DNA design: Polyomaviruses have circular, double-stranded DNA genomes.
  3. 3Statue of David: Polyomaviruses lack an envelope (naked viruses).
  4. 4Julius Caesar: JC virus (John Cunningham virus), a DNA virus in the Polyomaviridae family; the JC initials match Julius Caesar.
  5. 5Immunocompromised cane: The JC virus mainly affects immunocompromised individuals; it reactivates in HIV infection or other immunosuppression, and PML is an AIDS-defining illness in HIV-infected patients.
  6. 6AIDS ribbon: The JC virus mainly affects immunocompromised individuals; it reactivates in HIV infection or other immunosuppression, and PML is an AIDS-defining illness in HIV-infected patients.
  7. 7'Senator Count 200': JC virus is more likely to reactivate in HIV patients with a CD4+ count below 200 cells/mm3.
  8. 8'PML' bust: JC virus causes progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML), a rare, often fatal brain disease in immunocompromised patients. 'Progressive' = worsens over time, 'multifocal' = multiple brain regions, 'leukoencephalopathy' = white matter disease via demyelination.⭐ Also occurs with natalizumab, rituximab, and other immunosuppressants.
  9. 9Peeling table legs: PML is caused by JC virus destruction of oligodendrocytes, causing demyelination, disrupted conduction, and progressive deficits, often in parietal and occipital white matter, with hemiparesis, visual field defects, and cognitive impairment.
  10. 10White laurel leaves: MRI in PML shows non-enhancing, multifocal demyelinating lesions in subcortical and periventricular white matter, typically without mass effect or edema. Lesions are bright (hyperintense on T2) from demyelination and do not enhance.⭐ Contrast with CNS toxoplasmosis (ring-enhancing lesions with mass effect).
  11. 11Brutus' knife: BK virus, a DNA virus in the Polyomaviridae family (BK = Brutus, the knife-wielder).
  12. 12Flank stab wound: In immunosuppression, BK virus can reactivate and cause BK virus-associated nephropathy, with progressive renal dysfunction and rising creatinine in kidney transplant recipients.⭐ Biopsy: viral inclusions in tubular cells.
  13. 13Crotch blood: In immunocompromised patients, BK virus can cause hemorrhagic cystitis with hematuria and painful urination.⭐ Differential includes adenovirus and cyclophosphamide toxicity.
  14. 14Organs: BK nephropathy mainly occurs in kidney transplant recipients when immunosuppression allows reactivation, causing tubulointerstitial nephritis and graft failure. In hematopoietic stem cell transplant patients BK more often causes hemorrhagic cystitis.⭐ Treatment is reducing immunosuppression.

59. Klebsiella pneumoniae, Enterobacter spp., Serratia marcescens

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: hospitalized or ventilated patient with pneumonia or catheter UTI; Klebsiella in alcoholics/diabetics with lobar pneumonia and currant jelly sputum
  • Gram-negative rods, Enterobacteriaceae; Klebsiella and Enterobacter ferment lactose (Serratia is a slow/non-lactose fermenter)
  • Klebsiella: thick capsule, nonmotile, urease positive; Enterobacter and Serratia: motile; Serratia makes red prodigiosin
  • Treatment: often multidrug resistant (ESBL, carbapenemase/KPC); carbapenems, or newer agents for CRE
  • Buzzwords: currant jelly sputum, 3 A's (Aspiration, Abscess, Alcoholics/diAbetics), red pigment Serratia
  1. 1Red hues: Enterobacter spp., Serratia marcescens and Klebsiella pneumoniae are gram-negative bacilli in the Enterobacteriaceae family.
  2. 2Oxygen mask: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella can cause pneumonia (especially ventilator-associated).
  3. 3Urine bag: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella can cause urinary tract infections (especially catheter-associated).
  4. 4Hospital: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella are significant nosocomial (hospital-acquired) pathogens.
  5. 5Pills: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella commonly show multidrug resistance (ESBLs, carbapenemases).⭐ Carbapenem-resistant Enterobacterales (CRE) are a major threat.
  6. 6Pink milk carton: These organisms are classically grouped as lactose fermenters that give pink colonies on MacConkey agar; Klebsiella and Enterobacter ferment lactose readily, whereas Serratia may appear as a slow or non-lactose fermenter.⭐ Lactose fermentation = pink colonies on MacConkey; Serratia is often non-lactose fermenting.
  7. 7Pterodactyl: Enterobacter spp. are gram-negative bacilli.
  8. 8Flying pterodactyl: Enterobacter spp. are motile.
  9. 9Triceratops: Serratia marcescens is a gram-negative bacillus.
  10. 10Triceratops charging: Serratia marcescens is motile.
  11. 11Red triceratops residue: Serratia marcescens produces prodigiosin, a distinctive red pigment (red colonies, red-tinged sputum or urine).
  12. 12"Kleb" tail: Klebsiella pneumoniae is a gram-negative bacillus.
  13. 133 "A" kleb tail: Klebsiella pneumonia is notably common in alcoholics (and diabetics) and causes lung abscesses and aspiration pneumonia.⭐ The 3 A's: Aspiration pneumonia, Abscess in lungs, Alcoholics/diAbetics.
  14. 14Spike coverings: Klebsiella is surrounded by a prominent polysaccharide capsule.⭐ Capsule is the main virulence factor; mucoid colonies.
  15. 15Currant jelly: Sputum in Klebsiella pneumonia is viscous, blood-tinged and resembles currant jelly.⭐ Currant jelly sputum is classic for Klebsiella.
  16. 16Stuck in jelly: Klebsiella is nonmotile.⭐ Klebsiella is the nonmotile one among these three.
  17. 17Cavitary lesion CXR: Klebsiella pneumonia can cause pulmonary necrosis and cavitation (often upper lobes), mimicking tuberculosis.
  18. 18Ammonia bottle: Klebsiella produces urease (urease positive), which hydrolyzes urea into carbon dioxide and ammonia.⭐ Other urease-positive organisms: Proteus, H. pylori.

60. Legionella pneumophila

  1. 1The legion battleship represents Legionella pneumophila, a gram-negative bacillus.
  2. 2The silver paint over red represents that Legionella pneumophila requires a special silver stain for proper visualization, as it stains poorly with Gram stain.
  3. 3The blue oxidase ring represents that Legionella pneumophila is catalase- and oxidase-producing (oxidase positive), and this plays a vital role in aerobic respiration.
  4. 4The charcoal represents that Legionella pneumophila is optimally cultured on buffered charcoal yeast extract (BCYE) agar, which is enriched with iron plus cysteine as an essential growth factor.
  5. 5The S.S. Cysteine + iron anchor represents that Legionella pneumophila requires cysteine and iron for its growth, both of which are incorporated into buffered charcoal yeast extract (BCYE) agar.
  6. 6The steaming Pontiac represents Pontiac fever, caused by Legionella pneumophila, which manifests as a brief, self-resolving respiratory illness with symptoms resembling a mild flu.
  7. 7The patchy lower lung blueprint represents that in Legionnaires' disease, a severe form of pneumonia caused by Legionella pneumophila, chest X-rays often reveal patchy lobar infiltrates that progress to consolidation, predominantly in the mid-to-lower zones.
  8. 8The falling salt represents that Legionnaires' disease can cause electrolyte disturbances, such as hyponatremia, hypokalemia, and hypophosphatemia.
  9. 9The clutching head represents that neurological manifestations of Legionnaires' disease can include symptoms like headache and confusion.
  10. 10The brown paint represents that diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting are common extrapulmonary symptoms of Legionnaires' disease.
  11. 11The burning furnace represents that patients with Legionnaires' disease often present with a significant fever, usually surpassing 39°C (102.2°F).
  12. 12The cigarette represents that cigarette smokers are more susceptible to developing Legionnaires' disease (as well as other pulmonary infections).
  13. 13The pee stream represents that the diagnosis of Legionnaires' disease is often established through a urine antigen test, which detects specific bacterial antigens in the patient's urine.
  14. 14The macrolide crows represent that macrolides, particularly azithromycin, are effective against Legionella pneumophila.
  15. 15The brown paint (Fluoroquinolones) represents that fluoroquinolones, including levofloxacin and moxifloxacin, are effective against Legionella pneumophila.

61. Leishmania braziliensis & donovani (Blood and tissue protozoa)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: L. braziliensis causes skin ulcers (cutaneous/mucocutaneous); L. donovani causes fever, weight loss, huge spleen, pancytopenia after sandfly exposure.
  • Vector: Phlebotomine sandfly (promastigotes transmitted); amastigotes live inside macrophages.
  • Diagnosis: Giemsa-stained biopsy/aspirate showing amastigotes with a kinetoplast in macrophages.
  • Treatment: sodium stibogluconate (cutaneous and visceral); liposomal amphotericin B first-line for visceral disease.
  • Buzzwords: kala-azar (black fever), hepatosplenomegaly, pancytopenia, macrophages with amastigotes.
  1. 1Brazilian belt: Leishmania braziliensis is a protozoan that causes cutaneous and mucocutaneous leishmaniasis (New World).
  2. 2Undead human: Humans and other vertebrates (e.g., dogs, rodents) serve as reservoir hosts for Leishmania braziliensis.
  3. 3Zombie skin: L. braziliensis infection causes cutaneous leishmaniasis: papules that evolve into ulcerative lesions that often scar.⭐ L. braziliensis can spread to cause destructive mucocutaneous disease of the nose and mouth.
  4. 4Sandfly swarm: Phlebotomine sandflies are the vector; they inject the promastigote (flagellated) form of the parasite.⭐ Sandfly transmits promastigotes.
  5. 5Goats in cages: Inside the host, promastigotes transform into amastigotes, the non-flagellated intracellular form that replicates within macrophages.
  6. 6Many dots on spot on goats in cages: On Giemsa-stained smears, amastigotes appear within macrophages as small round to oval organisms, each with a nucleus and a rod-shaped kinetoplast.⭐ Macrophages filled with amastigotes (Leishman-Donovan bodies) are diagnostic.
  7. 7Man named Donovan: Leishmania donovani is a protozoan that causes visceral leishmaniasis (Old World).
  8. 8Dark skin spots: L. donovani causes visceral leishmaniasis, also called kala-azar ('black fever'), named for the skin hyperpigmentation seen in severe cases; post-kala-azar dermal leishmaniasis (hypopigmented lesions) may follow recovery.⭐ Kala-azar means 'black fever' in Hindi.
  9. 9Sweat: Visceral leishmaniasis presents with irregular (often double-quotidian) fever, weight loss, hepatosplenomegaly, and pancytopenia.
  10. 10Barren pan: Pancytopenia results from bone marrow involvement and splenic sequestration (empty pan = low blood cells); other features include fever, weight loss, and hepatosplenomegaly.
  11. 11Liver + spleen spots: Parasitized macrophages in the liver and spleen cause hepatosplenomegaly (massive splenomegaly in visceral disease).⭐ Hypergammaglobulinemia is also common.
  12. 12T-bone steak: Sodium stibogluconate (a pentavalent antimony) is used for both cutaneous and visceral leishmaniasis.
  13. 13Amphibians: Amphotericin B (liposomal) is a first-line treatment for leishmaniasis, particularly visceral leishmaniasis.⭐ Miltefosine is an oral alternative.

62. Leptospira interrogans (Leptospirosis, Spirochetes)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: fever, myalgias (calf), conjunctival suffusion after freshwater exposure (floods, swimming, sewage workers, farmers).
  • Thin, tightly coiled spirochete with hooked ends (question mark); seen on dark-field, not Gram stain.
  • Reservoirs: rodents and dogs shed in urine; enters via skin cuts or mucous membranes.
  • Severe form (Weil disease): jaundice, acute kidney injury, hemorrhage; treat with penicillin or doxycycline (watch for Jarisch-Herxheimer).
  • Buzzwords: Weil disease, conjunctival suffusion, flood water, dog or rat urine.
  1. 1Spiral wave: Leptospira interrogans is a spirochete bacterium.
  2. 2Question mark + spiral leash: L. interrogans has a tight helical coil with hooked ends resembling a question mark on dark-field microscopy.⭐ Visualized by dark-field microscopy; diagnosed by serology (MAT) or PCR.
  3. 3Tropical oasis + yellow water: Leptospira is common in tropical regions and in animals such as rodents and dogs; it is excreted in urine and transmitted to humans through contact with contaminated water (e.g., swimming or flood exposure).⭐ Risk groups: sewer workers, farmers, and people exposed to floods or freshwater.
  4. 4Sweat: Fever is the typical initial symptom, often with flu-like symptoms: headache, myalgia (especially calf), and malaise.⭐ Biphasic course: septicemic phase then immune phase.
  5. 5Pink glasses: Conjunctival suffusion (redness of the conjunctiva without purulent exudate) is frequently seen early in leptospirosis.⭐ Non-purulent conjunctival suffusion is a classic clue.
  6. 6Whale: Weil disease is the severe, potentially life-threatening form of leptospirosis, with jaundice, hemorrhage, renal failure, and respiratory distress.⭐ Weil disease = jaundice plus renal failure plus bleeding.
  7. 7Disseminated red floaties: Leptospira disseminates hematogenously, causing multi-organ involvement.
  8. 8Kidney dinghy: Acute kidney injury (azotemia with elevated BUN and creatinine, electrolyte disturbances, reduced urine output, proteinuria or hematuria) is a major complication of Weil disease; liver involvement is also seen.
  9. 9Yellow wetsuit: Liver involvement presents with jaundice, elevated bilirubin, and mild to moderate transaminase elevation (usually without marked hepatocellular necrosis).⭐ Penicillin or doxycycline treats; Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction can occur.
Show the original legend picture

63. Listeria monocytogenes

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: pregnant woman with fever (to neonatal sepsis/meningitis), newborn, elderly or immunocompromised patient with meningitis after deli meats/unpasteurized dairy
  • Gram-positive rod; beta-hemolytic (narrow zone), catalase positive, tumbling motility at 25 C, grows at 4 C, facultative intracellular
  • Actin rockets (ActA) allow cell-to-cell spread; listeriolysin O escapes phagosome
  • Dx: CSF/blood culture; Treatment: ampicillin (plus gentamicin in severe disease); cephalosporins are NOT active
  • Buzzwords: cold enrichment, tumbling motility, actin rocket tails, unpasteurized soft cheese
  1. 1Santa's list: Listeria monocytogenes is a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple rod-shaped ornaments: Listeria monocytogenes is a gram-positive rod.
  3. 3Beta light bulb: Listeria is beta-hemolytic, but often produces only a narrow zone of hemolysis on blood agar.
  4. 4Tumbling ornaments: In broth culture, Listeria shows characteristic tumbling motility (best at room temperature, about 25 C).⭐ Motile at 25 C, poorly at 37 C.
  5. 5Rocket: Listeria forms actin "rocket tails" (via ActA) in host cells, driving intracellular movement and cell-to-cell spread.⭐ Listeriolysin O lets it escape the phagolysosome.
  6. 6Rocket in bag: Listeria is a facultative intracellular pathogen, able to live both inside and outside host cells.⭐ Cell-mediated immunity (T cells) is needed for control, hence risk in pregnancy, elderly and immunocompromised.
  7. 7Catalase cat: Listeria produces catalase (catalase positive), which converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen.
  8. 8Icicles: Listeria can survive and multiply at refrigerator temperatures (cold enrichment), contributing to spread in refrigerated foods.
  9. 9Milk: Listeriosis outbreaks are frequently associated with dairy products such as unpasteurized milk, soft cheeses and ice cream.
  10. 10Soft cheese: Listeriosis outbreaks are frequently associated with soft cheeses (and also deli meats and unpasteurized dairy).⭐ Also deli meats and hot dogs.
  11. 11Pregnant woman: Pregnant women are much more susceptible to listeriosis than the general population because of immunologic changes in pregnancy; infection can cause fever, miscarriage, stillbirth or neonatal infection.⭐ Can cross the placenta (transplacental) and cause granulomatosis infantiseptica.
  12. 12Baby in meningitis helmet: Listeria monocytogenes is a significant cause of neonatal meningitis (also early-onset sepsis).⭐ Neonatal meningitis: Group B Strep, E. coli, Listeria.
  13. 13Old Santa Claus: Listeria is a significant cause of meningitis in adults over 60 and in immunocompromised patients, owing to impaired cell-mediated immunity.⭐ Also a cause in patients with impaired T-cell immunity (transplant, steroids).
  14. 14Amp: Ampicillin is the primary antibiotic for Listeria infections (gentamicin is added in severe disease).⭐ Cephalosporins are not active against Listeria, so add ampicillin to empiric meningitis regimens in at-risk patients.
Show the original legend picture

64. Lymphocytic Choriomeningitis Virus (LCMV) (Arenaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: often asymptomatic; flu-like illness that can progress to aseptic meningitis; congenital infection causes hydrocephalus and chorioretinitis; exposure to house mice or pet hamsters.
  • Micro: enveloped, helical, bisegmented (2 segments) ambisense ssRNA virus; sandy granules on EM from host ribosomes.
  • Diagnosis: serology (IgM/IgG) and RT-PCR; CSF lymphocytic pleocytosis.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; avoid rodent contact and excreta; pregnant women should avoid exposure.
  • Buzzwords: rodent-borne, house mouse, ambisense, sandy appearance, aseptic meningitis, congenital chorioretinitis.
  1. 1Arena: Arenaviruses (e.g. LCMV) are RNA viruses.
  2. 2Warm colors: Arenaviruses are RNA viruses (warm colors = RNA virus).
  3. 3Moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Arenaviruses are classified as negative-sense RNA viruses, although they also contain regions read in the positive sense (ambisense).
  4. 4Enveloping toga: Arenaviruses are enveloped viruses.
  5. 5Ambidextrous: Arenaviruses are ambisense: though classified as negative-sense, their segments contain both positive- and negative-sense regions.⭐ Ambisense genome is the distinguishing feature of arenaviruses.
  6. 6Column spirals: Arenavirus nucleocapsids are helical (elongated, coiled).
  7. 72 circles: Arenaviruses have a segmented genome of 2 RNA segments (L and S), which are circular.
  8. 8Sandy arena: Arenaviruses look granular ("sandy") on electron microscopy because they incorporate host ribosomes into virions during budding; the ribosomes are nonfunctional.⭐ "Sandy" granules = host ribosomes.
  9. 9Rodents: Rodents are the primary reservoir (rodent-borne viruses, roboviruses); humans are infected through contact with or inhalation of rodent urine, feces or saliva, or, for LCMV, transplacentally from mother to fetus.⭐ LCMV reservoir is the common house mouse (also pet hamsters).
  10. 10"LCV": Lymphocytic choriomeningitis virus (LCMV) is an ambisense RNA virus in the Arenaviridae family.
  11. 11Sweat: LCMV infection is often asymptomatic; when symptomatic it causes systemic symptoms (fever, anorexia, myalgias, headache, nausea, vomiting) and can progress to aseptic meningitis or meningoencephalitis (fever, headache, stiff neck, photophobia).⭐ Aseptic meningitis with lymphocytic CSF.
  12. 12Red turban covering head + encircling neck: Same: neurologic involvement (meningitis with headache, stiff neck, photophobia). Congenital infection can lead to hydrocephalus, chorioretinitis and developmental delay.⭐ Congenital LCMV mimics TORCH infections.
  13. 13Elevated flame: LCMV can be inactivated by heat (denatures proteins) and by detergents or lipid solvents (dissolve the envelope).⭐ Enveloped viruses are easily inactivated by soap and solvents.
  14. 14Radiating light: LCMV can also be inactivated by UV light (damages nucleic acids) and chemical disinfectants (destroy viral components).

65. M. avium, M. scrofulaceum, M. marinum

  1. 1Caged mockingbird: Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC) refers to Mycobacterium avium and Mycobacterium intracellulare.
  2. 2Crows: A macrolide (e.g., azithromycin or clarithromycin) combined with ethambutol is used to treat MAC infections.
  3. 3Sheriff Ethel: Ethambutol is used as part of MAC treatment.
  4. 4Immunocompromised cane: MAC is a common cause of disseminated disease in patients with AIDS.
  5. 5Speed limit 50: Macrolide prophylaxis is given for MAC when the CD4 count is below 50.
  6. 6Buttes: Rifabutin may be added as a third agent in treatment of MAC infection.
  7. 7Rifle: Rifampin is related to rifabutin.
  8. 8Speeding chrome bumper: Rifampin and rifabutin activate cytochrome P450.
  9. 9Leaping armadillo: Mycobacterium leprae is the causative agent of leprosy.
  10. 10Deputy zone: Dapsone is used to treat leprosy.
  11. 11Multi-lobed sand timer: Dapsone can cause agranulocytosis.
  12. 12Bite and seeds: Heinz bodies (denatured hemoglobin) form in red blood cells under oxidative stress and are removed by splenic macrophages, creating bite cells.
  13. 13Broken G6PD-free fruit: Dapsone can cause hemolytic anemia in patients with G6PD deficiency.
  14. 14Cloth: Clofazimine is used to treat lepromatous leprosy.
  15. 15Lion bandana: Leonine facies is seen in lepromatous leprosy.
  16. 16Rifle: Rifampin is used to treat leprosy.

66. Malassezia furfur

Exam snapshot
  • Pityriasis (tinea) versicolor: well-demarcated, finely scaling hypo- or hyperpigmented patches on the chest and back, usually non-itchy, in hot humid conditions.
  • Lipophilic yeast (normal skin flora); infection limited to the stratum corneum; lipases produce azelaic acid that inhibits melanocytes.
  • KOH prep shows 'spaghetti and meatballs' (short hyphae plus yeast clusters); fluoresces yellow-green under Wood's lamp.
  • Topical selenium sulfide (Selsun Blue), or topical azoles; oral fluconazole or itraconazole for extensive disease.
  • Spaghetti and meatballs, TPN lipids fungemia in neonates, seborrheic dermatitis, hot and humid conditions.
  1. 1"Ristorante Italiano di Malassezia": Malassezia furfur is a fungus that causes pityriasis (tinea) versicolor and seborrheic dermatitis.
  2. 2Fur coat: M. furfur: the fur in the name; a fungus causing pityriasis versicolor and seborrheic dermatitis.
  3. 3"Versi di calore sauce": Versi = versicolor: the sauce on the chest and back represents pityriasis versicolor, caused by M. furfur (calore = heat, a clue to hot humid climates).
  4. 4KOH salt: On KOH preparation of skin scrapings, M. furfur shows short hyphae and round yeast clusters.
  5. 5Spaghetti and meatballs: The KOH finding is classically described as "spaghetti and meatballs": short hyphae (spaghetti) and round yeast (meatballs).⭐ Spaghetti and meatballs = Malassezia.
  6. 6Heat lamp: Normally a harmless commensal yeast, M. furfur can convert to its pathogenic mycelial form in hot, humid conditions, which promote filamentous growth.
  7. 7Sweating chef: Same point: heat, humidity, and sweating favor the transition to the pathogenic hyphal form.
  8. 8Sauce on chest and back: Pityriasis versicolor presents as oval, well-demarcated patches or plaques with fine scale on the chest and back; they may be hypopigmented, hyperpigmented, or erythematous, can merge, and are usually not itchy.⭐ Hypopigmented patches do not tan; fluoresce under Wood's lamp.
  9. 9Olive oil: M. furfur is lipophilic, thriving on lipid-rich sebum; its lipases break down lipids into fatty acids (e.g. azelaic acid), which damage melanocytes and cause the pigment changes.
  10. 10Broken olive oil bottle: Same point: lipase-mediated lipid breakdown yields acids that injure melanocytes, causing pigment change.
  11. 11Corn on lasagna: M. furfur infection is superficial, confined to the stratum corneum of the epidermis, so there is little inflammation.
  12. 12Baby slurping noodle: M. furfur fungemia is a rare complication in neonates receiving total parenteral nutrition (TPN) through central venous catheters. Lipid-rich TPN supports yeast growth and catheter colonization, leading to bloodstream infection, sepsis, and thrombocytopenia.⭐ Lipid emulsions in TPN are the risk factor.
  13. 13Blue sun: Pityriasis versicolor is treated with topical selenium sulfide (Selsun Blue), which promotes shedding of the stratum corneum where the fungus lives.⭐ Also ketoconazole shampoo.

67. Moraxella catarrhalis

  1. 1Moray eel-cat: Moraxella catarrhalis is a gram-negative diplococcus.
  2. 2Glow of red flames: Moraxella catarrhalis is a gram negative diplococcus.
  3. 3Nose of plane: M. catarrhalis can colonize the nasopharynx.
  4. 4Blue gem ring: M. catarrhalis is an oxidase positive aerobe; can form a biofilm, produces beta-lactamase; positive hockey-puck sign.
  5. 5Bellows-fish: M. catarrhalis is an oxidase positive aerobe; can form a biofilm, produces beta-lactamase; positive hockey-puck sign.
  6. 6Algae: M. catarrhalis is an oxidase positive aerobe; can form a biofilm (bolsters its resistance against certain antibiotics), produces beta-lactamase; positive hockey-puck sign.
  7. 7Broken beta-lactam gyroscope: M. catarrhalis is an oxidase positive aerobe; can produce beta lactamase (an enzyme enabling the breakdown of beta-lactam antibiotics, including penicillins); positive hockey puck sign.
  8. 8Sliding plane wheel: M. catarrhalis is an oxidase positive aerobe; forms biofilms, produces beta-lactamase; positive hockey puck sign (indicating the smooth movement of colonies across agar surfaces).
  9. 9Amelia Ear-hurt: M. catarrhalis is a common cause of bacterial otitis media (OM) in children, often becoming chronically colonized.
  10. 10Red leaky plane nose: M. catarrhalis is a common cause of bacterial sinusitis.
  11. 11Blue bloater fish: M. catarrhalis is associated with Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) exacerbations and COPD-related bronchopneumonia.
  12. 12Cephalopod: M. catarrhalis is sensitive to cephalosporins, amoxicillin-clavulanate, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX), fluoroquinolones.
  13. 13Box of “AMMO” + clarinet harpoon: M. catarrhalis is sensitive to cephalosporins, amoxicillin-clavulanate, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX), fluoroquinolones.
  14. 14Rotten sulfa-eggs: M. catarrhalis is sensitive to cephalosporins, amoxicillin-clavulanate, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX), fluoroquinolones.
  15. 15Flower anemones: M. catarrhalis is sensitive to cephalosporins, amoxicillin-clavulanate, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX), fluoroquinolones.

68. Mucor spp. & Rhizopus spp. (Mucormycosis)

No Sketchy video/scene available for this organism in the current deck — notes below are compiled directly from your Anki cards.

ID / Micro
  • Non-septate, wide-angle (90°) branching hyphae (contrast with Aspergillus's septate, acute-angle hyphae)
  • Rhizopus = bread mold; transmitted via spore inhalation
  • Deferoxamine acts as a siderophore for Rhizopus, increasing its growth/pathogenicity and mucormycosis risk
Clinical
  • Classic setting: diabetic ketoacidosis (most common predisposing factor) and leukemic/immunocompromised patients (glucocorticoids, HIV/AIDS)
  • Proliferates in blood vessel walls, especially with excess glucose/ketone bodies
  • Headache, facial pain, black necrotic eschar on the face; penetrates the cribriform plate into the brain → rhinocerebral and frontal lobe abscesses, cavernous sinus thrombosis
Treatment
  • Amphotericin B plus surgical excision of necrotic tissue.

69. Mycobacterium leprae

  1. 1The leaping armadillo represents Mycobacterium leprae, an acid-fast bacillus.
  2. 2Armadillo County Jail represents that armadillos are the only known nonhuman hosts of Mycobacterium leprae, as their low body temperature makes them susceptible to infection, and they can transmit the bacterium to humans.
  3. 3The "Hansen" the armadillo represents that M. leprae causes leprosy (Hansen's disease).
  4. 4The icicles represent that Mycobacterium leprae prefers cooler temperatures, often affecting the skin and extremities.
  5. 5The mycolic acid tassels represent that Mycobacterium leprae stains acid-fast due to the high mycolic acid content in its cell membrane.
  6. 6The quick draw fuschia gun and holster represents that the Ziehl-Neelsen method uses carbol fuchsin stain to detect acid-fast bacteria, such as Mycobacterium leprae.
  7. 7The jail cell 1 represents that tuberculoid leprosy elicits a Th1 cell-mediated immune response, resulting in macrophage activation and containment of the bacteria.
  8. 8The prisoner in jail cell 1 represents that a strong Th1 cell-mediated immune response induces macrophage phagocytosis of Mycobacterium leprae bacteria, resulting in the tuberculoid variant of leprosy.
  9. 9The bald prisoner represents that tuberculoid leprosy typically presents with a small number of skin lesions that are flat or mildly elevated, often lacking hair.
  10. 10Shovel in dirt mound represents that the lepromin skin test, which involves injecting inactivated bacteria under the skin, is a diagnostic tool that helps classify the type of leprosy in an already diagnosed patient: a positive result, where a nodule forms, is typically associated with a stronger cellular immune response, more common in tuberculoid leprosy; a negative result, where no nodule forms, is associated with lepromatous leprosy, a more severe disease characterized by a weaker immune response, more disseminated lesions, and a higher bacterial load.
  11. 11The jail cell 2 represents lepromatous leprosy, characterized by a weaker Th1 response and a dominant Th2 humoral response, which fails to contain Mycobacterium leprae bacteria, leading to more disseminated disease.
  12. 12The laughing escapee from cell 2 represents that a Th2 humoral immune response in lepromatous leprosy is correlated with a weak or nonexistent Th1 cell-mediated immune response, preventing the containment of Mycobacterium leprae bacteria within macrophages.
  13. 13Touching hands represents that human-to-human transmission is a route for lepromatous leprosy, typically requiring close contact over many months.
  14. 14Gloves and socks represent that lepromatous leprosy often presents with stocking and glove neuropathy, characterized by loss of sensitivity to temperature and pain in the extremities.
  15. 15Long John lesions represent that lepromatous leprosy lesions often favor cooler areas of the body, particularly the extensor surfaces of the arms and legs, and the face (especially earlobes and nose), and the buttocks. These lesions are typically symmetric, poorly demarcated, and can coalesce into larger plaques or nodules over time.
  16. 16The lion face bandana represents that leonine facies deformity seen in lepromatous leprosy involves thickening of the skin, loss of eyebrows and eyelashes, collapse of the nose, and formation of nodular earlobes.
  17. 17The deputy next to jail cell 1 represents that a 6-month regimen of dapsone and rifampin is commonly used in the treatment of tuberculoid leprosy.
  18. 18The rifle next to jail cell 1 represents that a 6-month regimen of dapsone and rifampin is commonly used in the treatment of tuberculoid leprosy.
  19. 19The deputy next to jail cell 2 represents that for lepromatous leprosy, a longer treatment course (often 2-3 years) including dapsone, rifampin, and clofazimine, is typically needed.
  20. 20The rifle next to jail cell 2 represents that for lepromatous leprosy, a longer treatment course (often 2-3 years) including dapsone, rifampin, and clofazimine, is typically needed.
  21. 21The cloth next to jail cell 2 represents that for lepromatous leprosy, a longer treatment course (often 2-3 years) including dapsone, rifampin, and clofazimine, is typically needed.

70. Mycobacterium tuberculosis

  1. 1Tuberculosis cactus: Mycobacterium tuberculosis is an acid-fast bacillus.
  2. 2Mycolic acid tassels: Mycobacterium tuberculosis stains acid-fast due to the high mycolic acid content of its cell membrane.
  3. 3Quick draw fuchsia gun and holster: The Ziehl-Neelsen method uses carbol fuchsin stain to detect acid-fast bacteria, such as Mycobacterium tuberculosis.
  4. 4"Lowenstein General Store": Mycobacterium tuberculosis is cultured on Löwenstein-Jensen agar, a selective egg-based medium that inhibits most contaminating bacteria and supports the slow growth of M. tuberculosis (typically requiring 2-6 weeks to form visible colonies).
  5. 5Aerobic bellows: Mycobacterium tuberculosis is an obligate aerobe, requiring high oxygen levels for survival and growth, and is typically spread from human to human via respiratory droplets.
  6. 6Jail cage: Mycobacterium tuberculosis evades host defenses by surviving and replicating within macrophages. If the immune system does not quickly clear the infection, the bacteria persist intracellularly, leading to granuloma formation and, ultimately, tuberculosis (TB). The infection pathway can then follow one of three possible outcomes: a contained latent infection, a severe disseminated infection (miliary TB), or reactivation of a previously latent infection later in life.
  7. 7Cord-like lasso + cage: Cord factor of Mycobacterium tuberculosis is a membrane glycolipid that inhibits macrophage function, prevents phagolysosomal fusion, induces an inflammatory response, and contributes to the bacteria's growth in serpentine, cord-like arrangements.
  8. 8Spurs + phagosome-lysosome dust clouds: Sulfatides in Mycobacterium tuberculosis are crucial for evading the host's immune system, particularly by inhibiting phagosome-lysosome fusion in macrophages and modulating immune responses.
  9. 9Bullet holes in middle of lung cactus: In primary tuberculosis (TB), the areas most often affected on a CXR (e.g., focal infiltrates or consolidation) are the middle and lower lung fields. Clinically, primary TB is often asymptomatic; when symptoms do occur, they are usually mild and include low-grade fever and cough.
  10. 10Swollen cacti flowers: Primary TB often presents with hilar lymphadenopathy, reflecting involvement of the mediastinal lymph nodes.
  11. 11Gun Complex: The Ghon complex, a distinctive radiographic sign of primary TB, consists of a calcified lung lesion (Ghon focus) along with ipsilateral hilar or mediastinal lymphadenopathy.
  12. 12Necrotic cage wagons: TB is known for forming caseous necrotic granulomas, which consist of a collection of immune cells, predominantly macrophages. These caseating granulomas feature a central region of necrotic cell death, producing a soft, "cheese-like" core, surrounded by live macrophages, T lymphocytes, and Langhans multinucleated giant cells (fused macrophages).
  13. 13Sleeping kid: Latent TB is characterized by dormancy of Mycobacterium tuberculosis within the body, where the bacteria are contained and suppressed by the immune system but not completely eradicated.
  14. 14Burlap sack + kid: When primary TB is not completely cleared by the immune system, it most often resolves through fibrosis and calcification, especially in children, encapsulating Mycobacterium tuberculosis within lung tissue; this dormant state represents latent TB.
  15. 15Shovel in dirt mound: The purified protein derivative (PPD) skin test for TB measures induration following an intradermal injection of tuberculin antigen. Per the CDC, a positive result depends on risk factors: 5 mm of induration for high-risk individuals (e.g., those with HIV or recent TB exposure), 10 mm for those from high-TB-prevalence areas or with certain medical conditions, and 15 mm for individuals with no known risk factors.
  16. 16"BCG" syringe in dirt mound: Prior Bacille Calmette-Guerin (BCG) vaccination for TB can cause false-positive reactions on the purified protein derivative skin test.
  17. 17"Millet" sign: Miliary TB, marked by widespread hematogenous dissemination, is a severe manifestation of tuberculosis that can involve multiple organs (liver, spleen, bone marrow); it presents with nonspecific systemic symptoms such as fever, weight loss, and fatigue, and classically appears on CXR as numerous tiny nodules (a "millet seed" pattern); it can be rapidly fatal if untreated.
  18. 18Millet seeds spreading everywhere: Miliary TB, marked by widespread hematogenous dissemination, is a severe manifestation of tuberculosis that can involve multiple organs (liver, spleen, bone marrow); it presents with nonspecific systemic symptoms such as fever, weight loss, and fatigue, and classically appears on CXR as numerous tiny nodules (a "millet seed" pattern); it can be rapidly fatal if untreated.
  19. 19Skull on millet seed: Miliary TB, marked by widespread hematogenous dissemination that can involve multiple organs (liver, spleen, bone marrow), presents with nonspecific systemic symptoms such as fever, weight loss, and fatigue, and classically appears on CXR as numerous tiny nodules (a "millet seed" pattern); it can be rapidly fatal if untreated.
  20. 20Cane: Reactivation of latent TB occurs in states of immunosuppression (e.g., HIV/AIDS, aging, cancer, or immunosuppressive therapy). Downregulation of TNF-alpha, which is critical for granuloma formation and maintenance, impairs granuloma integrity and allows the bacteria to reactivate.
  21. 21Tied to "TNF" barrels: Reactivation of latent TB is associated with the use of TNF-alpha inhibitors, since TNF-alpha is important for granuloma formation and maintenance.
  22. 22Bullet holes in top of cactus: On imaging, reactivation (secondary) TB usually involves the upper lobes and may show cavitary lesions, fibrosis, and patchy infiltrates.
  23. 23Coughing blood + sweating: Reactivation TB often presents with cough, hemoptysis (coughing up blood or blood-tinged mucus), night sweats, anorexia, and weight loss.
  24. 24Cracked vertebral pots: Pott disease, or spinal TB, is the most common extrapulmonary form of TB, resulting from hematogenous spread from a primary pulmonary focus; it causes vertebral body destruction and soft tissue swelling, potentially leading to vertebral collapse, spinal deformity, and neurological deficits.
  25. 25Bullet hole in hat: CNS tuberculosis most commonly causes tuberculous meningitis, with symptoms such as headache, fever, and neck stiffness, but it can also form tuberculomas, localized granulomatous masses that may produce focal neurologic deficits or seizures.
  26. 26"RIPE" taters: The combination of rifampin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol (RIPE) is the standard regimen for treating active TB.
  27. 27Rifle: Rifampin and isoniazid are used in cases of latent TB to prevent reactivation.
  28. 28Isolated behind cage wagon: Rifampin and isoniazid are used in cases of latent TB to prevent reactivation.

71. Mycoplasma pneumoniae

  1. 1Walking on ice: Mycoplasma pneumoniae is a gram-indeterminate pleomorphic bacterium.
  2. 2No wall: Mycoplasma pneumoniae lacks a cell wall and thus cannot be visualized using Gram stain.
  3. 3Sterol net pattern: Cholesterol, a four-ring steroid, is a key component of the cell membrane of Mycoplasma pneumoniae, providing structural stability and membrane flexibility in this cell wall-lacking bacterium.
  4. 4Patchy clouds: In Mycoplasma pneumoniae infections, chest X-rays typically reveal reticulonodular or patchy infiltrates that appear disproportionately severe compared with the mild clinical symptoms, a hallmark of "walking pneumonia."
  5. 5Walking: Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection is known for causing "walking pneumonia," characterized by relatively mild, slowly progressive pulmonary symptoms such as low-grade fever, nonproductive cough, and malaise.
  6. 6Camo jersey: Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection is common in settings with close person-to-person contact (e.g., military recruits, college dormitories, and prisons), due to respiratory droplet transmission.
  7. 7"<30" jersey: Mycoplasma pneumoniae frequently affects adults under 30, with a higher incidence in this age group.
  8. 8IgM snowflakes connecting RBC pucks: Infection with Mycoplasma pneumoniae can induce IgM cold agglutinins, which bind red blood cells at low temperatures and cause them to clump together (agglutination), leading to hemolysis and potentially autoimmune hemolytic anemia. Diagnosis can be supported by a cold agglutinin test, which detects this antibody-mediated RBC clumping in vitro.
  9. 9"Don't EAT ON ice": Eaton's agar is a specialized medium for cultivating Mycoplasma pneumoniae; it contains sterols, serum, and other growth factors.
  10. 10Macrolide crows: Macrolides, such as azithromycin, are used to treat Mycoplasma pneumoniae infections because the organism lacks a cell wall, making beta-lactam antibiotics ineffective.

72. Naegleria fowleri

  1. 1Niagara Falls: Naegleria fowleri is a CNS protozoan.
  2. 2Freshwater: Naegleria fowleri thrives in warm, stagnant freshwater environments such as lakes, rivers, hot springs, and poorly chlorinated swimming pools, particularly when water temperatures are above 30°C (86°F).
  3. 3Cribs: Naegleria fowleri enters the nasal passages and can invade the central nervous system by migrating along the olfactory nerves through the cribriform plate to reach the brain.
  4. 4Neck brace and red turban: Naegleria fowleri is the causative agent of primary amebic meningoencephalitis, which may present with symptoms of meningitis (headache, fever, stiff neck, nausea, vomiting) and encephalitis (confusion, altered sensorium, neurological deterioration, coma), and it often progresses rapidly to death.
  5. 5Dead barrel rider: Primary amebic meningoencephalitis typically progresses rapidly and has a high mortality rate.
  6. 6Windsurfer: Infections with Naegleria fowleri are often associated with aquatic activities in freshwater, such as swimming or diving.
  7. 7Contaminated water bottles: Naegleria fowleri infections have been linked to the use of contaminated tap water in nasal irrigation systems and, rarely, to exposure from improperly disinfected contact lens solutions.
  8. 8Champagne spinal needle cork: Diagnosis of Naegleria fowleri infection involves lumbar puncture to obtain cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), which is then analyzed for the presence of the amoeba.
  9. 9Amphibians: While amphotericin B has been used to treat Naegleria fowleri infections, its effectiveness is limited, and the majority of infections still result in death despite treatment.

73. Neisseria gonorrhoeae

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: sexually active patient with purulent urethral or cervical discharge, or migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis and pustular rash (disseminated infection); neonatal conjunctivitis within days of birth.
  • Micro: gram-negative diplococci inside neutrophils, oxidase positive, grows on Thayer-Martin, no capsule, IgA protease and pilus antigenic variation.
  • Diagnosis: NAAT (urine or swab); Gram stain and culture on Thayer-Martin.
  • Treatment/prevention: ceftriaxone plus empiric treatment for chlamydia coinfection (doxycycline); condoms; neonatal prophylaxis; test and treat partners.
  • Buzzwords: intracellular diplococci, PID, Fitz-Hugh-Curtis violin-string adhesions, disseminated gonococcal infection, ophthalmia neonatorum.
  1. 1Gonzo's Bar: N. gonorrhoeae are gram-negative diplococci and the cause of gonorrhea.
  2. 22 red cherries in mixed drink: N. gonorrhoeae diplococci are found within polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMNs) on Gram stain (they survive intracellularly).⭐ Gram-negative intracellular diplococci in PMNs.
  3. 3Broken cake capsule: N. gonorrhoeae has no capsule (unlike N. meningitidis).⭐ Lack of capsule explains why there is no vaccine and re-infection is possible.
  4. 4Embracing couple: Gonorrhea is a reportable sexually transmitted infection (STI).
  5. 5Care-free workers: N. gonorrhoeae can reside in asymptomatic carriers (especially women and pharyngeal or rectal infection), allowing silent spread.
  6. 6Blindfolded baby cherub: N. gonorrhoeae can be transmitted to the newborn in the birth canal, causing ophthalmia neonatorum within about 5 days of birth; the infection is typically purulent and severe. Chlamydia trachomatis also causes neonatal conjunctivitis at 5-14 days, usually milder and less purulent.⭐ Gonococcal conjunctivitis appears earlier (day 2-5) than chlamydial (day 5-14).
  7. 7Leaky tap: Gonorrhea can cause urethritis that is often asymptomatic but may show WBCs in urine (sterile pyuria).
  8. 8Flaming milk bomb: Symptomatic gonococcal urethritis causes dysuria and purulent urethral discharge.
  9. 9Apple seat cushion: Ascending gonococcal urethritis can involve the prostate, causing prostatitis.
  10. 10Orchid: Ascending infection can involve the epididymis and testis, causing epididymo-orchitis.
  11. 11Neck of chandelier leaking candle wax: Gonorrhea can cause mucopurulent cervicitis.
  12. 12Dripping red wax between calendar dates: Intermenstrual bleeding, such as postcoital spotting, is a warning symptom of gonococcal cervicitis.
  13. 13Arms of chandelier leaking candle wax: Ascending infection causes pelvic inflammatory disease (PID), including salpingitis and tubo-ovarian abscess, which can lead to ectopic pregnancy and infertility from tubal scarring.⭐ PID is a leading cause of ectopic pregnancy and infertility.
  14. 14Fitz, Hugh, & Curtis Trio: Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome, a complication of PID, is perihepatitis in which infection spreads to the liver capsule and peritoneum, causing right upper quadrant pain.
  15. 15Violin strings: Perihepatic inflammation leads to thin, string-like adhesions between the liver capsule and the abdominal wall ("violin string" adhesions).⭐ Violin-string adhesions = Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (also seen with chlamydia).
  16. 16Cracked piano leg: Disseminated gonococcal infection includes purulent arthritis (septic arthritis, often a single large joint).⭐ Most common cause of septic arthritis in sexually active young adults.
  17. 17Broken bass heel string: Disseminated gonococcal infection includes tenosynovitis, commonly of the tendons of the hands, wrists or ankles (e.g. Achilles).
  18. 18Red suits: Disseminated gonococcal infection includes dermatitis (pustular or vesiculopustular skin lesions).⭐ Triad: tenosynovitis, dermatitis, migratory polyarthralgia.
  19. 19Gnats: Nucleic acid amplification testing (NAAT) is the test of choice for diagnosing N. gonorrhoeae.
  20. 203 axes in Gonzo's bar: Ceftriaxone is the drug of choice for gonorrhea (third-generation cephalosporin).⭐ Add doxycycline for presumptive chlamydia coinfection.
  21. 21Clam lady + Cycle + Crow: Treat for presumptive Chlamydia trachomatis coinfection with doxycycline (or a macrolide such as azithromycin).
  22. 22Protective equipment: Counsel patients on barrier methods, such as consistent condom use, to prevent transmission.

74. Neisseria meningitidis

  1. 1The red room represents that Neisseria meningitidis is a gram-negative diplococcus of the Neisseria genus, is oxidase-positive, a strict human pathogen, can be cultivated on chocolate agar or Thayer-Martin medium, evades immunity through antigenic variation of pili, and is normally cleared by the membrane attack complex (MAC); deficiency in MAC components increases susceptibility to Neisseria infections.
  2. 2The "MAC" private eye represents that Neisseria meningitidis is a gram-negative diplococcus of the Neisseria genus, is oxidase-positive, a strict human pathogen, can be cultivated on chocolate agar or Thayer-Martin medium, evades immunity through antigenic variation of pili, and is normally cleared by the membrane attack complex (MAC); deficiency in MAC components increases susceptibility to Neisseria infections.
  3. 3The college dorm setting represents that outbreaks of meningococcal disease occur in close-contact settings, such as military barracks and college dormitories, due to respiratory droplet transmission; disease can manifest as meningitis (infection of the meninges) or meningococcemia (bacterial invasion of the bloodstream).
  4. 4The bottle of MALT liquor represents that Neisseria meningitidis ferments both glucose and maltose, in contrast to Neisseria gonorrhoeae, which ferments only glucose.
  5. 5The long cotton swab represents that the organism expresses pili that mediate adherence to the nasopharyngeal epithelium, enabling initial colonization, and transmission occurs via respiratory droplets.
  6. 6The ace card represents that Neisseria meningitidis produces IgA protease, which cleaves secretory IgA, thereby promoting mucosal adherence and immune evasion.
  7. 7The capsule represents that meningococcus is encapsulated, and this capsule provides protection against phagocytosis. The quadrivalent vaccine (covering serogroups A, C, Y, and W-135) consists of polysaccharide capsule antigens conjugated to diphtheria toxoid, which improves the T-cell dependent immune response.
  8. 8The syringes covered by capsules represent that meningococcus is encapsulated, and this capsule provides protection against phagocytosis. The quadrivalent vaccine (covering serogroups A, C, Y, and W-135) consists of polysaccharide capsule antigens conjugated to diphtheria toxoid, which improves the T-cell dependent immune response.
  9. 9The empty capsule with "B" marker represents that serogroup B meningococcus has a capsule that is poorly immunogenic because it mimics host neural adhesion molecules. Vaccines introduced in 2014 protect against serogroup B using recombinant outer membrane proteins instead of capsular polysaccharide, unlike older vaccines (before 2014).
  10. 10The flag with sickle represents that the meningococcal vaccine is particularly important for asplenic patients, such as those with sickle cell disease, because the spleen is crucial for clearing encapsulated organisms.
  11. 11The envelope represents that after colonization, Neisseria meningitidis can invade the bloodstream, causing meningococcemia. Lipooligosaccharide (LOS), an envelope (outer membrane) component similar to LPS, is anchored in the bacterial membrane and triggers proinflammatory cytokine release.
  12. 12The burning envelopes represent that shedding of LOS blebs amplifies inflammation, causing vascular damage, endothelial damage, capillary leakage, microthrombosis, and hemorrhagic necrosis.
  13. 13The leaky sprinklers represent that capillary leakage in meningococcal disease occurs when LOS damages endothelium, causing plasma to escape into tissues, with loss of intravascular volume leading to hypovolemia and hypotension and shock -> multi-organ failure due to poor tissue perfusion.
  14. 14The electrical sparks from outlet represent that capillary leakage in meningococcal disease occurs when LOS damages endothelium, causing plasma to escape into tissues, with loss of intravascular volume leading to hypovolemia and hypotension and shock -> multi-organ failure due to poor tissue perfusion.
  15. 15The dark spots on carpet and on boxers represent that endothelial injury and vasoconstriction -> thrombocytopenia and microthrombosis -> disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) -> the classic petechial rash.
  16. 16The WF water tower represents that severe meningococcemia causes endothelial injury, microthrombi, and hypotension -> adrenal ischemia with maximal vasoconstriction (in response to low blood pressure) stressing fragile adrenal capillaries -> bilateral adrenal hemorrhage (a.k.a. Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome) -> adrenal insufficiency -> low cortisol and aldosterone -> hypoglycemia, hyponatremia, and hyperkalemia -> further contributing to shock.
  17. 17The firefighter wielding axes represents that ceftriaxone is the drug of choice for treatment of meningococcal infections, with alternatives: penicillin G (if the strain is susceptible) or chloramphenicol in case of severe β-lactam allergy.
  18. 18The police with rifle blocking friends represent that post-exposure prophylaxis is required for close contacts who had >8 hours of exposure within the previous 7 days; rifampin is most commonly used (especially in children), ceftriaxone is preferred in pregnant contacts, and ciprofloxacin can be used as an alternative in adults.

75. Neisseria Overview

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: gonorrhea (urethritis, PID, septic arthritis) or meningococcal meningitis/sepsis with petechiae; recurrent infection suggests terminal complement (C5-C9) deficiency.
  • Micro: gram-negative diplococci, oxidase positive, grow on Thayer-Martin (chocolate agar with VPN); gonococcus ferments glucose only, meningococcus glucose and maltose.
  • Diagnosis: Gram stain, culture on Thayer-Martin; NAAT for gonorrhea.
  • Treatment/prevention: ceftriaxone; meningococcal vaccination and chemoprophylaxis for contacts; condoms for gonorrhea.
  • Buzzwords: diplococci, chocolate agar, IgA protease, pilus antigenic variation, LOS, MAC deficiency.
  1. 1Red curtains: Neisseria are gram-negative bacteria; the genus includes N. gonorrhoeae and N. meningitidis.
  2. 2Handcuffs: Neisseria are gram-negative diplococci (paired, kidney-bean shaped).
  3. 3Movie candy: N. gonorrhoeae ferments glucose only, whereas N. meningitidis ferments both glucose and maltose.⭐ Meningococcus = Maltose and glucose (MeninGOCOCCUS).
  4. 4Blue onyx pendant: Neisseria are oxidase positive.
  5. 5Chocolate bar: Neisseria do not grow well on plain sheep blood agar but grow on chocolate agar (blood agar heated to lyse RBCs, releasing factors V [NAD+] and X [hemin]).⭐ Factors V and X are also needed by Haemophilus.
  6. 6Vice city Private iNvestigator: VPN is the selective supplement added to chocolate agar: Vancomycin (kills gram-positives), Polymyxin (kills most gram-negatives except Neisseria), Nystatin (kills fungi) and Trimethoprim (kills Proteus).
  7. 7Starring Thayer Martin: Thayer-Martin medium = chocolate agar plus VPN antibiotics; this enriched, selective medium is used clinically to isolate Neisseria species.⭐ Thayer-Martin is the classic selective medium for Neisseria.
  8. 8MAC, P.IL.: The membrane attack complex (MAC), formed at the terminal stage of the complement pathway, is an essential host defense against Neisseria infections.
  9. 95-9 PM: Deficiency of terminal complement components C5-C9 increases susceptibility to recurrent Neisseria infections.⭐ Recurrent meningococcemia or disseminated gonococcal infection: check CH50.
  10. 10Variable pili earrings: Pili mediate attachment to mucosal surfaces and undergo frequent antigenic variation to evade the immune system.⭐ Antigenic variation of pili hinders vaccine development for gonorrhea.
  11. 11Torn Ace card: Neisseria produce IgA protease, which cleaves IgA at the hinge region and allows easier mucosal colonization.⭐ Also made by H. influenzae and S. pneumoniae.
  12. 12The LOSt Flame: Lipooligosaccharide (LOS) is a proinflammatory endotoxin-like molecule of Neisseria, similar to endotoxin of other gram-negative bacteria.⭐ LOS lacks the O antigen of LPS.
  13. 13Opaque window: Opa (opacity) proteins on the Neisseria outer surface promote tight binding to each other and to host cells, aiding colonization.

76. Nocardia asteroides

  1. 1No limit CARD game: Nocardia asteroides is a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple hues: Nocardia asteroides is a gram-positive bacillus.
  3. 3Branching tree: Nocardia asteroides is characterized by its unique filamentous, branching bacilli structure.
  4. 4Aerobic bellows: Nocardia asteroides is an obligate aerobic organism, requiring oxygen for its survival and growth.
  5. 5Soil: Nocardia asteroides is predominantly found in soil, where it survives as a saprophyte; exposure occurs through inhalation or traumatic skin inoculation, leading to infection.
  6. 6Mycolic acid tassels: Nocardia asteroides has mycolic acids (long-chain fatty acids) in its cell wall, which allow it to stain partially pink/red with carbol fuchsin on acid-fast staining; this partial staining is weaker than the full acid-fast reaction seen in Mycobacterium tuberculosis, reflecting Nocardia's lower mycolic acid content.
  7. 7Pink acid fast gun: Nocardia asteroides has mycolic acids (long-chain fatty acids) in its cell wall, which allow it to stain partially pink/red with carbol fuchsin on acid-fast staining; this partial staining is weaker than the full acid-fast reaction seen in Mycobacterium tuberculosis, reflecting Nocardia's lower mycolic acid content.
  8. 8Catalase cat: Nocardia asteroides produces catalase (i.e., is catalase-positive), an enzyme that converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen.
  9. 9Ammonia spray: Nocardia asteroides produces urease (i.e., is urease-positive), an enzyme that hydrolyzes urea into carbon dioxide and ammonia.
  10. 10Immunocompromised cane + man: Nocardiosis predominantly affects immunocompromised patients and is more common in men than women (about 2:1 to 3:1).
  11. 11Dented badge over lungs: Pulmonary nocardiosis may manifest with multiple solid nodules and cavities, resembling tuberculosis.
  12. 12Coughing: Patients with pulmonary nocardiosis may experience symptoms similar to pneumonia (e.g., fever, cough, dyspnea, chest pain).
  13. 13Tuberculosis cactus: Pulmonary nocardiosis may manifest with multiple solid nodules and cavities, resembling tuberculosis.
  14. 14Hole in hat: CNS nocardiosis is a rare infection that may present with brain abscesses and multiple ring-enhancing lesions.
  15. 15Red cow spots: Cutaneous nocardiosis is a rare manifestation of infection that can result from direct inoculation or secondary spread from disseminated pulmonary infection, presenting variably as red, indurated, tender nodules, pustules, or ulcerative lesions with associated inflammation.
  16. 16Sulfa eggs: Sulfonamides, specifically trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX), are the treatment of choice for Nocardia asteroides infections, inhibiting bacterial folate synthesis, which is essential for DNA and protein production; because Nocardia's mycolic acids differ from those of Mycobacterium tuberculosis, isoniazid is ineffective, making TMP-SMX the preferred agent.

77. Norovirus & Norwalk Virus (Caliciviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: outbreak of acute vomiting and watery diarrhea on a cruise ship, in a school, or after shellfish or buffet food
  • Positive-sense, nonenveloped ssRNA virus (Caliciviridae); the leading cause of viral gastroenteritis in adults and of foodborne outbreaks in the U.S.
  • Incubation 12-48 hours, self-limited in 1-3 days; diagnosis is clinical (RT-PCR on stool if needed)
  • Treatment is supportive (oral rehydration); no specific antiviral and no routine vaccine
  • Prevention: hand washing with soap and water (alcohol sanitizers are less effective), surface bleach cleaning; highly contagious with a very low infectious dose
  1. 1Narwhal: Noroviruses (including the Norwalk virus) are positive-sense RNA viruses in the Caliciviridae family.
  2. 2"Cali-sea-cruise": Noroviruses belong to the Caliciviridae family; the 'Cali-sea-cruise' pun ties the family name to cruise ship outbreaks.
  3. 3Warm colors: Noroviruses are RNA viruses (the warm colors group RNA viruses). Most RNA viruses replicate in the cytoplasm.
  4. 4Positive sun: Noroviruses are positive-sense, single-stranded RNA viruses: the genome can act directly as mRNA and is translated by host ribosomes immediately after entry. Naked positive-sense RNA alone is infectious.
  5. 5Statue of David: Noroviruses lack an envelope (naked viruses). This makes them resistant to ethanol, detergents, and drying, so they persist on surfaces and spread easily.
  6. 6Roll of tickets: After entry the whole genome is translated into one long polyprotein, which viral proteases cleave into functional proteins (typical of positive-sense RNA viruses). Protease inhibitors are therefore a potential target.
  7. 7Cruise ship: Norovirus outbreaks occur in confined settings such as cruise ships. It is highly contagious, with a very low infectious dose, and spreads fecal-oral, via vomit aerosols, and on contaminated surfaces.
  8. 8Children: Outbreaks also occur in day care centers and schools, where close contact and poor hand hygiene allow rapid spread.
  9. 9Shellfish buffet: Outbreaks also follow consumption of contaminated shellfish (filter feeders, e.g., raw oysters) or food handled by many people at buffets and cafeterias. Infected food handlers are a classic source.
  10. 10Mud spray: Norovirus causes acute, severe, 'explosive' watery diarrhea with nausea, vomiting, cramps, low-grade fever, and malaise. Symptoms begin 12-48 hours after exposure and resolve in 1-3 days. Treatment is supportive with rehydration.⭐ Think norovirus: sudden vomiting + watery diarrhea outbreak on a cruise ship; no vaccine, alcohol gel is poor.
