78. Paracoccidioides brasiliensis

  1. 1Brazilian pirate: Paracoccidioides brasiliensis is a dimorphic fungus.
  2. 2Central and South America map: Paracoccidioides brasiliensis is endemic to Central and South America.
  3. 3Captain's wheel: The yeast form of Paracoccidioides brasiliensis, characterized by multipolar budding, has multiple buds radiating from a central cell, resembling a captain's wheel.
  4. 4Small red center of captain's wheel: The multipolar budding yeast form of Paracoccidioides brasiliensis is typically much larger than an RBC.
  5. 5Butterfly: Paracoccidioides brasiliensis exhibits dimorphism, growing as mold in cooler environments (25–30°C) and as yeast at human body temperature (35–37°C).
  6. 6Water droplets: Paracoccidioides brasiliensis is primarily transmitted via inhalation of mold spores.
  7. 7Coughing: Important clinical symptoms of paracoccidioidomycosis include cough and lymphadenopathy that can affect cervical, axillary, and even inguinal chains of lymph nodes.
  8. 8Beads by neck: Important clinical symptoms of paracoccidioidomycosis include cough and lymphadenopathy that can affect cervical, axillary, and even inguinal chains of lymph nodes.
  9. 9Medallions over chest: Paracoccidioidomycosis can lead to granuloma formation in the lungs and other organs, particularly in disseminated disease, because the immune system attempts to contain the yeast; macrophages and T cells form structured granulomas to limit fungal spread.
  10. 10Unhealthy mouth: Chronic paracoccidioidomycosis often presents with granulomatous oral and upper respiratory mucosal ulcers, characterized by ragged borders, small spots of hemorrhage, a granular appearance, and potential tissue destruction.
  11. 11Pinecones: Azoles, particularly itraconazole, are the treatment of choice for mild paracoccidioidomycosis.
  12. 12Amphibians: Amphotericin B is the treatment of choice for severe cases of paracoccidioidomycosis.

79. Parainfluenza virus (Measles/Mumps/Parainfluenza)

  1. 1PARAnormal MIXer: the Paramyxoviridae family of viruses includes the measles virus, mumps virus, and parainfluenza virus.
  2. 2Warm colors: Paramyxoviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses.
  3. 3Moon with negative-sign-shaped clouds: Paramyxoviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 4Ghosts with envelopes: Paramyxoviruses are enveloped and have a helical capsid.
  5. 5Water droplets: Paramyxoviruses are transmitted via respiratory droplets.
  6. 6Live puppet show: the MMR vaccine is a live attenuated vaccine given to prevent measles, mumps, and rubella.
  7. 7Pregnant puppet: avoid administering the MMR vaccine to pregnant people or those with certain immunocompromising conditions.
  8. 8Weasel in a ruby dress: measles is caused by the rubeola virus (sometimes itself called "rubeola").
  9. 94 "C" buttons: the 4 C's of measles (rubeola) are Cough, Coryza, Conjunctivitis, and Koplik spots.
  10. 10Coughing weasel: measles can present with a nonproductive, dry, harsh, paroxysmal cough.
  11. 11Runny nose: measles can present with coryza (inflammation of the nasal mucosa), with nasal congestion, watery or clear rhinorrhea (runny nose), and sneezing.
  12. 12Red eyes: measles can present with conjunctivitis — inflammation of the conjunctiva causing red, watery eyes and light sensitivity, typically without discharge.
  13. 13Blue candies in a bowl: Koplik spots are small, bluish-white or gray-white spots with a red halo on the buccal mucosa (inside the cheeks, opposite the molars), appearing 1–2 days before the characteristic rash — pathognomonic for measles.
  14. 14Sweating weasel: measles can present with a high, abrupt-onset fever exceeding 40°C (104°F), lasting 3–5 days during the prodromal phase, with chills, malaise, and fatigue.
  15. 15Ruby crown with falling rubies: the maculopapular rash of measles typically begins on the face (especially the hairline and behind the ears) and spreads downward to the neck, trunk, and extremities over 2–3 days; it is red, blotchy, slightly raised, may coalesce into larger areas of redness, and often appears as prodromal symptoms fade.
  16. 16Solid red dress: same measles maculopapular rash pattern — begins on the face and spreads downward over 2–3 days, red and blotchy, may coalesce, appearing as the prodrome resolves.
  17. 17Lung-shaped bow tie: pneumonia is an early sequela of measles and the most common cause of death associated with measles infection.
  18. 18"Tales of SSPEnce" sign: subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE) is a rare, serious late complication of measles, developing 7–10 years after infection.
  19. 19Grey and white turban: SSPE is characterized by progressive white matter demyelination, with behavioral and cognitive decline, myoclonic jerks, seizures, and eventual coma; diagnosis shows elevated CSF measles antibodies and white matter demyelination or cortical atrophy on imaging.
  20. 20Tentacles and berries near the weasel: the measles virus has the glycoprotein hemagglutinin (HA), which binds the virus to erythrocytes.
  21. 21Sticky substance on the weasel's hands: the measles virus has a fusion (F) protein that fuses infected lymphocytes into syncytia, forming multinucleated Warthin–Finkeldey giant cells — commonly found in lymphoid tissue (tonsils, lymph nodes), often with eosinophilic intranuclear/cytoplasmic inclusions, and pathognomonic for measles.
  22. 22"A" party hat on the weasel: vitamin A reduces the risk of measles complications, including death, by supporting epithelial integrity and immune function.
  23. 23Mummy guest: the mumps virus, a Paramyxoviridae member, causes parotid gland swelling, fever, and malaise, with possible complications of orchitis, meningitis, pancreatitis, and hearing loss.
  24. 24Swollen cheeks: the mumps virus replicates in the parotid salivary glands, causing parotitis — tender, swollen glands producing a puffy-cheek appearance, pain on chewing/swallowing, fever, malaise, and occasional headache or myalgia.
  25. 25Orchid: mumps can progress to orchitis — painful, usually unilateral testicular inflammation that may lead to testicular atrophy and, rarely, infertility.
  26. 26Neck brace: mumps can progress to viral meningitis when the virus spreads from the salivary glands or respiratory tract to the CNS, causing headache, fever, neck stiffness, and photophobia.
  27. 27Mummy child headed to the puppet show: the MMR vaccine includes a live attenuated mumps virus.
  28. 28Tentacles and berries on the mummy: the mumps virus has hemagglutinin (HA) for host cell attachment.
  29. 29Sticky substance on the mummy's hands: the mumps virus has a fusion (F) protein that fuses epithelial/glandular cells into syncytia (multinucleated giant cells), mainly in the salivary glands, pancreas, testes, and CNS — unlike measles, where giant cells form mainly in lymphoid tissue.
  30. 30Scalpel on a tray held by the mummy: the mumps virus has neuraminidase (NA), which cleaves sialic acid residues to release the virus from host cells.
  31. 31Baying seal howling at the moon: parainfluenza virus causes laryngotracheobronchitis (croup), with a distinctive "barking" cough (like a seal's call), stridor, hoarseness, and respiratory distress, often worse at night.
  32. 323 wolves: parainfluenza virus has 3 key virulence factors — hemagglutinin (HA) for host cell attachment, neuraminidase (NA) for virion release, and fusion (F) protein for viral entry and cell-to-cell fusion.
  33. 33Steeple (church spire): chest X-ray in croup may show the "steeple sign" from subglottic tracheal narrowing.
  34. 34Wind blowing the door in: croup causes inspiratory stridor — a high-pitched sound on inspiration from airflow obstruction through the inflamed upper airway.

80. Parvovirus B19 (Parvoviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: child with 'slapped cheek' rash then lacy rash (fifth disease); adult with symmetric small-joint arthritis; aplastic crisis in sickle cell; hydrops fetalis in pregnancy.
  • Microbiology: naked (non-enveloped) icosahedral virus with a small linear single-stranded DNA genome; targets erythroid precursors via the P antigen (globoside); spread by respiratory droplets.
  • Diagnosis: clinical for fifth disease; anti-B19 IgM and/or PCR; bone marrow shows giant pronormoblasts in aplastic crisis.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive; transfusion for aplastic crisis; IVIG in immunocompromised chronic infection; intrauterine transfusion for fetal hydrops.
  • Buzzwords: 'slapped cheek', 'fifth disease', 'aplastic crisis', 'hydrops fetalis', 'P antigen', 'ssDNA', 'smallest DNA virus'.
  1. 1B19 bomber: Parvovirus B19, a DNA virus in the Parvoviridae family.
  2. 2Cool colors: Parvovirus B19 is a DNA virus.
  3. 3Statue of David: Parvovirus B19 lacks an envelope (naked virus), so it is resistant to many disinfectants and drying.
  4. 4Tiny town: Parvovirus B19 is among the smallest known DNA viruses.
  5. 5Single runway strip: Parvovirus B19 has a single-stranded DNA genome (one of the few ssDNA viruses).⭐ Few DNA viruses are ssDNA; others are dsDNA.
  6. 6Water gun spray: Parvovirus B19 is transmitted mainly by respiratory droplets (also vertically and by blood products).
  7. 7Torch: Parvovirus B19 belongs to the TORCHeS infections that cross the placenta: Toxoplasma, Other (varicella-zoster, parvovirus B19, Zika, HIV), Rubella, CMV, HSV, Syphilis.
  8. 8Five finger slap on cheek: Parvovirus B19 causes erythema infectiosum, known as 'fifth disease' or 'slapped cheek syndrome'.⭐ Fifth of the classic childhood exanthems.
  9. 9Inferiorly spreading flame on nose of plane: Erythema infectiosum begins with mild low-grade fever, malaise, and coryza, then the facial slapped-cheek rash spreads inferiorly to a lacy, reticular rash on trunk and extremities. It is most contagious during the prodrome, before the rash appears.⭐ Once the rash appears the child is no longer contagious.
  10. 10Pink colors on shirt: The rash of erythema infectiosum is pink-red: slapped cheeks followed by a lacy, reticular rash on trunk and limbs.
  11. 11Kneeling on legos + pain lines: In adults, parvovirus B19 typically causes symmetric arthralgias or arthritis of the small joints (hands, wrists, knees) and sometimes generalized edema, often after exposure to children.⭐ Can mimic rheumatoid arthritis.
  12. 12Adult surrounded by children: Adults often have recent exposure to children at school or daycare, where the virus circulates; parents and teachers are at risk.
  13. 13Sickle + plastic bone: Parvovirus B19 can cause transient aplastic crisis in sickle cell disease or other chronic hemolytic anemias (e.g., hereditary spherocytosis, beta-thalassemia) by infecting erythroid precursors via the P antigen, abruptly decreasing RBC production. Smear shows few or no reticulocytes; marrow shows giant pronormoblasts with viral inclusions.⭐ Low reticulocytes in a patient with hemolytic anemia = think B19.
  14. 14Balloon bear sprayed with water: Fetal parvovirus B19 infection can cause hydrops fetalis (fluid in two or more fetal compartments) from destruction of erythroid precursors causing severe anemia, high-output heart failure, and hepatic dysfunction with low plasma proteins, lowering oncotic pressure and causing edema and possible fetal demise.⭐ Highest fetal risk in the first half of pregnancy.

81. Pasteurella multocida (Pasteurellaceae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: cellulitis within 24 hours of a dog or cat bite or scratch (rapid onset, often within hours).
  • Gram-negative coccobacillus; catalase +, oxidase +, polysaccharide capsule; bipolar (safety-pin) staining.
  • Grows on blood agar (5% sheep blood); zoonosis from respiratory flora of cats and dogs.
  • Complications: abscess, septic arthritis, osteomyelitis, necrotizing fasciitis.
  • Treatment: penicillin or amoxicillin-clavulanate; buzzwords: cat bite, rapid cellulitis, safety pin.
  1. 1Louis Pasteur: Pasteurella multocida is a gram-negative coccobacillus.
  2. 2Red hues: Pasteurella multocida stains pink-red on Gram stain because it is gram-negative.
  3. 3Oval shaped-flask: The organism is a short, oval coccobacillus.
  4. 4Labrador: Pasteurella multocida is a zoonotic pathogen found in the normal oral/respiratory flora of many animals; it is transmitted mainly by animal bites and scratches (e.g., dogs and cats).⭐ Cat bites are more likely than dog bites to become infected with Pasteurella.
  5. 5Inflamed red bite: Infection causes rapidly progressive cellulitis, usually within 24 hours of a bite or scratch, and can evolve into necrotizing fasciitis, a rapidly spreading, life-threatening infection of the fascia and subcutaneous tissue.⭐ Cellulitis within 24 hours of an animal bite = Pasteurella until proven otherwise.
  6. 6Fish bones: Deeper wound infections can cause more severe complications such as osteomyelitis (and septic arthritis).
  7. 7Catalase cat: Pasteurella multocida is catalase positive; catalase converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen.
  8. 8Oxidase blue ring: Pasteurella multocida is oxidase positive; oxidase is an enzyme of the aerobic electron transport chain.
  9. 9Glass capsule: The bacteria are surrounded by a polysaccharide capsule, a key virulence factor.
  10. 10Sheep's blood: Pasteurella multocida grows readily on 5% sheep blood agar.⭐ It does not grow on MacConkey agar, unlike most enteric gram-negative rods.
  11. 11Safety pin: It shows bipolar (safety-pin) staining: both ends of the rod stain more intensely than the center.⭐ Safety-pin appearance is the classic microscopy clue.
  12. 12Purple pencil: Pasteurella multocida infections are treated with penicillin, or with a beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combination (e.g., amoxicillin-clavulanate), which also covers mixed bite-wound flora.⭐ Amoxicillin-clavulanate is the usual empiric choice for animal bites.

82. Picornaviridae Overview

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: fecal-oral viral syndromes: hepatitis A, polio (paralysis), coxsackie (hand-foot-mouth, herpangina, myocarditis), echovirus/enterovirus aseptic meningitis; rhinovirus causes the common cold.
  • Microbiology: small (20-30 nm), naked icosahedral, positive-sense ssRNA viruses that replicate in the cytoplasm and make one polyprotein cleaved by proteases.
  • Diagnosis: clinical or serology (HAV IgM); PCR for enteroviruses; CSF shows lymphocytic pleocytosis, normal glucose, mildly elevated protein, negative bacterial culture.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; vaccines exist for HAV and poliovirus (IPV/OPV); no vaccine for coxsackie, echo, or rhinoviruses.
  • Buzzwords: 'pico = small', 'naked +ssRNA', 'polyprotein', 'enteroviruses', 'aseptic meningitis', 'rhinovirus = respiratory, acid-labile'.
  1. 1Mountain peak: The Picornaviridae family includes hepatitis A virus, poliovirus, coxsackievirus A and B, echovirus, and rhinovirus.⭐ Mnemonic: PERCH (Polio, Echo, Rhino, Coxsackie, HAV).
  2. 2'Peak Animal Nursery' + baby animals: Picornaviruses are very small, with a 20-30 nm icosahedral capsid; 'pico' means small.
  3. 3Warm colors: Picornaviruses are RNA viruses (warm colors = RNA), positive-sense and single-stranded.
  4. 4Positive sun: Picornaviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  5. 5Statue of David: Picornaviruses lack an envelope; they consist of a protein capsid around the RNA genome (naked viruses), so they resist ether and many disinfectants.
  6. 6Poop: Hepatitis A, poliovirus, coxsackievirus A and B, and echovirus spread by the fecal-oral route; rhinovirus spreads by respiratory droplets and contact.
  7. 7Coin stamp machine: The genomic RNA of positive-sense viruses resembles mRNA and can be translated directly by host ribosomes.⭐ Naked +ssRNA genome is infectious by itself.
  8. 8Roll of tickets: The picornavirus genome is translated into one long polyprotein, which viral proteases cleave into functional proteins.⭐ Protease inhibitors are a therapeutic concept for this group.
  9. 9Admissions booth in the open area: Positive-sense RNA viruses replicate in the cytoplasm; genomic RNA is immediately translated by host ribosomes, with no need to enter the nucleus.
  10. 10'A'-tagged hippos: Hepatitis A virus (a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family; a hepatovirus).⭐ HAV is a picornavirus, unlike hepatitis B (DNA), C (flavivirus), and E (hepevirus).
  11. 11'ENTER' aviary: The Enterovirus genus of Picornaviridae includes poliovirus, coxsackievirus A and B, and echovirus.
  12. 12Flamingos: Poliovirus (a positive-sense RNA virus in Picornaviridae); the flamingo's single leg is a cue for paralysis.⭐ Causes asymmetric flaccid paralysis; prevention by IPV/OPV.
  13. 13Cockatoos: Coxsackievirus A and B (positive-sense RNA viruses in Picornaviridae).⭐ Coxsackie A: hand-foot-mouth, herpangina; B: myocarditis, pericarditis.
  14. 14Mockingbirds echoing each other: Echovirus (a positive-sense RNA virus in Picornaviridae); the echoing birds cue 'echo'.
  15. 15Rhinos: Rhinovirus (a positive-sense RNA virus in Picornaviridae); rhinos cue the nose.
  16. 16Enlarged liver spot: Hepatitis A causes liver inflammation (hepatitis) with moderate hepatomegaly and, less often, splenomegaly.⭐ Acute self-limited; no chronic carrier state.
  17. 17Head-shaped aviary: Aseptic meningitis, usually caused by enteroviruses, is a self-limited viral inflammation of the meninges. CSF bacterial cultures and Gram stain are negative (no organisms seen), hence 'aseptic'.
  18. 18'100% aseptic zone' sign: Aseptic meningitis, usually caused by enteroviruses, is a self-limited viral inflammation of the meninges. CSF bacterial cultures and Gram stain are negative (no organisms seen), hence 'aseptic'.
  19. 19'No organisms' feed: Aseptic meningitis, usually caused by enteroviruses, is a self-limited viral inflammation of the meninges. CSF bacterial cultures and Gram stain are negative (no organisms seen), hence 'aseptic'.
  20. 20'No sugar added' feed: Aseptic meningitis typically has normal CSF glucose (about 2/3 of serum glucose) because the pathogen does not consume glucose and the inflammation is lymphocytic rather than neutrophilic.⭐ Normal glucose with lymphocytes distinguishes viral from bacterial meningitis.
  21. 21'Source of protein' feed: Aseptic meningitis often has mildly elevated CSF protein because inflammation increases meningeal permeability; the rise is milder than in bacterial meningitis, where protein is markedly elevated.
  22. 22Child in helmet: Aseptic meningitis most often affects children and young adults.⭐ Enteroviruses are the most common cause.
  23. 23Mud on nose and mouth: Rhinovirus, the main cause of the common cold, infects the upper respiratory tract and spreads by respiratory droplets and hand contact, unlike other picornaviruses that spread fecal-orally.⭐ Rhinovirus is acid-labile, so it does not survive the stomach, and grows best at about 33 C.

83. Plasmodium: Infection and Life Cycle

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: traveler with cyclic high fever, chills, anemia, jaundice, and splenomegaly; severe disease (cerebral malaria, ARDS, renal failure) is usually P. falciparum.
  • Microbiology: P. falciparum (irregular fever, banana gametocytes), P. vivax/ovale (tertian, hypnozoites, Schuffner dots), P. malariae (quartan), P. knowlesi (quotidian, macaques).
  • Diagnosis: Giemsa-stained thick and thin blood smears (gold standard); repeat smears if negative and suspicion is high.
  • Treatment/prevention: artemisinin-based or resistant-malaria regimens; primaquine/tafenoquine for hypnozoites; bed nets and chemoprophylaxis.
  • Buzzwords: 'Anopheles', 'ring forms', 'banana-shaped gametocytes', 'hypnozoites', 'tertian 48 h', 'quartan 72 h', 'sickle trait protects'.
  1. 1Podium: Plasmodium species are blood protozoa that cause malaria and are transmitted by the bite of the female Anopheles mosquito.
  2. 2Malodorous air: 'Mal-aria' (bad air): Plasmodium species are blood protozoa that cause malaria and are transmitted by the bite of the female Anopheles mosquito.
  3. 3'Annapolis high school': 'Anopheles': Plasmodium species are blood protozoa that cause malaria and are transmitted by the bite of the female Anopheles mosquito.
  4. 4Mosquito mascot: Plasmodium species are blood protozoa that cause malaria and are transmitted by the bite of the female Anopheles mosquito. Only the female mosquito feeds on blood.
  5. 5Flame on head + sweat drops: Malaria presents with high-grade fever, chills, and flu-like symptoms (headache, myalgias), often cyclic with rigors.
  6. 6Pale complexion + white suit: Most malaria infections cause anemia, partly through intravascular hemolysis of infected RBCs.
  7. 7Yellow glasses: Hemolysis releases heme that is converted to unconjugated bilirubin; when production exceeds hepatic conjugation capacity it causes jaundice, seen as scleral icterus.
  8. 8Liver and spleen flasks: Malaria may cause hepatosplenomegaly: the spleen sequesters and destroys infected RBCs, and the liver processes hemoglobin and mounts an immune response, enlarging both organs.⭐ Splenic rupture is a rare complication.
  9. 9Broken plates: Mild to moderate thrombocytopenia is common in malaria from splenic sequestration, immune-mediated destruction, and consumption during inflammation.
  10. 10'World champs' distribution: Malaria is endemic to Central and South America, Southeast Asia, and sub-Saharan Africa.
  11. 11False mask dancer with Africa shadow: Plasmodium falciparum, a blood protozoan endemic to sub-Saharan Africa, Southeast Asia, and parts of South America.
  12. 12Irregular button pattern + false mask: P. falciparum tends to produce an irregular fever pattern (no clear periodicity).
  13. 13Severe dancing + false mask: Most cases of severe malaria are caused by P. falciparum.⭐ P. falciparum infects RBCs of all ages, giving the highest parasitemia.
  14. 14Kidney, liver, and lung sweat spots: RBCs infected with P. falciparum adhere to and obstruct renal, hepatic, and pulmonary microvessels, potentially causing renal failure (blackwater fever/acute tubular necrosis), liver dysfunction, and ARDS.
  15. 15Brain tophat + false mask: P. falciparum-infected RBCs adhere to and occlude cerebral capillaries and postcapillary venules, causing cerebral malaria with impaired consciousness, delirium, and seizures.⭐ Cytoadherence/sequestration is mediated by PfEMP1 knobs.
  16. 16Hiding behind sickle knife + alpha/beta chalice: Sickle cell trait and alpha- and beta-thalassemia confer relative protection against P. falciparum.⭐ Also HbC and Duffy-negative status (protects against P. vivax).
  17. 17Watermelon with bite + seeds: G6PD deficiency (bite cells with Heinz bodies) may offer protection against P. falciparum.
  18. 18Axe flatware: Plasmodium vivax, a blood protozoan endemic to Southeast Asia, the Americas, and Africa; it can cause severe disease.⭐ P. vivax prefers reticulocytes and needs the Duffy antigen to enter RBCs.
  19. 19Oval placemat: Plasmodium ovale, a blood protozoan endemic to tropical West Africa.
  20. 20Sleeping near axe + oval on liver table: P. vivax and P. ovale form hypnozoites (dormant liver stage) that persist in hepatocytes and cause relapse weeks to months later.⭐ Treat with primaquine or tafenoquine after checking G6PD.
  21. 21Tertian button pattern: P. vivax and P. ovale cause periodic fever spikes every 48 hours (tertian fever).
  22. 22Malodorous cologne: Plasmodium malariae, a blood protozoan endemic to sub-Saharan Africa, Southeast Asia, western Pacific islands, and parts of South America.
  23. 23Quartan button pattern + malodorous cologne: P. malariae causes periodic fever spikes every 72 hours (quartan fever).⭐ P. malariae can cause nephrotic syndrome (immune-complex).
  24. 24'Know Your Moves': Plasmodium knowlesi, a blood protozoan endemic to Southeast Asia.
  25. 25Monkey cutout + 'Know Your Moves': Macaques are the reservoir hosts of P. knowlesi (a zoonotic malaria).
  26. 26Quotidian button pattern + 'Know Your Moves': P. knowlesi causes periodic fever spikes every 24 hours (quotidian fever).⭐ It can cause severe disease because of rapid replication.
  27. 27Mosquito with sporozoite football: The life cycle begins when a female Anopheles takes a blood meal and injects sporozoites into the bloodstream, starting the exoerythrocytic (liver) cycle.
  28. 28Sporozoite football cup in liver case: Sporozoites travel to the liver and infect hepatocytes.
  29. 29Multiple basketballs in schizont cup: In hepatocytes sporozoites divide asexually into multinucleated schizonts, each containing about 8-24 merozoites (up to thousands in the liver stage depending on species).
  30. 30Merozoite basketballs rupturing from schizont cup: Liver schizonts rupture and release merozoites into the bloodstream.
  31. 31Hockey cup in liver case: Sporozoites of P. vivax and P. ovale can become hypnozoites, dormant in hepatocytes for weeks to months; when they reactivate they divide by schizogony into schizonts, causing relapse.
  32. 32Merozoite basketball in RBC case: Merozoites in the bloodstream infect RBCs, beginning the erythrocytic cycle.
  33. 33Class ring in RBC case: Merozoites develop into ring forms, the early stage of intraerythrocytic development (signet-ring appearance).
  34. 34Circular trophy in RBC case: Ring forms progress to mature trophozoites within RBCs.
  35. 35Schizont cup with merozoite basketballs in RBC case: Mature trophozoites develop into schizonts containing merozoites.
  36. 36Broken cup + RBC case: Schizont rupture lyses the RBC and releases merozoites that infect new RBCs; the synchronous rupture causes the fever paroxysms.
  37. 37Male + female bathrooms: A small number of merozoites differentiate into male and female gametocytes, which are taken up by a mosquito to complete the sexual cycle.
  38. 38Blood smear + gem disco ball: Microscopic examination of Giemsa-stained blood smears is the gold standard for diagnosing malaria; intracellular trophozoites, schizonts, or gametocytes confirm infection.⭐ Thick smear for sensitivity, thin smear for species and parasitemia.
  39. 39Bananas + red drink near false mask: P. falciparum gametocytes are crescent- or banana-shaped on peripheral smear, longer than the diameter of an RBC, distinguishing it from other species whose gametocytes are round.⭐ Banana gametocytes = P. falciparum.
  40. 40Stippled Schiffner basketball under axe + oval: P. vivax and P. ovale cause changes in infected RBCs seen as Schuffner dots (fine reddish stippling of the cytoplasm).⭐ Infected RBCs in vivax/ovale are enlarged.

84. Plasmodium: Treatment and Prophylaxis

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: traveler to endemic area with fever; severe malaria (parasitemia >5%, altered mentation, seizures, multisystem failure) needs IV artesunate.
  • Characteristics: chloroquine inhibits heme polymerization in the food vacuole; resistance (via transporter mutations) is widespread, especially P. falciparum.
  • Resistant malaria: artemisinin-based therapy, atovaquone-proguanil, quinine plus doxycycline, or mefloquine (limited by neuropsychiatric effects).
  • Radical cure: primaquine or tafenoquine kill liver hypnozoites of P. vivax/P. ovale; check G6PD first (hemolysis if deficient).
  • Buzzwords: 'cinchonism', 'heme polymerase', 'hypnozoites', 'G6PD before primaquine', 'bed nets', 'continue prophylaxis 4 weeks'.
  1. 1Sectional Champs map: Chloroquine was used so widely that chloroquine-resistant malaria is now widespread; chloroquine-sensitive areas are limited (e.g., Central America west of the Panama Canal, Hispaniola, parts of the Middle East and Korea).⭐ Chloroquine-resistant P. falciparum is the default assumption in most endemic areas.
  2. 2Colorful queen: Chloroquine is an antimalarial (a 4-aminoquinoline) that acts as a blood-stage schizonticide.
  3. 3Chameleon eating fly: Chloroquine is given orally, has a large volume of distribution, and maintains sustained plasma levels.
  4. 4Heme crown knocked down: Chloroquine accumulates in the parasite food vacuole and forms a drug-heme complex that blocks heme polymerization (crystallization into hemozoin). Free heme is cytotoxic and the schizont dies.⭐ Mechanism: inhibits heme polymerase.
  5. 5False-masked chaperone: P. falciparum (false mask) is frequently resistant to chloroquine.
  6. 6Expelling colorful party crasher: Chloroquine resistance results from mutations in genes encoding transport proteins (e.g., PfCRT) that efflux the drug, so it does not accumulate in the food vacuole.
  7. 7Artemis queen: Artemisinins (artemether, artesunate) are potent antimalarials and first-line treatment for resistant malaria, usually combined with a partner drug (ACT).
  8. 8Ato-vampire queen + iguana: Atovaquone-proguanil is a dual-agent drug effective against chloroquine-resistant malaria, used for treatment and prophylaxis.⭐ Prophylaxis can stop 7 days after leaving the endemic area.
  9. 9Bicycle wheels + tonic water: A tetracycline (doxycycline) plus quinine (tonic water) is effective therapy for chloroquine-resistant malaria.
  10. 10Tin can + headphones + sunglasses + brain hat: Quinine can cause cinchonism: tinnitus, hearing loss, vision loss/blurring, and altered mental status (also headache, nausea).⭐ Quinine can also cause hypoglycemia and QT prolongation.
  11. 11My flock queen: Mefloquine is effective against chloroquine-resistant malaria but is an unfavored option.
  12. 12Brain bonnet: Mefloquine has limited use because of neuropsychiatric adverse effects (vivid dreams, sleep, mood and behavior disturbances, psychosis, seizures).⭐ Avoid in patients with psychiatric illness or seizure history.
  13. 13Prom queen: Primaquine is active against liver hypnozoites of P. vivax and P. ovale (radical cure).
  14. 14Tough queen: Tafenoquine, a single-dose alternative, also kills liver hypnozoites.
  15. 15'G6PD free' watermelon: Primaquine (and tafenoquine) are contraindicated in G6PD deficiency: oxidative stress causes hemolysis, anemia, and hemoglobinuria.⭐ Test G6PD before use.
  16. 16Artemis + ivy streamers + skull: IV artesunate is indicated for severe, life-threatening malaria (parasitemia >5%, decreased consciousness, seizures, multisystem collapse).⭐ Follow IV artesunate with a full oral course.
  17. 17Basketball net: Sleeping under insecticide-treated bed nets prevents mosquito bites and reduces malaria transmission.
  18. 18PPX shield: Malaria chemoprophylaxis is indicated for travelers to areas at high risk of malaria transmission.
  19. 19'Homecoming = 4 weeks!' flyer: Continue mefloquine, chloroquine, or doxycycline for 4 weeks after the last exposure (after returning from the malaria-endemic area).⭐ Atovaquone-proguanil needs only 7 days after return.
Show the original legend picture

85. Pneumocystis jirovecii

No Sketchy video/scene available for this organism in the current deck — notes below are compiled directly from your Anki cards.

ID / Micro
  • Fungus, respiratory transmission; asymptomatic in the immunocompetent
  • Stains with methenamine silver (shared with Cryptococcus neoformans, Coccidioides, and Legionella)
  • BAL shows "disc-shaped yeast"/cysts containing dark oval bodies
Clinical
  • AIDS-defining illness at CD4+ < 200 cells/mm³
  • Interstitial (atypical) pneumonia — no productive cough, no consolidation, "ground-glass" appearance on CXR
  • Diagnosis confirmed via bronchoalveolar lavage
Prophylaxis & Treatment
  • First-line for both prophylaxis and treatment: TMP-SMX, started in HIV patients at CD4+ < 200/mm³
  • Second-line prophylaxis: dapsone or atovaquone

86. Poliovirus

  1. 1The polio flamingo represents poliovirus, a positive-sense RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family.
  2. 2The warm colors represent that poliovirus is a positive-sense RNA virus.
  3. 3The positive sun represents that poliovirus is a positive-sense RNA virus.
  4. 4The baby flamingo represents that poliovirus is part of the Picornaviridae family.
  5. 5The Statue of David represents that poliovirus lacks an envelope, classifying it as a naked virus.
  6. 6The flamingos protected from acid represent that poliovirus is acid-stable, allowing it to survive in the gastric environment and facilitating fecal-oral transmission.
  7. 7The patches of poop represent that poliovirus enters the body via the oropharynx and gut mucosa, particularly Peyer's patches in the ileal submucosa, from where it can spread systemically; this process typically takes about 2-3 weeks.
  8. 8The "breeding season 2-3 weeks" sign represents that poliovirus enters the body via the oropharynx and gut mucosa and initially replicates in the oropharynx and gut mucosa, particularly Peyer's patches in the ileal submucosa, from where it can spread systemically; this process typically takes about 2-3 weeks.
  9. 9The anterior hornbill beak represents that after replicating in the oropharynx and gut, poliovirus enters the bloodstream, reaches peripheral nerves, travels via retrograde axonal transport, and destroys anterior horn motor neurons, which are lower motor neurons, causing neurological damage.
  10. 10The folding of one leg represents that the paralysis induced by poliovirus infection typically manifests asymmetrically, affecting one side of the body more severely, and may be accompanied by myalgias and flaccid weakness with decreased reflexes and lower motor neuron damage.
  11. 11The distressed puff of air represents that poliovirus can cause respiratory insufficiency from destruction of anterior horn motor neurons, with diaphragmatic (C3-C5), and intercostal paralysis leading to respiratory failure, the major cause of death in polio; bulbar poliomyelitis involves brainstem motor neurons (CN IX, X, XII), causing dysphagia, impaired gag reflex, and aspiration risk, which can further compromise respiration.
  12. 12The meningitis helmet represents that poliovirus can cross the blood-brain barrier and infect the meninges, causing aseptic meningitis, which may present with fever, headache, nausea, vomiting, neck stiffness, and sometimes photophobia.
  13. 13The sulking child with syringe bones represents that the Salk vaccine is an inactivated poliovirus vaccine (IPV) administered intramuscularly to protect against poliomyelitis; it has been the only polio vaccine used in the United States since 2000 and primarily elicits a systemic humoral immune response, producing circulating IgG antibodies that prevent paralytic disease, but because it is injected and not ingested, it induces minimal mucosal (gut) IgA immunity, so vaccinated individuals can still be infected and shed virus.
  14. 14The "Savin's life" plus coin in opening represents that the live attenuated Sabin vaccine is given orally to induce both systemic IgG and mucosal IgA responses, preventing paralytic disease and limiting gut viral replication. Originally trivalent, it was replaced in 2016 by bivalent OPV to reduce the risk of vaccine-derived polioviruses (VDPVs), particularly type 2, which can rarely revert to neurovirulent forms and cause paralysis. OPV is used globally for mass immunization due to ease of oral administration and herd immunity benefits, but it is contraindicated in immunocompromised individuals because of the risk of vaccine-associated paralytic poliomyelitis (VAPP).

87. Proteus mirabilis

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: complicated UTI with alkaline urine, flank pain, and staghorn calculi.
  • Microbiology: gram-negative bacillus, lactose non-fermenter, urease positive, highly motile (swarming on agar), H2S producer.
  • Diagnosis: urine culture; alkaline urine pH; struvite (magnesium ammonium phosphate) stones, coffin-lid crystals, on imaging/urinalysis.
  • Treatment: antibiotics (e.g., trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole for uncomplicated UTI); large stones often need urologic removal because bacteria persist in the stone.
  • Buzzwords: 'swarming', 'urease', 'struvite', 'staghorn calculi', 'alkaline urine', 'fishy/ammonia odor'.
  1. 1Proteus: Proteus mirabilis, a gram-negative bacillus.
  2. 2Red hues: Red hues = gram-negative: Proteus mirabilis is a gram-negative bacillus (lactose non-fermenter).
  3. 3Swarming tentacles: Proteus mirabilis shows swarming motility on agar plates, spreading in concentric waves (a clue to its flagellar motility).⭐ 'Swarming' on culture is a classic buzzword.
  4. 4Staghorn helmet: Urease-positive bacteria like Proteus can cause staghorn calculi (struvite stones) that fill the renal pelvis and calyces like stag horns, causing flank pain and renal impairment and harboring bacteria.⭐ Staghorn calculus = think Proteus.
  5. 5Ammonia bottle: Proteus produces urease, which hydrolyzes urea into CO2 and ammonia, raising urine pH. The alkaline urine promotes precipitation of magnesium ammonium phosphate (struvite) stones.
  6. 6Stone: Struvite stones cause pain and urinary tract damage and act as a nidus for recurrent Proteus infections, since bacteria hide within the stone.⭐ Urine pH is alkaline (>7).
  7. 7Peeing: Proteus mirabilis is a common cause of urinary tract infections, especially complicated cases or those with abnormal urinary tracts or catheters.
  8. 8Stinky fish: Proteus mirabilis produces a characteristic fishy (ammonia) odor.
  9. 9Eggs: Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (a sulfonamide combination) is active against Proteus mirabilis and commonly used for uncomplicated UTIs; the egg is a cue for the antibiotic choice.⭐ Fluoroquinolones and cephalosporins are other options.

88. Pseudomonas aeruginosa

  1. 1Pseudo Mona: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a gram-negative bacillus.
  2. 2Red bathtub: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a gram-negative bacillus.
  3. 3Water: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is commonly found in aquatic environments (e.g., lakes, rivers, swimming pools).
  4. 4Blue oxidase ring: Pseudomonas aeruginosa produces oxidase (i.e., is oxidase-positive), an enzyme that plays a vital role in aerobic respiration.
  5. 5Catalase cat: Pseudomonas aeruginosa produces catalase (i.e., is catalase-positive), an enzyme that converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen.
  6. 6Blue-green residue: Pseudomonas aeruginosa produces characteristic pigments, such as pyocyanin (blue), pyoverdin (yellow-green), pyomelanin (brown), and pyorubin (red).
  7. 7Aromatic grapes: Cultures of Pseudomonas aeruginosa often emit a fruity, grape-like odor.
  8. 8Aerobic bellows: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is an obligate aerobe, requiring oxygen for growth and survival.
  9. 9Nurse: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a prevalent cause of nosocomial (hospital-acquired) infections.
  10. 10Chloride + coughing: In cystic fibrosis, the CFTR chloride channel is defective, leading to decreased Cl- secretion into the airway lumen and decreased water in the mucus; uninhibited ENaC then causes Na+ to move from the mucus into cells, followed by water, resulting in dehydrated, thick mucus. This impairs mucociliary clearance, promoting Pseudomonas aeruginosa colonization, biofilm formation, chronic lung infection, and ultimately respiratory failure.
  11. 11Candy: Diabetics and intravenous drug users have increased susceptibility to Pseudomonas osteomyelitis.
  12. 12Mortar and pestle: Diabetics and intravenous drug users have increased susceptibility to Pseudomonas osteomyelitis.
  13. 13Fish bones: Diabetics and intravenous drug users have increased susceptibility to Pseudomonas osteomyelitis.
  14. 14Glass capsule: Pseudomonas aeruginosa produces a capsule-like polysaccharide called alginate.
  15. 15Burning fire: Burn injuries significantly elevate the risk of wound infections caused by Pseudomonas aeruginosa: barrier loss plus a moist wound leads to Pseudomonas colonization, then infection, then sepsis.
  16. 16Chamber pot: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is frequently associated with urinary tract infections, particularly in the presence of indwelling urinary catheters, due to biofilm formation.
  17. 17Bathtub: Pseudomonas aeruginosa can cause hot tub folliculitis, producing itchy red bumps around hair follicles after exposure to warm, poorly chlorinated water.
  18. 18Dalmatian spots: Ecthyma gangrenosum, characterized by black necrotic skin lesions, can be a manifestation of Pseudomonas aeruginosa sepsis, particularly in immunocompromised patients.
  19. 19Running dalmatian: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is motile, exhibiting swarming motility on semisolid surfaces.
  20. 20Ear trumpet: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a common cause of otitis externa ("swimmer's ear"), causing pain, itching, and purulent discharge.
  21. 21Accordion + bowtie: Exotoxin A of Pseudomonas aeruginosa inactivates elongation factor-2 (EF-2) via ADP ribosylation, similar to diphtheria toxin.
  22. 22Piper: Piperacillin and ticarcillin are antipseudomonal penicillins effective against Pseudomonas aeruginosa.
  23. 23Flower: Fluoroquinolones can be used to treat Pseudomonas aeruginosa infections, but resistance emerges easily.
  24. 24Sai: Aminoglycosides (e.g., tobramycin, gentamicin, amikacin) are commonly used against Pseudomonas aeruginosa infections.

89. Rabies virus

  1. 1The Rabid Bulldog represents Rabies virus, a negative-sense RNA virus in the Rhabdoviridae family.
  2. 2The warm colors represent that Rhabdoviridae are negative-sense, single-stranded RNA viruses.
  3. 3The moon with negative sign-shaped clouds represents that Rhabdoviridae are negative-sense, single-stranded RNA viruses.
  4. 4The curly tail represents that Rhabdoviridae have a helical nucleocapsid.
  5. 5The hoodie represents that Rhabdoviridae are enveloped viruses that acquire their envelope by budding from the host cell membrane; their spike-shaped glycoprotein (G protein) mediates attachment to host cell receptors.
  6. 6The bullets represent that the capsule of Rhabdoviridae has a bullet- or rod-shaped morphology.
  7. 7The bat represents that rabies is a zoonotic disease (i.e. it is transmitted from animals to humans) primarily spread by bats in the developed world.
  8. 8The raccoon represents that in the United States, the primary wildlife reservoirs of rabies virus are bats, raccoons, skunks, and foxes, with the predominant reservoir varying by geographic region.
  9. 9The skunk represents that in the United States, the primary wildlife reservoirs of rabies virus are bats, raccoons, skunks, and foxes, with the predominant reservoir varying by geographic region.
  10. 10The fox represents that in the United States, the primary wildlife reservoirs of rabies virus are bats, raccoons, skunks, and foxes, with the predominant reservoir varying by geographic region.
  11. 11The cigar represents that the host cell attachment site for rabies virus is the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor on the postsynaptic membrane of the neuromuscular junction, also called the motor end plate.
  12. 12The acetyl cola cans represent that the host cell attachment site for rabies virus is the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor on the postsynaptic membrane of the neuromuscular junction, also called the motor end plate.
  13. 13The outlet cover plate represents that the host cell attachment site for rabies virus is the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor on the postsynaptic membrane of the neuromuscular junction, also called the motor end plate.
  14. 14The baby bulldogs near the motor area of the car represent that after inoculation (usually via an animal bite), rabies virus initially replicates in muscle tissue near the motor end plate (postsynaptic membrane of the neuromuscular junction).
  15. 15The bullet leash represents that after replicating in muscle near the motor end plate, the rabies virus binds to nicotinic acetylcholine receptors and travels retrogradely along peripheral nerves to the CNS; once there, it spreads centrifugally via autonomic and somatic nerves to highly innervated tissues such as the salivary glands, cornea, and skin, enabling viral shedding and transmission.
  16. 16The tree roots represent that after reaching the CNS, the rabies virus can infect neurons within the dorsal root ganglia, which contributes to sensory symptoms such as pain or tingling at the site of the bite.
  17. 17Sweating represents that the prodrome includes flu-like symptoms (fever, sore throat, headache, photophobia) and abnormal sensations (burning, warmth) at the site of the bite wound.
  18. 18Foam on mouth represents that in rabies, the virus travels via peripheral nerves to the CNS and then to highly innervated submaxillary salivary glands, causing hypersalivation, laryngeal spasm, and dysphagia that together produce the characteristic foaming at the mouth and enable viral shedding in saliva.
  19. 19The red turban represents that rabies causes a severe viral encephalitis in which infection and the immune response produce neuronal injury, edema, and inflammatory infiltrates in the brain parenchyma, presenting with fever, headache, altered mental status, seizures, and focal neurological deficits, and is usually fatal once symptoms appear.
  20. 20The iNtEGRIty boat represents that Negri bodies are eosinophilic, pinkish-red, round or oval cytoplasmic inclusions in neurons, seen on H&E stain, representing aggregates of rabies virus nucleocapsid proteins, and are pathognomonic for rabies infection on postmortem examination.
  21. 21The rust spots represent that Negri bodies are eosinophilic, pinkish-red, round or oval cytoplasmic inclusions in neurons, seen on H&E stain, representing aggregates of rabies virus nucleocapsid proteins, and are pathognomonic for rabies infection on postmortem examination.
  22. 22The seahorse decal and pyramid-shaped tent represent that Negri bodies are primarily found in hippocampal pyramidal neurons and cerebellar Purkinje cells in rabies infection on postmortem examination.
  23. 23The bungee cords and cerebellar tree represent that Negri bodies are primarily found in hippocampal pyramidal neurons and cerebellar Purkinje cells in rabies infection on postmortem examination.
  24. 24The antibody keys represent that passive immunity in rabies post-exposure prophylaxis is achieved by administering rabies immune globulin (RIG), which contains pre-formed antibodies against the rabies virus; these provide immediate, short-term protection by neutralizing the virus at the wound site and preventing it from entering peripheral nerves, bridging the time until active immunity develops from the vaccine.
  25. 25The syringe tranquilizer gun and skull and crossbones represent that active immunity in rabies post-exposure prophylaxis is achieved with the rabies vaccine, an inactivated (killed) virus vaccine; it stimulates the patient's immune system to produce neutralizing antibodies over 7-14 days, providing long-term protection against rabies; pre-exposure prophylaxis with rabies inactivated (killed) vaccine is indicated in high-risk groups (zoo keepers, vets, lab workers, travelers to rabies-endemic regions).

90. Rhinovirus

  1. 1Rhinos: Rhinoviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses in the Picornaviridae family.
  2. 2Warm colors: Rhinoviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  3. 3Positive sun: Rhinoviruses are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 4Baby rhino: Rhinoviruses are part of the Picornaviridae family.
  5. 5Statue of David: Rhinoviruses lack envelopes, classifying them as naked viruses.
  6. 6Acidic lemon: Rhinoviruses are acid-labile, meaning they cannot survive in the hostile environment of the gastrointestinal tract, so unlike other picornaviruses, they are not transmitted via the fecal-oral route.
  7. 7Sneeze spray: Rhinoviruses are primarily transmitted via respiratory droplets, either by inhalation or indirectly through fomites, or contaminated objects like doorknobs or phone screens.
  8. 8"Please wash hands": Proper hand hygiene is crucial in reducing the transmission risk of rhinovirus, especially through fomites (contaminated objects).
  9. 91 camera: Rhinovirus utilizes the intercellular adhesion molecule 1 (ICAM-1) receptor present on host cells to facilitate cellular entry.
  10. 10Rhinos in 33° shade: Optimal replication of rhinovirus occurs at slightly cooler temperatures (~33°C/91.4°F), approximately the temperature of the nasal cavity, explaining its predilection for causing upper respiratory infections.
  11. 11Mud on nose and mouth: Rhinovirus most commonly causes self-limited upper respiratory infections, presenting with rhinorrhea, nasal congestion, sneezing, and sore throat.
  12. 12Multicolored tent: Rhinoviruses have over 100 serotypes, which makes vaccine development impractical because immunity to one serotype provides little to no protection against the others.

91. Rickettsia Overview

Exam snapshot
  • Headache and fever followed by a maculopapular or petechial rash from small-vessel vasculitis, often after an arthropod vector bite.
  • Obligate intracellular, weakly gram-negative coccobacilli; need host CoA and NAD+; stain with Giemsa or Gimenez; infect endothelial cells.
  • Clinical suspicion plus serology (immunofluorescence) or PCR; Weil-Felix (Proteus OX cross-reactivity) is historical.
  • Doxycycline is first-line for all rickettsial infections (including children and pregnancy); do not wait for confirmation.
  • Obligate intracellular, vasculitis, Weil-Felix, arthropod vectors, Giemsa/Gimenez.
  1. 1"Rickett's tires": Rickettsia spp. are weakly gram-negative coccobacilli.
  2. 2Indoor stadium: Rickettsia are obligate intracellular pathogens that need host cells for replication and survival.
  3. 3Colorless crowd: Although the cell wall is chemically gram-negative-like, Rickettsia stain poorly with Gram stain; they are visualized with Giemsa or Gimenez stain.⭐ Gram-negative cell wall but poor Gram staining.
  4. 4CoAch: Rickettsia depend on host cells for coenzyme A (CoA), needed for metabolism, and for NAD+, needed for energy production and growth.
  5. 5"NAD+" water bottle: Same point: host-derived NAD+ (along with CoA) is required, which is why they cannot grow outside host cells.
  6. 6Light pink tennis balls: Rickettsia are weakly gram-negative (light pink) coccobacilli.
  7. 7Weil and Felix: The Weil-Felix test is an agglutination assay based on cross-reactivity between Proteus antigens (OX-19, OX-2, OX-K) and Rickettsia antibodies. It was historically the main diagnostic test, has been largely replaced by immunofluorescence and other serology, but may still be used where resources are limited.⭐ Weil-Felix: Proteus antigens agglutinate with Rickettsia antibodies.
  8. 8Holding head: Common initial symptoms of rickettsial infection include headache and fever.
  9. 9Sweating: Fever is a common early symptom of rickettsial infection, along with headache.
  10. 10Broken red strings: Rickettsiae infect endothelial cells, causing injury and inflammation (small-vessel vasculitis) with increased vascular permeability and red blood cell leakage, producing a maculopapular or petechial rash.⭐ Vasculitis explains rash, edema, and hypotension.
  11. 11Bumpy red ground: The resulting rash is maculopapular or petechial, from small-vessel vasculitis and RBC leakage.
  12. 12Bicycle tires: Doxycycline is first-line treatment for all rickettsial infections, including children and pregnant women, because benefits outweigh risks.⭐ Treat empirically; do not delay for test results.

92. Rickettsia prowazekii

Exam snapshot
  • Epidemic typhus: fever, headache, and a rash that starts on the trunk and spreads outward, sparing palms, soles, and face, in crowded, unsanitary settings.
  • Weakly gram-negative coccobacillus, obligate intracellular; transmitted by body louse feces rubbed into bite wounds.
  • Clinical and epidemiologic suspicion; serology (immunofluorescence); Weil-Felix is historical; stain with Giemsa or Gimenez.
  • Doxycycline is first-line; control by delousing and hygiene.
  • Epidemic typhus, body louse (Pediculus humanus corporis), war and refugee camps, centrifugal rash, Brill-Zinsser disease.
  1. 1"PRO bootcamp": Rickettsia prowazekii is a weakly gram-negative coccobacillus that causes epidemic typhus.
  2. 2Indoor stadium: R. prowazekii is an obligate intracellular pathogen requiring host cells for replication and survival.
  3. 3Colorless crowd: Despite a gram-negative-type cell wall, R. prowazekii stains poorly with Gram stain and is visualized with Giemsa or Gimenez stain.
  4. 4CoAch: R. prowazekii depends on host cells for coenzyme A (CoA) and NAD+, needed for metabolism, energy production, and growth.
  5. 5"NAD+" water bottle: Same point: host NAD+ (and CoA) are required for its energy production and growth.
  6. 6Bicycle tires: Doxycycline is first-line treatment for R. prowazekii infection, including in children and pregnant women.
  7. 7Outward football play: The epidemic typhus rash starts on the trunk and spreads outward to the limbs (centrifugal), usually sparing the face, palms, and soles. Early lesions are small pink macules; later they become dull red and maculopapular (blanchable); in severe cases petechiae develop from small-vessel vasculitis.⭐ Centrifugal spread, sparing palms and soles (opposite of RMSF, which starts on wrists and ankles).
  8. 8Red clothes + white helmet, gloves, and shoes: Same rash description: the trunk (red clothes) is involved, while the face (helmet), palms (gloves), and soles (shoes) are spared.
  9. 9Military coach: Epidemic typhus is associated with war (military recruits, prisoners of war), refugee camps, poverty, homelessness, crowding, and unsanitary conditions.
  10. 10Lice footballs: Transmitted by body louse feces: the louse bites and defecates near the bite site, and scratching rubs the infected feces into the skin.⭐ Vector: Pediculus humanus corporis (body louse); not by the bite itself.
  11. 11"The outbreak" play: "Epidemic" typhus: outbreaks occur in crowded, unsanitary settings where body louse infestations spread rapidly from person to person.⭐ Recrudescent disease years later is Brill-Zinsser disease.
  12. 12Painful tackle: Myalgias and arthralgias are common in epidemic typhus, from systemic small-vessel vasculitis and inflammation.
  13. 13Tackling chest: Some patients develop pulmonary complications such as pneumonia, due to systemic vasculitis, impaired immune responses, and secondary lung infection.
  14. 14Holding head + confused: Epidemic typhus can cause encephalitis from rickettsial vasculitis in the brain; vascular injury and ischemia cause fever, dizziness, and confusion, and in severe cases seizures or coma.

93. Rickettsia rickettsii

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: fever, headache and myalgias after a tick bite, followed 2-14 days later by a rash starting on wrists and ankles
  • Weakly gram-negative coccobacillus, obligate intracellular, infects endothelial cells (vasculitis)
  • Dx: clinical, then skin biopsy (immunohistochemistry), PCR, serology; Giemsa or Gimenez stain
  • Treatment: doxycycline (all ages, including children and pregnancy), started empirically
  • Buzzwords: Rocky Mountain spotted fever, Dermacentor tick, centripetal rash, palms and soles, Weil-Felix
  1. 1Rickett's rock climbing: Rickettsia rickettsii is a weakly gram-negative coccobacillus.
  2. 2Rock climbing holds: Rocky Mountain spotted fever, a serious tick-borne illness, is caused by Rickettsia rickettsii.⭐ Most cases in the southeastern and south-central U.S., not the Rockies.
  3. 3Indoor arena: Rickettsia rickettsii is an obligate intracellular pathogen that needs host cells for replication and survival.
  4. 4Colorless crowd: Although its cell wall is similar to that of gram-negative bacteria, R. rickettsii stains poorly with Gram stain; Rickettsia are visualized with Giemsa or Gimenez stain.
  5. 5CoAch: R. rickettsii depends on host cells for coenzyme A (CoA) and for NAD+, both needed for metabolism and growth.⭐ It cannot make its own CoA or NAD+, so it is an obligate intracellular parasite.
  6. 6"NAD+" water bottle: R. rickettsii depends on host cells for NAD+ (and coenzyme A), which are essential for energy production and growth.
  7. 7Bicycle tire: Doxycycline is first-line treatment for R. rickettsii infection, including in children and pregnant women.⭐ Start empirically; do not wait for confirmatory tests. Chloramphenicol is an alternative only in pregnancy.
  8. 8"Derma-Center" arena: The Dermacentor tick (dog or wood tick) is the primary vector; the tick transmits R. rickettsii through its saliva during a blood meal, usually requiring 6-10 hours of attachment; the bite is often painless and unnoticed, and most infections occur in spring and summer.
  9. 9Tick climbing holds: The Dermacentor tick is the primary vector of R. rickettsii; transmission usually requires 6-10 hours of attachment, the bite is often painless and unnoticed, and most infections occur in spring and summer.
  10. 10Holding head: The RMSF rash typically appears 2-14 days after early symptoms (fever, headache, myalgias), so patients may initially present without a rash.⭐ Do not wait for the rash to treat.
  11. 11No spots: The RMSF rash typically develops days after the onset of fever, headache and myalgias, so early disease can look like a nonspecific viral illness without spots.
  12. 12Spots on wrists and ankles: The RMSF rash usually begins on the wrists and ankles and spreads centrally to the trunk. Early lesions are small, pink macules that may become maculopapular, and in severe cases petechial; the palms and soles are often involved.⭐ Centripetal spread (wrists/ankles to trunk) is the classic pattern.
  13. 13Spots on whole body: The rash spreads centrally to the trunk and can become petechial, often involving the palms and soles, in more severe disease.⭐ Contrast with the rash of typhus (begins on trunk and spreads outward, sparing palms and soles).
  14. 14Grabbing head + sweating: RMSF manifests with headache and fever, caused by systemic inflammation and cytokine release from endothelial infection and small-vessel vasculitis.
  15. 15Soreness + rash: RMSF classically presents with headache, fever, myalgias and rash, resulting from endothelial infection and small-vessel vasculitis (vascular leak).⭐ Classic triad: fever, headache, rash, plus tick exposure.
