94. Rotavirus & Colorado Tick Fever Virus (Reoviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Rotavirus: most common cause of severe watery diarrhea and dehydration in children under 5 worldwide, peaking in winter
  • Mechanism: NSP4 enterotoxin plus villous destruction causes secretory and malabsorptive diarrhea; diagnosis by stool antigen (ELISA)
  • Reoviridae: naked, double-stranded segmented RNA viruses (rotavirus 11 segments), replicating in the cytoplasm
  • Treatment is supportive (oral rehydration); prevention is the live oral vaccine (RotaTeq, Rotarix), with a small intussusception risk
  • Colorado tick fever: Rocky Mountain tick bite (Dermacentor andersoni), biphasic fever, flu-like illness, usually no rash
  1. 1Rio Grande Prix: Reoviruses (e.g., rotavirus and Colorado tick fever virus) are RNA viruses; 'Reo' stands for Respiratory Enteric Orphan.
  2. 2Warm colors: Reoviruses are RNA viruses (the warm colors group RNA viruses).
  3. 3Statue of David: Reoviruses lack an envelope (naked viruses). They are resistant to drying and stomach acid and spread fecal-oral.
  4. 4Double-stranded rope: Reoviruses are double-stranded RNA viruses. Because they are dsRNA, they are not classified as positive- or negative-sense, and they replicate in the cytoplasm. They carry their own RNA-dependent RNA polymerase in the virion, because host cells cannot copy dsRNA.
  5. 5No positive sun + no moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Reoviruses are not classified as positive-sense or negative-sense (no sun, no negative moon) because the genome is double-stranded RNA. Each segment is transcribed by the viral polymerase to make mRNA in the cytoplasm.
  6. 611 boats: Reoviruses have segmented genomes (about 10-12 dsRNA segments); rotavirus has 11 segments. Segmentation allows reassortment between strains, which can generate new strains.⭐ Rotavirus = 11 segments, which can reassort.
  7. 7Boat rotor: Rotavirus is a double-stranded RNA virus in the Reoviridae family. Its wheel-like appearance on electron microscopy gives the name (Latin rota = wheel).
  8. 8Brown water: Rotavirus causes gastroenteritis with profuse watery diarrhea (non-bloody), often with vomiting and fever, and can lead to dehydration in infants. Diagnosis is by stool antigen detection (ELISA).
  9. 9"Nine SPeed 4 stroke": Rotavirus watery diarrhea is mediated by the NSP4 (nonstructural protein 4) enterotoxin, which increases Cl- permeability and causes Cl- secretion into the intestinal lumen. Na+ follows to maintain electroneutrality, water follows Na+ osmotically, and destruction of villous epithelium worsens fluid and electrolyte loss.check original
  10. 10"Chlo-rider" boat: NSP4 enterotoxin increases chloride permeability, so Cl- is secreted into the lumen and Na+ and water follow. Destruction of mature villous enterocytes adds malabsorptive loss of fluid and electrolytes.check original
  11. 11Snow-capped mountains: Rotavirus incidence peaks in winter in temperate climates, because the virus survives longer in cold, low-humidity conditions and indoor crowding promotes fecal-oral spread.
  12. 12Kids: Children, especially those under 5 years old, are most susceptible to rotavirus. Day care outbreaks are common.
  13. 13"#1" foam finger: Rotavirus is the leading cause of severe diarrhea in young children worldwide. Vaccination has dramatically reduced hospitalizations.
  14. 14"Welcome to Colorado" + tick: Colorado tick fever virus is a dsRNA virus in the Reoviridae family. It is transmitted by the Rocky Mountain wood tick (Dermacentor andersoni).
  15. 15Vomiting + sweating: Colorado tick fever presents with flu-like symptoms (fever, chills, headache, myalgias, fatigue, anorexia, vomiting), often with a biphasic ('saddleback') fever, in high-elevation areas of the western U.S. (Rocky Mountain states). Unlike Rocky Mountain spotted fever, it typically has no rash.⭐ Tick-borne fever without rash in the Rockies = Colorado tick fever.
  16. 16Water bottle: Treatment of rotavirus and Colorado tick fever is supportive. Oral rehydration therapy is the mainstay for rotavirus (IV fluids if severe dehydration).
  17. 17"Live" + syringe microphone: The rotavirus vaccine is a live attenuated oral vaccine: RotaTeq (pentavalent, 3 doses at 2, 4, and 6 months) or Rotarix (monovalent, 2 doses at 2 and 4 months). The first dose must be given before 15 weeks of age and the series completed by 8 months, to reduce intussusception risk. It is contraindicated in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) and prior intussusception.
  18. 18Telescope: The rotavirus vaccine can rarely cause intussusception (bowel 'telescoping' into itself): viral replication in the gut causes Peyer's patch hypertrophy, which can act as a lead point. Presents with colicky abdominal pain, vomiting, and 'currant jelly' stool.
Show the original legend picture

95. RSV / hMPV

  1. 35RSV tombstone: respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) belongs to the Pneumoviridae family (genus Orthopneumovirus), which also includes human metapneumovirus (hMPV); RSV was previously classified under Paramyxoviridae before Pneumoviridae became its own family.
  2. 36Warm colors: Pneumoviridae are negative-sense RNA viruses.
  3. 37Moon with negative-sign-shaped clouds: Pneumoviridae are negative-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 38More ghosts with envelopes: Pneumoviridae are enveloped viruses with a helical nucleocapsid.
  5. 39Ghost baby holding a "G" letter: Pneumoviridae infect respiratory epithelial cells via the G protein, which is also a major target for neutralizing antibodies.
  6. 40Sticky hands: Pneumoviridae fuse with the host cell membrane and form syncytia via the F protein, another major target for neutralizing antibodies (targeted by a humanized monoclonal antibody used prophylactically).
  7. 41More water droplets: Pneumoviridae are transmitted via direct contact or large respiratory droplets.
  8. 42Bronchial tree pattern with infiltrates on the baby ghosts: RSV is the most common cause of bronchiolitis and pneumonia in infants, infecting bronchiolar epithelium and causing necrosis, mucus plugging, and airway obstruction; CXR typically shows hyperinflation and peribronchial infiltrates rather than lobar consolidation.
  9. 43Baby ghosts: RSV primarily affects infants under 1 year old, with peak severity at 2–6 months; prematurity is a risk factor for severe infection, and RSV is a major cause of illness in the first year of life.
  10. 44Ribs: ribavirin, a nucleoside analog, can be used to treat RSV in immunocompromised patients (pregnancy is an important contraindication).
  11. 45Pale ghost with an antibody rattle: palivizumab, a monoclonal IgG1 antibody against RSV, binds the virus to prevent it from entering and infecting cells; it may be used prophylactically to prevent severe RSV disease in high-risk children (premature infants, chronic lung disease, or hemodynamically significant heart disease).

96. Rubella virus

  1. 1Warm colors: Rubella virus and Togaviridae are RNA viruses with a single positive-sense strand.
  2. 2Positive sun: Rubella virus and Togaviridae are RNA viruses with a single positive-sense strand.
  3. 3Man in toga carrying robe: Togaviridae are enveloped viruses.
  4. 4Arbor: Members of Togaviridae (genus Alphavirus) are arthropod-borne viruses (or arboviruses) transmitted to humans by insect vectors (e.g., mosquitoes).
  5. 5Mosquitoes swarming around man: Members of Togaviridae (genus Alphavirus) are arthropod-borne viruses (or arboviruses) transmitted to humans by insect vectors (e.g., mosquitoes).
  6. 6Compass on horse: The equine encephalitides—Eastern Equine Encephalitis Virus (EEEV, eastern North America), Western Equine Encephalitis Virus (WEEV, western North America), and Venezuelan Equine Encephalitis Virus (VEEV, Central and South America)—are Alphaviruses (family Togaviridae).
  7. 7Neck brace: Neuroinvasive manifestations of the equine encephalitides include meningitis (fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, nausea/vomiting) and encephalitis (fever, altered mental status, seizures, focal neurologic deficits, movement disorders); Eastern Equine Encephalitis Virus (EEEV) is the most severe, carrying the highest risk of death and long-term neurologic morbidity.
  8. 8Red turban + hitting head: Neuroinvasive manifestations of the equine encephalitides include meningitis (fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, nausea/vomiting) and encephalitis (fever, altered mental status, seizures, focal neurologic deficits, movement disorders); Eastern Equine Encephalitis Virus (EEEV) is the most severe, carrying the highest risk of death and long-term neurologic morbidity.
  9. 9Rubies: Rubella is the only member of the Rubivirus genus (within the Matonaviridae family) and presents in three distinct clinical forms depending on age: congenital rubella, childhood rubella, and adult rubella.
  10. 10Child emperor wearing robe: Rubella virus is enveloped.
  11. 11Roll of tickets: The RNA genome of Rubella virus is translated into a single, extended polyprotein, which is then cleaved by proteases.
  12. 12Nucleus-shaped dome in background: Positive-sense RNA viruses replicate in the cytoplasm, outside the nucleus, with their genomic RNA ready for immediate translation by host ribosomes.
  13. 13Child emperor: Rubella virus is an important cause of a childhood exanthem (widespread skin rash).
  14. 14Servants fanning king: Rubella virus spreads via respiratory droplets, replicates in the nasopharynx during an incubation period of 2–3 weeks (asymptomatic), spreads to lymph nodes, and then enters the bloodstream (viremia, meaning virus in the blood), often causing a mild prodrome of low-grade fever, malaise, mild URI symptoms, and tender lymphadenopathy that precedes the characteristic rash.
  15. 15Spreading rubies: Rubella virus causes a childhood exanthem—a spreading maculopapular rash that begins on the face and extends to the trunk and extremities—occurring after viremia as an immune-mediated response to viral antigens in small skin blood vessels; the rash spreads more rapidly than measles, and unlike measles, rubella typically does not darken or coalesce, resolving within about three days (“3-day measles”).
  16. 16Beaded necklace behind ears: Rubella is often associated with tender lymphadenopathy, particularly in the posterior auricular, suboccipital, and posterior cervical regions, from immune response to viral replication.
  17. 17Kneeling adult in pain: Adults infected with rubella virus may experience arthralgias (joint pain), often affecting small joints, sometimes accompanied by mild fever and malaise.
  18. 18Baby sculptures on walls: Congenital rubella infection encompasses negative pregnancy outcomes (e.g., miscarriage, stillbirth) and the congenital rubella syndrome in the neonate.
  19. 19“TORCHeS”: Congenital rubella syndrome (CRS) is part of the TORCHeS group of infections: T: Toxoplasma; O: Other (varicella-zoster virus, parvovirus B19, Zika virus, HIV); R: Rubella; C: Cytomegalovirus (CMV); He: Herpes simplex virus (HSV); S: Syphilis—a group of infections that are transmitted vertically through the placenta.
  20. 20Blueberry gemmed babies: CRS presents with purpuric skin lesions known as the ‘blueberry muffin rash' consist of purplish, non-blanching nodules caused by extramedullary hematopoiesis in response to fetal anemia.
  21. 21Golden babies: CRS can present with hyperbilirubinemia and jaundice due to fetal hepatocellular injury and cholestasis caused by in utero rubella infection.
  22. 22Empty eyes: CRS presents with congenital cataracts (other ocular findings include corneal clouding, glaucoma, and retinopathy) because in utero rubella infection disrupts normal development of the lens, cornea, anterior chamber, and retina.
  23. 23Covering ears: CRS presents with congenital bilateral sensorineural hearing loss due to rubella virus–induced damage to the developing cochlea and auditory neurons during fetal development.
  24. 24Tiny head: CRS presents with microcephaly (other neurological findings include meningoencephalitis, and developmental delays) due to rubella virus–mediated disruption of fetal brain development during early gestation.
  25. 25Aqueductus arteriosus: CRS presents with patent ductus arteriosus (other cardiac findings include branch pulmonary artery stenosis, aortic stenosis, ASD and VSD) due to rubella virus–mediated disruption of cardiac morphogenesis during early gestation.
  26. 26LIVE puppet show: Active immunity against rubella is achieved with a live, attenuated vaccine eliciting humoral and cell-mediated immune responses; rubella cases and CRS are rare in countries with routine MMR vaccination, but cases can occur in immigrants or travelers from regions with low vaccination coverage or in individuals who have not completed the vaccine series.
  27. 273 puppets + syringe sword: Measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) comprise a trivalent vaccine, while the quadrivalent version (MMRV) adds varicella; it is recommended for children aged 12 months and older, except in pregnant women and immunocompromised individuals.
  28. 28Pregnant puppet running away from syringe sword: MMR is contraindicated in pregnancy because it is a live attenuated vaccine that could theoretically infect the fetus and cause congenital rubella syndrome.
  29. 29Only 200 tickets: MMR is contraindicated in patients with significant immunodeficiency (e.g., HIV with CD4 <200) because the live attenuated vaccine virus could replicate uncontrolled, causing serious systemic infection.

97. Salmonella Intro to Salmonella & NTS

Exam snapshot
  • Gram-negative, motile (peritrichous flagella), facultative intracellular rod; H2S producer (black colonies on Hektoen agar), non-lactose fermenter.
  • NTS (S. Enteritidis, S. Typhimurium) acquired from poultry, eggs, and reptiles/amphibians (turtles).
  • Usual presentation: self-limited gastroenteritis (fever, cramps, diarrhea); no antibiotics, supportive care only.
  • Bacteremia risk and osteomyelitis in sickle cell disease (also young infants, elderly, immunocompromised): treat with fluoroquinolone or ceftriaxone.
  • Invasion via M cells of Peyer's patches; low gastric acid (PPIs, antacids) lowers the infectious dose.
  1. 1Red rod salmon: Salmonella is a gram-negative rod. It is a non-lactose fermenter in the Enterobacterales and ferments glucose.
  2. 2Swimming salmon: Salmonella is motile via peritrichous flagella (H antigen), unlike Shigella, which is non-motile.⭐ Salmonella motile, Shigella non-motile.
  3. 3Fishbowl capsule: Salmonella produces a protective polysaccharide capsule (as given in the Sketchy picture). The best-known capsule is the Vi antigen of S. Typhi, which helps evade host defenses; most NTS lack it.
  4. 4Open cage: Salmonella is a facultative intracellular organism: it can survive and replicate inside host macrophages as well as outside cells.
  5. 5Salmo crossing M window: Invasive serotypes (all typhoidal and some NTS) cross the intestinal barrier via enterocytes and M cells, the specialized epithelial cells overlying lymphoid tissue of Peyer's patches.
  6. 6Lemons: Salmonella is acid-labile, so gastric acid is a major host defense. Low-acid states (PPIs, antacids, achlorhydria) reduce the infectious dose.
  7. 7Farting salmon: Salmonella produces hydrogen sulfide (H2S), which gives black colonies on certain media (e.g., hydrogen sulfide-detecting agars).
  8. 8Black plates: H2S production gives black colonies on Hektoen enteric agar, where Salmonella is distinguished from Shigella (colorless/green colonies, no H2S).
  9. 9"Thai Food": Typhoidal Salmonella (TS) serotypes are S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi, which cause enteric (typhoid) fever.
  10. 10"Auntie Rita's" Turmeric: Nontyphoidal Salmonella (NTS) comprises the many serotypes other than S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi; S. Enteritidis and S. Typhimurium are the most common.
  11. 11Smorgasbord: NTS causes foodborne and waterborne illness, spread through a wide variety of fresh and processed foods.
  12. 12Chicken and eggs: NTS is zoonotic; poultry (chicken, eggs) are the primary reservoir, so undercooked poultry and raw eggs are classic sources.
  13. 13Turtle handler: Animal contact, especially reptiles and amphibians (e.g., pet turtles), can transmit NTS.
  14. 14Queasy kid with flame bandana: The most common presentation of NTS is self-limited gastroenteritis: fever, nausea, vomiting, abdominal cramps, and diarrhea (can be bloody and inflammatory).
  15. 15Red river: NTS can cause extraintestinal bloodstream infection (bacteremia), leading to severe sepsis or death, especially in the immunocompromised, infants, and elderly.
  16. 16Aorta tree: Bacteremic NTS can disseminate, causing endovascular infection (e.g., infected aortic aneurysm/atherosclerotic plaques), meningitis, and osteomyelitis.
  17. 17Glowing brain tree: Disseminated NTS can seed the CNS and cause meningitis, particularly in infants and immunocompromised patients.
  18. 18Fish bones: Disseminated NTS can seed bone and cause osteomyelitis.
  19. 19Sickling guy: Osteomyelitis is a classic complication of invasive NTS in patients with sickle cell disease (functional asplenia) and other underlying conditions.⭐ Sickle cell + osteomyelitis: Salmonella and S. aureus.
  20. 20"Don't touch" Abx: NTS gastroenteritis is self-limited; manage with supportive care and hydration. Antibiotics are generally not indicated, and may prolong carriage.
  21. 21Flowers: Fluoroquinolones are the preferred antibiotic for NTS when treatment is indicated (to prevent invasive disease in high-risk patients and to treat invasive infection).
  22. 223 axes: Third-generation cephalosporins (e.g., ceftriaxone) are an alternative for NTS, used to prevent invasive disease when indicated and to treat invasive infection, including in children.
Show the original legend picture

98. Salmonella Typhoidal Salmonella

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: traveler to South-Central/East Asia or Southern Africa with stepwise fever, relative bradycardia, rose spots, and abdominal pain.
  • S. Typhi/Paratyphi: humans are the only reservoir; fecal-oral spread; chronic gallbladder carriers (Typhoid Mary).
  • Vi capsule blunts neutrophil response; bacteria multiply inside macrophages of Peyer's patches.
  • Week 3 complications: sepsis, hepatosplenomegaly, intestinal bleeding, and perforation.
  • Treatment: antibiotics (typically a fluoroquinolone; ceftriaxone or azithromycin where resistance exists).
  • Vaccines: oral live attenuated (Ty21a), injectable Vi polysaccharide, and Vi-conjugate (to tetanus toxoid) vaccine.
  1. 1Thai Food sign: S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi are the typhoidal Salmonella (TS) serotypes; they cause enteric (typhoid) fever in humans.
  2. 2Parrot: S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi are the typhoidal Salmonella (TS) serotypes, which cause enteric (typhoid) fever.
  3. 3"No animals" sign: Humans are the exclusive reservoir of TS; there is no animal reservoir, unlike NTS.⭐ Humans only: TS vs. poultry/reptiles for NTS.
  4. 4Outhouse: TS is acquired by consuming food or water contaminated with human feces (fecal-oral route), so poor sanitation drives spread.
  5. 5Parrots in papaya tree: TS can colonize the gallbladder, leading to a chronic, asymptomatic carrier state that sheds bacteria in feces and perpetuates transmission.⭐ Gallstones raise carrier risk; chronic carriage predisposes to gallbladder cancer.
  6. 6Thai mountains: TS predominantly occurs in developing countries with poor sanitation; endemic regions include South-Central and East Asia and Southern Africa.
  7. 7Vi-olet punch: TS possesses the Vi polysaccharide capsular antigen, which helps resist host defenses by blunting the neutrophilic response (inhibits neutrophil chemotaxis, opsonization, and oxidative burst).
  8. 8MacroCAGE: TS triggers a monocytic rather than neutrophilic response: monocytes/macrophages are recruited to sites harboring the bacteria, such as Peyer's patches.
  9. 9Caged parrots: TS survives and extensively proliferates within macrophages, which carry the organism through the reticuloendothelial system.
  10. 10Flaming lanterns: Fever is a classic early sign of TS infection and the hallmark of enteric fever.
  11. 11Stepwise rock island: Enteric fever begins with a stepwise rising fever that reaches a high plateau, typically over the first week.
  12. 12Fallen heart watch: Relative bradycardia (pulse lower than expected for the degree of fever, Faget sign) is a characteristic feature of enteric fever, typically developing in the first week.
  13. 13Red algae: Flat, rose-colored macules ("rose spots") may develop on the chest and abdomen, typically during the second week. Constipation, headache, and abdominal pain are also common.
  14. 14Dugong with liver and spleen spots: If untreated, sepsis, hepatosplenomegaly, and intestinal bleeding and perforation may develop in the third week.
  15. 15Leaking intestine engine: Peyer's patch necrosis from the monocytic infiltrate causes intestinal bleeding and perforation in the third week of untreated enteric fever.⭐ Typhoid perforation typically involves the terminal ileum.
  16. 16Flowers: Enteric fever requires antibiotic treatment, typically a fluoroquinolone (ceftriaxone or azithromycin where resistance is common).
  17. 17"LIVE music" sign + syringe mic: A live attenuated oral vaccine (Ty21a) protects against enteric fever. It is contraindicated in immunocompromised patients and cannot be given with concurrent antibiotics.
  18. 18Skull and Syringe shirt + Vi-olet punch: The Vi capsular polysaccharide vaccine is given by injection to protect against enteric fever; it is a purified polysaccharide vaccine (T-cell independent).
  19. 19Rhesus + Vi-olet punch: The conjugate vaccine (Vi capsular polysaccharide linked to tetanus toxoid protein) is given by injection and gives more robust, T-cell dependent protection against enteric fever.
Show the original legend picture

99. Scabies, Lice, Crabs

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: intensely itchy rash worse at night with finger-web burrows (scabies); itching with nits in clothing seams (body lice) or pubic hair (crabs)
  • Sarcoptes scabiei is a mite; Pediculus humanus corporis is body louse; Phthirus pubis is pubic louse
  • Dx: skin scraping (mite, eggs, feces) for scabies; visualize lice or nits
  • Treatment: topical permethrin (plus oral ivermectin for scabies); nit comb for crabs; hygiene and hot-washing clothes for body lice
  • Buzzwords: burrows, nocturnal pruritus, Norwegian (crusted) scabies, nits, maculae ceruleae
  1. 1Scabbard: Scabies (Sarcoptes scabiei) is a tiny parasitic mite that infects only humans.
  2. 2Tunnel: Scabies mites burrow under the skin to lay eggs, leaving visible burrow lines; larvae hatch and travel to the skin surface to molt.
  3. 3Dorm setting: Scabies spreads by prolonged skin-to-skin contact and is seen in crowded households and institutions (nursing homes, prisons).⭐ Close contacts should be treated at the same time.
  4. 4Camp Itch 'n Scratch: Scabies causes intense pruritus with excoriations, worst at night, from a delayed (type IV) hypersensitivity reaction to the mites, their eggs and fecal matter.⭐ Itching may persist for weeks after successful treatment.
  5. 5Nighttime setting: Scabies itching is most pronounced at night, from delayed hypersensitivity to mites, eggs and feces.
  6. 6Red accessories: Scabies commonly involves skin folds: finger webs, wrists, elbows, knees, waistline and buttocks. In infants, the head, palms and soles are more commonly involved.⭐ Spares the face in adults.
  7. 7Triangle hat: Scabies is diagnosed by seeing the triangular head of a mite at the end of a burrow, irregular burrow lines, or excoriated erythematous papules; skin scraping shows mites, eggs or feces.
  8. 8Sandwich crusts + Norwegian flag: Crusted (Norwegian) scabies occurs with a very high mite load or immunocompromise, causing thick crusted scales and cracking.⭐ Highly contagious; seen in HIV and transplant patients.
  9. 9Camp counselor Ivan: Scabies can be treated with oral ivermectin and/or topical permethrin; Norwegian scabies needs both.
  10. 10suPER flaMETHRower: Topical permethrin (applied neck down and washed off after 8-14 hours) treats scabies, and is combined with oral ivermectin for Norwegian scabies.⭐ Permethrin is first line; ivermectin is useful in outbreaks or crusted disease.
  11. 11Corporal outfit: Pediculosis corporis (Pediculus humanus corporis), the body louse, lives in clothing, lays eggs in the seams and feeds on the skin.⭐ The louse lives in clothes, not on the body.
  12. 12Seam of fabric: Body lice (Pediculus humanus corporis) lay their eggs in clothing seams and feed on human skin.
  13. 13Camp Itch 'n Scratch: Lice infestation causes pruritus and excoriations from local bites.
  14. 14Bunkhouse + stinky teenager: Body lice are associated with crowded living conditions and poor hygiene; lice can transmit epidemic typhus (Rickettsia prowazekii), relapsing fever (Borrelia recurrentis) and trench fever (Bartonella quintana).
  15. 15"Don't forget to BATHE! <3 mom": Improved hygiene and heat-washing of clothing and bedding treat pediculosis corporis.
  16. 16suPER flaMETHRower: For severe infestations, or if nits are found in body hair, permethrin should be used.
  17. 17Crab shorts: Pediculosis pubis (Phthirus pubis), pubic lice or crabs, is a translucent gray insect with claws that grip coarse hair; the nit is attached to the base of the hair shaft; found in pubic, axillary and facial hair and eyelashes.
  18. 18Hair in comb: Pubic lice (Phthirus pubis) grip coarse hair with claws, and nits attach at the base of the hair shaft; they are found in pubic, axillary and facial hair and eyelashes.
  19. 19Kiss: Pubic lice (crabs) are most commonly transmitted by sexual contact.⭐ Eyelash infestation in a child raises concern for sexual abuse.
  20. 20Blue polka dots: Blue-gray discoloration of bite sites (maculae ceruleae) can occur because crab saliva contains an anticoagulant.
  21. 21Camp Itch 'n Scratch: Crab infestation causes pruritus and excoriations from local bites.
  22. 22Glasses on hair: Crab lice or nits can be seen with the naked eye; a magnifying glass or dermatoscope is helpful.
  23. 23Comb: Treatment of crabs includes a nit comb (to remove nits) and topical permethrin.
  24. 24suPER flaMETHRower again: Treatment of crabs includes a nit comb and topical permethrin; treat sexual partners and wash clothing and bedding.

100. Shigella spp

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: bloody (dysenteric) diarrhea with fever and abdominal cramps, with fecal leukocytes; very low infectious dose (10-100 organisms), spread person to person fecal-oral.
  • Gram-negative, non-motile, non-lactose-fermenting, non-H2S-producing rod (colorless/green colonies on Hektoen agar, unlike black Salmonella).
  • Invades M cells and spreads cell to cell via actin rockets; Type III secretion system injects effector proteins.
  • Shiga toxin (S. dysenteriae type 1) inactivates 60S ribosomal subunit (28S rRNA) and causes HUS (anemia, thrombocytopenia, AKI).
  • Treatment: fluoroquinolone or azithromycin (ceftriaxone), but mainly supportive; avoid antimotility drugs.
  • Buzzwords: day-care outbreaks, men who have sex with men, travelers, and HUS in children.
  1. 1She-gorilla: Shigella spp. are gram-negative bacilli and belong to the Enterobacterales; they are non-lactose fermenters.
  2. 2Red hues: Shigella spp. are gram-negative bacilli (red/pink on Gram stain).
  3. 3Green tutu: On Hektoen enteric agar, Shigella forms blue-green (colorless) colonies because it does not produce H2S. Salmonella makes black colonies on HE due to H2S.
  4. 4Chained she-gorilla: Shigella spp. are non-motile (no flagella), unlike Salmonella, which is motile.⭐ Shigella non-motile, Salmonella motile.
  5. 5Protected from acid: Shigella is relatively resistant to gastric acid, so very small inocula (10-100 organisms) can cause infection, which facilitates person-to-person spread.
  6. 6Motion tail: Shigella induces apoptosis in host cells and polymerizes host actin into tails ("actin rockets") to move through the cytoplasm and spread cell to cell, making it a facultative intracellular bacterium.
  7. 7"M" pad: Shigella invades M cells overlying Peyer's patches of the gut mucosa, then infects the colonic epithelium from the basolateral side.
  8. 8Red stool: The main sign of shigellosis is diarrhea, which is often bloody (dysentery), usually with fever and abdominal cramps.
  9. 9Inflammatory flames: Shigellosis diarrhea is inflammatory, with leukocytes (and often blood) in stool, reflecting invasion of the colonic mucosa.
  10. 10Glomerular knot: In rare cases, Shiga toxin from S. dysenteriae type 1 causes hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) by damaging endothelial cells of glomerular capillaries. This triggers platelet aggregation, causing thrombocytopenia, microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), and acute kidney injury.⭐ HUS triad: MAHA, thrombocytopenia, AKI.
  11. 11Popped red balloon: In MAHA (part of HUS), red blood cells are mechanically sheared into schistocytes as they pass through small vessels occluded by platelet-rich clots, lowering the RBC count.
  12. 12Aggregation of plates: In HUS, platelet aggregation in damaged glomerular capillaries consumes platelets (thrombocytopenia) and impairs renal function, causing acute kidney injury.
  13. 13Young ringmaster: HUS occurs more commonly in children under 5 years of age.
  14. 1460S sand timer: Shiga toxin of S. dysenteriae (similar to the Shiga-like toxin of enterohemorrhagic E. coli O157:H7) inactivates the 60S ribosomal subunit by removing an adenine from 28S rRNA, inhibiting protein synthesis.
  15. 15Turkey baster: Shigella has a type III secretion system, a needle-like apparatus that injects effector proteins into host cells, helping the bacteria invade, evade immune responses, and survive intracellularly.
Show the original legend picture

101. Smallpox / Cowpox virus

  1. 1Boxes: Poxviruses (e.g., smallpox virus, cowpox virus, molluscum contagiosum virus) are DNA viruses in the Poxviridae family.
  2. 2Cool colors: Poxviruses are large, complex DNA viruses.
  3. 3Workers preparing envelopes: Poxviruses acquire their envelope by wrapping themselves in multiple lipoprotein membranes derived from the host's intracellular membranes.
  4. 4Prepackaged boxes: Poxviruses pack all the enzymes needed for replication into each virion, bringing their own encoded machinery (e.g., DNA-dependent RNA polymerase).
  5. 5Conveyor belt: Poxviruses pack all the enzymes needed for replication into each virion, bringing their own encoded machinery (e.g., DNA-dependent RNA polymerase).
  6. 6Circled Headquarters (HQ) on map: Unlike most DNA viruses, which replicate within the host's nucleus, poxviruses exclusively replicate within the host's cytoplasm, using their own encoded enzymes to bypass the need for the host cell's nuclear machinery.
  7. 7Locations outside HQ: Unlike most DNA viruses, which replicate in the nucleus, poxviruses exclusively replicate within the host's cytoplasm, using their own encoded enzymes to bypass the need for the host cell's nuclear machinery.
  8. 8“Guarnieri's shipping”: Guarnieri bodies (type B inclusions) are cytoplasmic eosinophilic (pink) inclusion bodies seen in cells infected with poxviruses, especially smallpox (variola virus); they appear as round or oval, dense pink-staining structures under light microscopy and are diagnostic of active viral replication.
  9. 9Red “G's” outside HQ: Guarnieri bodies (type B inclusions) are cytoplasmic eosinophilic (reddish-pink) inclusion bodies seen in cells infected with poxviruses, especially smallpox (variola virus); they appear as round or oval, dense pink-staining structures under light microscopy and are diagnostic of active viral replication.
  10. 10Dumbbells: Poxviruses are brick- or oval-shaped viruses with a distinctive dumbbell-shaped core that contains the viral double-stranded DNA and enzymes, flanked by lateral bodies.
  11. 11“World's largest”: Poxviruses are the largest known DNA viruses.
  12. 12Small boxes: Smallpox virus (or variola virus, a DNA virus in the Poxviridae family) causes smallpox, a febrile illness with a synchronous pustular rash, and is transmitted via respiratory droplets and direct contact with lesion material.
  13. 13“Same day shipping”: Smallpox lesions are deep, firm, well-circumscribed, and often uniform in size and stage, typically appearing on the face and extremities; in contrast, chickenpox lesions are superficial, vesicular, and appear in crops, so lesions of different stages (macules, papules, vesicles, crusts) are present simultaneously.
  14. 14Cowpox: Cowpox virus (a DNA virus in the Poxviridae family) causes pustular skin lesions, often on the hands or arms, with possible fever and lymphadenopathy, transmitted by contact with infected animals.
  15. 15Material from udder dispenser: Cowpox in humans historically resulted from contact with infected cow udders; transmission from domestic cats has become more common in recent years.
  16. 16Snail: Molluscum contagiosum virus (a DNA virus in the Poxviridae family) causes small, firm, umbilicated, flesh-colored papules on the skin, transmitted by direct contact or via contaminated objects.
  17. 17“Snail mail” + dome shell: Molluscum contagiosum lesions are flesh-colored, dome-shaped, smooth papules (2–5 mm) with a characteristic central umbilication; they are firm, painless, may appear in clusters, and commonly occur on the trunk, limbs (especially the antecubital and popliteal fossae), and genital area.
  18. 18Kid with snail stamps on trunk: Children with molluscum contagiosum commonly exhibit clustered, umbilicated skin lesions, predominantly on the trunk and limbs, typically acquired through direct skin-to-skin contact with an infected person or fomites (e.g., towels, clothing, toys).
  19. 19Adult with immunocompromised care: In adults, widespread or persistent molluscum contagiosum lesions may indicate underlying immunodeficiency (e.g., HIV), and the virus is typically acquired through direct skin-to-skin contact or sexual transmission.

102. Sporothrix schenckii

Exam snapshot
  • Rose gardener with a thorn prick developing nodules and ulcers that ascend the arm along lymphatics.
  • Thermally dimorphic fungus: mold at 25-30 C, yeast at 35-37 C; found in soil, plants, thorns, sphagnum moss.
  • Biopsy or culture; yeast phase shows cigar-shaped budding yeast; mold phase shows rosette (daisy-like) conidia.
  • Itraconazole for cutaneous and lymphocutaneous disease; amphotericin B for severe or disseminated disease; SSKI is historical.
  • Rose gardener's disease, lymphocutaneous spread, cigar-shaped yeast, rosette conidia.
  1. 1Shanked by a thorn: Sporothrix schenckii is a dimorphic fungus that causes cutaneous (subcutaneous) mycosis after traumatic inoculation.
  2. 2Garden: S. schenckii lives in soil, plant debris, tree bark, and thorny vegetation such as rose bushes and sphagnum moss.
  3. 3Dimorphic butterfly: S. schenckii is thermally dimorphic: mold in cooler environments (25-30 C) and yeast at body temperature (35-37 C).⭐ Mold in the cold, yeast in the heat.
  4. 4Roses: Sporotrichosis is called "rose gardener's disease" because infection follows a prick from thorns or other contaminated plant material.⭐ Rose gardener's disease.
  5. 5Branching rose stems: The mold phase has septate, branching hyphae with small round conidia in rosette or daisy-like clusters at the tips of conidiophores (spore-bearing stalks).
  6. 6Cigar: The yeast phase appears as small oval to elongated, cigar-shaped yeast cells with narrow-based budding, often surrounded by histiocytes, multinucleated giant cells, and granulomatous inflammation.⭐ Cigar-shaped yeast.
  7. 7Thorny vine around arm: Sporotrichosis typically follows traumatic inoculation of contaminated plant matter or soil, causing subcutaneous nodules and ulcers.
  8. 8Ascending vines: In lymphocutaneous sporotrichosis, the fungus ascends from the inoculation site along lymphatics, forming sequential subcutaneous nodules that may enlarge, become tender, and ulcerate, usually on the hands or forearms.⭐ Ascending nodular lymphangitis after a thorn prick.
  9. 9Roses on vines on arm: Same point: nodules arranged along the lymphatic channel of the arm (lymphocutaneous sporotrichosis).
  10. 10Pinecones: Oral itraconazole is the preferred treatment for cutaneous and lymphocutaneous sporotrichosis.⭐ Severe or disseminated disease needs amphotericin B.
  11. 11Potassium iodide pesticide: A saturated solution of potassium iodide (SSKI) was historically used for sporotrichosis; modern antifungals (itraconazole) are now preferred.
Show the original legend picture

103. Staphylococcus aureus

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: abscesses, impetigo, post-influenza pneumonia, acute endocarditis (IVDU, tricuspid), septic arthritis and osteomyelitis
  • Gram-positive cocci in clusters; catalase +, coagulase + (distinguishes from S. epidermidis/saprophyticus), golden pigment, beta-hemolytic, ferments mannitol
  • Toxins: TSST-1 (superantigen, toxic shock syndrome), exfoliative toxin (scalded skin), preformed enterotoxin (food poisoning in 1-6 h)
  • Protein A binds Fc of IgG, blocking opsonization and phagocytosis; nasal carriage is the reservoir
  • Treatment: nafcillin/oxacillin for MSSA; vancomycin for MRSA (altered PBP, mecA gene)
  1. 1Golden staff: Staphylococcus aureus is a gram-positive coccus that grows in grape-like clusters; its colonies are golden because of staphyloxanthin pigment.
  2. 2Purple robe: S. aureus is a gram-positive coccus (purple on Gram stain), seen in clusters.
  3. 3Catalase cat: S. aureus is catalase positive: catalase converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen (bubbles on testing). Staphylococci are catalase +, whereas streptococci are catalase -.
  4. 4Coagulated river: S. aureus is coagulase positive: coagulase converts fibrinogen to fibrin, forming a clot in plasma. This distinguishes it from other catalase-positive, coagulase-negative staphylococci (S. epidermidis, S. saprophyticus).⭐ Coagulase + = S. aureus.
  5. 5Beta light bulb: Most strains are beta-hemolytic: complete lysis of red cells gives a clear zone around colonies on blood agar.
  6. 6Tall man in yellow: S. aureus ferments mannitol, turning mannitol salt agar yellow. The high-salt medium selects for staphylococci, and mannitol fermentation distinguishes S. aureus from non-pathogenic staphylococci, which usually do not ferment mannitol.
  7. 7"A" bound to IgG: Protein A is a key virulence factor that binds the Fc region of IgG, which prevents opsonization, complement activation, and phagocytosis.
  8. 8Nose: S. aureus is normal flora of the anterior nares and skin; nasal carriage is a source of autoinfection and of spread (e.g., in healthcare workers).
  9. 9Coughing + patchy cloth: S. aureus pneumonia shows patchy infiltrates on chest X-ray that can progress to lobar consolidation, and may cause abscesses, cavities, or empyema.
  10. 10Icosahedron viral lanterns: S. aureus pneumonia classically follows a viral respiratory infection, especially influenza (viral damage to the airway epithelium predisposes to bacterial superinfection).⭐ Post-influenza pneumonia: S. aureus (also S. pneumoniae).
  11. 11Joint bandages: S. aureus is the most common cause of septic arthritis in adults (N. gonorrhoeae is common in sexually active young adults). It presents as an acutely warm, swollen, tender joint, with purulent synovial fluid on aspiration.
  12. 12Inflamed camel humps: S. aureus causes skin and soft tissue infections: abscesses, impetigo, folliculitis, furuncles, carbuncles, and cellulitis.
  13. 13Hearts on running camel: S. aureus is the most common cause of acute infective endocarditis, with rapid onset of fever, chills, tachycardia, and sepsis, and it can infect previously normal valves because of its virulence.
  14. 14Three pyramids + mortar and pestle: Tricuspid valve endocarditis caused by S. aureus is strongly associated with IV drug use. It causes septic pulmonary emboli (multiple lung nodules/cavitations).⭐ IVDU + endocarditis = tricuspid valve, S. aureus.
  15. 15Fish bones: S. aureus is the leading cause of osteomyelitis (bone infection), commonly from hematogenous spread or direct inoculation. (Salmonella is classic in sickle cell disease.)
  16. 16Exfoliative burn: Exfoliative toxin (a protease) cleaves desmoglein-1 in the stratum granulosum, causing staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome with widespread skin peeling, mostly in infants and young children. Nikolsky sign is positive.
  17. 17Superhero cape + bolt: TSST-1 is a superantigen that cross-links MHC II on antigen-presenting cells with the T-cell receptor outside the antigen groove, causing massive polyclonal T-cell activation and cytokine release. It causes toxic shock syndrome: high fever, diffuse desquamating rash, hypotension/shock, and multi-organ involvement (classically tampon use or surgical packing).
  18. 18Rapid camel running + nausea: Ingestion of preformed, heat-stable enterotoxin causes food poisoning with rapid onset (Sketchy: ~30 min to 8 h; classically 1-6 h): abrupt nausea, vomiting, and cramps. It is self-limited and non-febrile, and there is usually little or no diarrhea.
  19. 19Mayo and meats: Foods contaminated by food handlers and left at room temperature, such as mayonnaise-based dishes, custards, and meats, are classic sources of staphylococcal food poisoning. Reheating does not help because the toxin is heat-stable.⭐ Short incubation + mayo/custard/potato salad = S. aureus.
  20. 20Merciful pharaoh: Some strains are methicillin-resistant (MRSA), which is resistant to all beta-lactams except newer anti-MRSA cephalosporins. MRSA is a major cause of both hospital- and community-acquired infection.
  21. 21Altered builders: MRSA carries the mecA gene, which encodes an altered penicillin-binding protein (PBP2a) with low affinity for beta-lactams, so cell-wall synthesis proceeds despite the drug.
  22. 22Purple caravan: Vancomycin is the drug of choice for serious MRSA infections. Alternatives include linezolid, daptomycin (not for pneumonia), and ceftaroline.
  23. 23Pencil staff: Nafcillin (or oxacillin, dicloxacillin) is a penicillinase-resistant beta-lactam used for methicillin-sensitive S. aureus; it is effective because it resists staphylococcal beta-lactamase.
Show the original legend picture

104. Staphylococcus epidermidis & saprophyticus

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: S. epidermidis - prosthetic valve/joint or catheter infection; S. saprophyticus - UTI in a young sexually active woman.
  • Micro: gram-positive cocci in clusters, catalase positive, coagulase negative; S. epidermidis is novobiocin sensitive, S. saprophyticus is novobiocin resistant.
  • Diagnosis: culture; repeated positive blood cultures for epidermidis; urinalysis and culture for saprophyticus.
  • Treatment/prevention: S. epidermidis - vancomycin (often methicillin resistant), remove device; S. saprophyticus - TMP-SMX or nitrofurantoin.
  • Buzzwords: biofilm, blood culture contaminant, honeymoon cystitis, novobiocin, coagulase negative.
  1. 1Plumber: Staphylococcus epidermidis is a gram-positive coccus.
  2. 2Sappy couple: Staphylococcus saprophyticus is a gram-positive coccus.
  3. 3Purple hues: S. epidermidis and S. saprophyticus are gram-positive cocci (purple = gram-positive).
  4. 4Catalase cat: Both produce catalase (catalase positive), an enzyme that converts hydrogen peroxide to water and oxygen.⭐ Catalase positive separates staphylococci from streptococci.
  5. 5Runny juice: Both are coagulase negative (do not convert fibrinogen to fibrin, so no clot); the coagulase test distinguishes them from coagulase-positive S. aureus.⭐ They are the "coagulase-negative staphylococci".
  6. 6Ammonia spray: Both produce urease (urease positive), hydrolyzing urea into carbon dioxide and ammonia.
  7. 7Plumber's exposed navel: S. epidermidis is susceptible to novobiocin, whereas S. saprophyticus is resistant.⭐ Saprophyticus = resistant ("Sapro = Stubborn to novobiocin").
  8. 8Dirty hands: S. epidermidis is part of the normal flora of skin and mucous membranes.
  9. 9Petri dish pan: As normal skin flora, S. epidermidis is a common contaminant of blood cultures.⭐ Multiple positive cultures suggest true infection rather than contamination.
  10. 10Plumbing joints: S. epidermidis is one of the most frequently isolated pathogens in prosthetic joint infections.
  11. 11Tubing: S. epidermidis is a leading cause of catheter-associated (intravascular device) infections.
  12. 12Heart valve: S. epidermidis is the most common cause of prosthetic valve endocarditis (early, within about 2 months of surgery).
  13. 13Gunk on pipes and tubes: S. epidermidis produces biofilm (extracellular polysaccharide slime), allowing adherence to prosthetic devices and IV catheters.⭐ Biofilm is the key virulence factor and protects against antibiotics.
  14. 14Van: Vancomycin is the drug of choice for S. epidermidis because most strains are resistant to nafcillin and methicillin (mecA); biofilm formation further impairs antibiotic penetration.⭐ Often requires device removal as well.
  15. 15Honeymooner: S. saprophyticus commonly causes uncomplicated urinary tract infection in young, sexually active women (honeymoon cystitis).⭐ Second most common cause of UTI in this group.
  16. 16Bladder cup: S. saprophyticus is an important cause of uncomplicated UTI, second after E. coli (E. coli and Klebsiella are also frequent causes).⭐ Suspect in a UTI that is nitrite negative, urease positive with alkaline urine.
  17. 17Inflamed prostate fruit: S. saprophyticus has been reported as a cause of acute bacterial prostatitis (uncommon; E. coli is far more common).
Show the original legend picture

105. Streptococcus agalactiae (Group B Strep)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: neonate with sepsis, pneumonia or meningitis in the first days to weeks of life; mother colonized in the vagina or rectum.
  • Micro: gram-positive cocci in chains, beta-hemolytic, group B antigen, encapsulated; bacitracin resistant, PYR negative, hippurate positive, CAMP positive.
  • Diagnosis: CAMP test, hippurate hydrolysis; vaginal-rectal culture at 36-37+6 weeks.
  • Treatment/prevention: intrapartum IV penicillin (or ampicillin) prophylaxis for colonized mothers.
  • Buzzwords: neonatal meningitis and sepsis, CAMP factor, arrowhead hemolysis, hippurate positive, bacitracin resistant.
  1. 1Galactic baby: Streptococcus agalactiae (group B strep) is a gram-positive coccus.
  2. 2Purple hues: S. agalactiae is a gram-positive coccus (purple = gram-positive).
  3. 3Hippo: S. agalactiae is hippurate hydrolysis positive; this helps differentiate it from other streptococci such as S. pyogenes, which is hippurate negative.
  4. 4Glass helmet: S. agalactiae is surrounded by a polysaccharide capsule, an important virulence factor.⭐ The capsule helps evade phagocytosis in neonates.
  5. 5CAMP tent + golden staff: When S. agalactiae and S. aureus are grown near each other on blood agar, the CAMP factor produced by S. agalactiae enhances the hemolysis caused by S. aureus, producing an "arrowhead" zone of hemolysis; the CAMP test differentiates S. agalactiae from other streptococci.⭐ CAMP positive = group B strep.
  6. 6Beta light bulb: S. agalactiae is beta-hemolytic (complete lysis of red cells, clear zones around colonies on blood agar).
  7. 7Inhibited basset hound: S. agalactiae is bacitracin resistant and PYR negative, distinguishing it from S. pyogenes (bacitracin sensitive, PYR positive).⭐ Group A = Bacitracin sensitive; group B = Bacitracin resistant.
  8. 8Meningitis helmet: S. agalactiae is a leading cause of neonatal bacterial meningitis.
  9. 9Red onesie: S. agalactiae is one of the most common causes of neonatal sepsis.
  10. 10Coughing baby: Infected neonates can present with pneumonia (respiratory distress soon after birth).
  11. 11Red arrow: The CAMP test produces a characteristic arrowhead zone of enhanced hemolysis where S. agalactiae meets S. aureus on blood agar; used to identify S. agalactiae.
  12. 12"35 wk": Routine screening for S. agalactiae colonization is recommended for pregnant women at 36-37 weeks of gestation (older teaching: 35-37 weeks) because it can pass to the neonate in the birth canal.⭐ Screen with a vaginal-rectal swab culture.
  13. 13Purple pencil in mothership: If screening is positive, intravenous penicillin is given intrapartum to prevent neonatal infection.⭐ Penicillin G (or ampicillin) is the standard intrapartum prophylaxis.
Show the original legend picture

106. Streptococcus gallolyticus (Group D Strep)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: older adult with subacute endocarditis or bacteremia; underlying colon cancer or polyps.
  • Micro: gram-positive cocci in chains or pairs, group D, bile tolerant, nonhemolytic (gamma), formerly S. bovis.
  • Diagnosis: blood cultures; colonoscopy is required after S. gallolyticus bacteremia or endocarditis.
  • Treatment/prevention: penicillin, ceftriaxone or vancomycin; evaluate for colorectal neoplasia.
  • Buzzwords: S. bovis, colon cancer, endocarditis, bile esculin, group D.
  1. 1Galloping cow: Streptococcus bovis/equinus complex (SBSEC) has been reclassified, with S. gallolyticus (formerly S. bovis biotype I) the clinically important member.
  2. 2Big D ears: S. gallolyticus is a group D streptococcus.
  3. 3Purple moon: S. gallolyticus is a gram-positive coccus (purple = gram-positive).
  4. 4Chains & pairs of planets: S. gallolyticus appears as gram-positive cocci in chains or pairs.
  5. 5Glass helmet: S. gallolyticus is encapsulated.
  6. 6Gamma aurora: S. gallolyticus is gamma hemolytic (nonhemolytic, no hemolysis on blood agar; it still grows).⭐ Nonhemolytic, unlike group A and B strep.
  7. 7Liver + bile asteroid: S. gallolyticus grows in bile salt media (bile tolerant, as in bile esculin agar), which aids survival in the GI tract and lab identification.⭐ Group D strep: bile esculin positive; Enterococcus also grows in 6.5% NaCl, S. gallolyticus does not.
  8. 8Moon moss: S. gallolyticus forms biofilms on collagen-rich surfaces such as damaged heart valves.
  9. 9Colon milky way: S. gallolyticus is part of the normal GI flora of some animals and humans.
  10. 10Heart lantern: S. gallolyticus can cause bacteremia and endocarditis.⭐ Endocarditis with S. gallolyticus should prompt a colonoscopy.
  11. 11Crab constellation: There is a strong association between S. gallolyticus infection and colorectal neoplasia (colon cancer).⭐ Bacteremia with S. bovis = look for colon cancer.
  12. 12Squid: S. gallolyticus is sensitive to third-generation cephalosporins (e.g. ceftriaxone), vancomycin and penicillin.
  13. 13Van: Vancomycin is an effective treatment (S. gallolyticus is sensitive to it, as to penicillin and ceftriaxone).
  14. 14Purple pencil rocket: Penicillin (PCN) is an effective treatment for S. gallolyticus, which is also sensitive to third-generation cephalosporins and vancomycin.
