107. Streptococcus pneumoniae

  1. 1Sir Pneumo: Streptococcus pneumoniae is a gram-positive coccus.
  2. 2Purple hues: Streptococcus pneumoniae and viridans group streptococci are gram-positive cocci.
  3. 3"Alpha knight tournament": Streptococcus pneumoniae and viridans streptococci demonstrate alpha hemolysis, the partial breakdown of hemoglobin producing a greenish discoloration around colonies on blood agar.
  4. 4Sir Pneumo's armor: Streptococcus pneumoniae bacteria are surrounded by a polysaccharide capsule.
  5. 5Sensitive chin: Streptococcus pneumoniae is distinguished from viridans group streptococci by its sensitivity to optochin, an antibiotic used to identify alpha-hemolytic streptococci.
  6. 6Double lances: Streptococcus pneumoniae bacteria appear as lancet-shaped diplococci on microscopy.
  7. 7No bile boots: Streptococcus pneumoniae is bile-soluble (i.e., cannot grow in bile), demonstrated by the lysis and disappearance of its colonies on agar after the addition of bile salts.
  8. 8One-sided rusty chest armor: A possible clinical manifestation of Streptococcus pneumoniae infection is lobar pneumonia, which is often associated with rust-colored sputum.
  9. 9MOPS: Streptococcus pneumoniae is a leading cause of meningitis, otitis media, pneumonia, and sinusitis, remembered by the acronym MOPS.
  10. 10#1 sign: Streptococcus pneumoniae is a leading cause of meningitis, otitis media, pneumonia, and sinusitis (MOPS).
  11. 11IgA shield: The virulence factor IgA protease, produced by Streptococcus pneumoniae, cleaves IgA antibodies, promoting bacterial adherence to mucosal surfaces.
  12. 12Sickle: Patients with sickle cell disease have increased susceptibility to infections by encapsulated bacteria, such as Streptococcus pneumoniae.
  13. 13Macrolide crow: While penicillin or amoxicillin are first-line therapies against Streptococcus pneumoniae, macrolides such as azithromycin and clarithromycin can be used as alternative therapies.
  14. 143 axes flag: While penicillin or amoxicillin are first-line therapies against Streptococcus pneumoniae, ceftriaxone may be used to treat pneumococci with reduced susceptibility to penicillins.
  15. 15"Adults in the Mezzanine, Children on the Ground" vaccine sign: The CDC recommends pneumococcal conjugate vaccines (PCV) for young children, certain at-risk individuals, and seniors without prior vaccination. There are two pneumococcal vaccines: PPSV23 (a polysaccharide vaccine used in adults), which elicits a T-cell-independent response producing short-lived IgM with little immune memory, and PCV (a conjugate vaccine used in children), which links polysaccharide to a protein carrier, generating a T-cell-dependent response with stronger IgG production and long-lasting immunity, making it effective in children.
  16. 16Green jester: Viridans group streptococci are gram-positive cocci.
  17. 17No armor: Viridans group streptococci are non-encapsulated.
  18. 18Resistant chin: Viridans group streptococci are distinguished from Streptococcus pneumoniae by their resistance to optochin, an antibiotic used to identify alpha-hemolytic streptococci.
  19. 19Protective bile boots: Viridans group streptococci are bile-insoluble, indicated by the persistence of their colonies on agar after the addition of bile acids.
  20. 20Yellow teeth: Streptococcus mutans, part of the viridans group streptococci, is part of the normal oral flora and plays a role in tooth decay and dental caries.
  21. 21Damaged mitre hat: Viridans group streptococci are a common cause of infective endocarditis in patients with mitral valve disease.
  22. 22Plate shield: Viridans group streptococci utilize dextrans (insoluble extracellular polysaccharides) to adhere to tooth enamel and to fibrin-platelet aggregates on damaged heart valves, potentially leading to dental plaque and infective endocarditis, respectively.
  23. 23Deck of cards: Viridans group streptococci utilize dextrans (insoluble extracellular polysaccharides) to adhere to tooth enamel and to fibrin-platelet aggregates on damaged heart valves, potentially leading to dental plaque and infective endocarditis, respectively.

108. Streptococcus pyogenes

  1. 1Pie bakers: Streptococcus pyogenes (group A strep) is a gram-positive coccus.
  2. 2Purple stripes: Streptococcus pyogenes is a gram-positive coccus.
  3. 3“Hot Apple” pie + glass capsule: Streptococcus pyogenes produces a hyaluronic acid capsule, a critical virulence factor that helps it evade host immune defenses.
  4. 4Beta light bulb: Streptococcus pyogenes demonstrates beta-hemolysis, which refers to the ability to completely lyse hemoglobin → clear, transparent zones around colonies on blood agar.
  5. 5Flaky pie crust: Streptococcus pyogenes can manifest as impetigo, a superficial skin condition that often presents with “honey-crusted” lesions around the nose and mouth.
  6. 6Red neckerchief on pie chef: Streptococcus pyogenes is a significant cause of pharyngitis, often referred to as “strep throat.”
  7. 7Red puffy gloves: Streptococcus pyogenes is one of the most common causes of skin infections, including impetigo, cellulitis, erysipelas, and necrotizing fasciitis.
  8. 8Licking strawberry: Scarlet fever caused by pyrogenic exotoxin-producing Streptococcus pyogenes is characterized by symptoms such as strawberry tongue (a red and swollen tongue caused by the outermost layer of the tongue's surface peeling off), pharyngitis, and a desquamative “sandpaper” rash that typically spares the palms and soles.
  9. 9Red frosting + hot on hands and feet: Scarlet fever caused by pyrogenic exotoxin-producing Streptococcus pyogenes is characterized by symptoms such as strawberry tongue, pharyngitis, and a desquamative “sandpaper” rash that typically spares the palms and soles.
  10. 10Superhero cape + bolt: The pyrogenic exotoxins expressed by Streptococcus pyogenes are superantigens that are associated with toxic shock syndrome (TSS) may present with fever, rash, hypotension.
  11. 11“B2” + necrotic cookie: Some strains of Streptococcus pyogenes produce SpeB, a cysteine protease that contributes to the rapid tissue destruction seen in necrotizing fasciitis.
  12. 12“M” on hat: The M protein is a virulence factor expressed on the surface of Streptococcus pyogenes and plays multiple roles in pathogenesis.
  13. 13Mitre hat: M protein on the surface of Streptococcus pyogenes bacteria shares structural similarities with human proteins found in the heart and other tissues → rheumatic heart disease (the mitral valve is most commonly affected).
  14. 14Inhibiting eating: The M protein of Streptococcus pyogenes confers resistance to phagocytosis.
  15. 15Crumbs on face + red neckerchief: Both pharyngitis and soft tissue infections caused by Streptococcus pyogenes can precede PSGN.
  16. 16Red neckerchief on master chef: Rheumatic fever is a possible complication of untreated Streptococcus pyogenes pharyngitis.
  17. 17“JONES” cupcakes: The JONES criteria is used for diagnosing acute rheumatic fever; J = joints, O(with a heart symbol) = myocarditis, N = nodules, E = erythema marginatum, S = Sydenham chorea.
  18. 18“J” + elbow frosting: Rheumatic fever often presents with migratory polyarthritis, impacting larger joints such as the knees and elbows.
  19. 19“O(heart)” + ring pattern: Acute rheumatic fever can result in pancarditis, affecting the pericardium, myocardium, and endocardium, with the latter primarily impacting the heart valves, especially the mitral valve.
  20. 20“N” + nodular candies: In acute rheumatic fever, subcutaneous nodules may be found, typically on joint extensor surfaces like the elbows and forearms.
  21. 21“E” + ring pattern: In acute rheumatic fever, erythema marginatum can manifest as a non-itchy, ring-shaped rash, primarily appearing on the trunk and limbs.
  22. 22“S” cupcake falling: Acute rheumatic fever can lead to Sydenham chorea, a neurological manifestation characterized by sudden, involuntary, and purposeless movements primarily affecting the face, hands, and feet, often accompanied by emotional instability and muscle weakness; this symptom can emerge weeks to months after the initial streptococcal infection.
  23. 23Glomerular knot: Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (PSGN) arises from immune complex deposition in the glomeruli, typically presenting with features of nephritic syndrome (hematuria, oliguria, hypertension, edema) when symptomatic.
  24. 24Cola: Acute PSGN cases may present with gross hematuria, frequently characterized as tea-like, cola-hued, or rust-colored urine.
  25. 25Later in the month: PSGN typically manifests as nephritic syndrome a few weeks following an acute streptococcal pharyngitis.
  26. 26Crumbs on face + red neckerchief: Both pharyngitis and soft tissue infections caused by Streptococcus pyogenes can precede PSGN.
  27. 27Purple pencil: Early antibiotic treatment, particularly of penicillin, of Streptococcus pyogenes pharyngitis significantly reduces the risk of rheumatic fever; PSGN can still occur after an episode of either streptococcal pharyngitis or skin infection even if the initial infection was appropriately treated.
  28. 28Lysed “O” doughnuts: Streptolysin O is a Streptococcus pyogenes virulence factor that functions as a hemolytic toxin, enabling the bacteria to lyse and damage host red blood cells and immune cells, thereby aiding in evasion of the host immune response.
  29. 29“P” cupcakes: Streptokinase, a Streptococcus pyogenes virulence factor (despite the name, it does not act like typical kinases that add phosphate groups), is a proteolytic enzyme that facilitates fibrin degradation by converting plasminogen to plasmin → invasion of deeper tissues.
  30. 30DNA twists: DNases of Streptococcus pyogenes facilitate the degradation of host DNA, assisting the bacteria in evading host immune defenses.
  31. 31Basset licking pastries: Streptococcus pyogenes is susceptible to bacitracin and pyrrolidonyl arylamidase (PYR) test-positive, distinguishing it from Streptococcus agalactiae, which is bacitracin-resistant and PYR test-negative.
  32. 32“O” doughnuts + antibody tongs: Antistreptolysin O titers are useful in diagnosing conditions like acute rheumatic fever or PSGN that arise after a streptococcal pharyngeal infection, whereas anti-DNase titers might be less responsive, clinicians often measure anti-DNase titers to enhance diagnostic sensitivity.

109. Tissue Nematodes (Dracunculus, Wuchereria, Onchocerca, Toxocara, Loa loa)

Exam snapshot
  • Clues: Dracunculus = painful leg ulcer with emerging worm; Onchocerca = river blindness plus itchy skin; Wuchereria = elephantiasis; Loa loa = Calabar swellings and worm in eye; Toxocara = visceral/ocular larva migrans.
  • All are tissue nematodes with eosinophilia; vectors: copepods (Dracunculus), black fly (Onchocerca), mosquito (Wuchereria), deer fly (Loa), dog/cat feces eggs (Toxocara).
  • Diagnosis: skin snip (Onchocerca), blood smear (Wuchereria, Loa), adult worm emergence (Dracunculus), serology/clinical (Toxocara).
  • Treatment: ivermectin (Onchocerca), diethylcarbamazine (Wuchereria, Loa), albendazole (Toxocara), supportive worm extraction (Dracunculus).
  • Buzzwords: Guinea worm, river blindness, leopard skin, elephantiasis, Calabar swelling, ocular larva migrans.
  1. 1Dracula: Dracunculus medinensis (Guinea worm), a tissue nematode.
  2. 2Water cooler: Infection occurs when humans drink water containing copepods (small crustaceans) that have ingested D. medinensis larvae.⭐ Prevention is by filtering drinking water.
  3. 3Small cups: Copepods serve as the intermediate hosts, harboring infective larvae.
  4. 4Shoelace emerging from foot: The adult female worm emerges through a painful skin ulcer, typically on the lower extremity, when the limb contacts water.⭐ Worm emerges from a blistering leg ulcer.
  5. 5Wrapped shoelace: Traditional treatment is slow extraction by winding the emerging worm around a stick over days to weeks.⭐ There is no effective drug; pulling too fast breaks the worm.
  6. 6Bilobed slingshot kid + pink granules by Dracula: Eosinophilia is a common finding in D. medinensis infection (bilobed eosinophils with bright red-orange granules), especially during larval migration.
  7. 7Fly guy: Onchocerca volvulus causes onchocerciasis (river blindness); an infected black fly (Simulium) bite injects larvae that mature into adults in the skin and release microfilariae.⭐ Black fly breeds near fast-flowing rivers.
  8. 8Covering eyes: Microfilariae in ocular tissues can cause visual impairment and ultimately blindness, a severe complication of onchocerciasis.⭐ Onchocerciasis is a leading infectious cause of blindness worldwide.
  9. 9Pink granules + fly guy: Eosinophilia is a common finding in Onchocerca volvulus infection.
  10. 10Light and dark spots: Onchodermatitis: inflammation from dying microfilariae causes pruritic dermatitis with hyperpigmented and hypopigmented patches ('leopard skin').
  11. 11Microscope: Diagnosis is by finding Onchocerca microfilariae in a skin snip (biopsy) under the microscope.⭐ Skin snip, not blood smear, is used for Onchocerca.
  12. 12No dumping, drains to river: Ivermectin is the treatment of choice for onchocerciasis, killing microfilariae.⭐ Ivermectin kills microfilariae, not adults, so repeated dosing is needed; DEC can precipitate severe reactions (Mazzotti).
  13. 13Witch: Wuchereria bancrofti, a tissue (filarial) nematode.
  14. 14Hammer pants: W. bancrofti causes lymphatic filariasis: adult worms block lymphatic vessels, causing impaired drainage, chronic inflammation, and fibrosis, leading to elephantiasis (massive lymphedema of limbs and genitals).
  15. 15Sleeves and collar ruffles: Infection can cause lymphangitis and painful lymphadenopathy from inflammation of lymphatic vessels and nodes.
  16. 16Witch coughing: Tropical pulmonary eosinophilia is a hypersensitivity reaction to microfilariae in the lungs, with nocturnal cough, wheezing, dyspnea, and marked eosinophilia.⭐ Nocturnal cough with high eosinophilia in an endemic area.
  17. 17Mosquitoes: A variety of mosquitoes (Culex, Aedes, Anopheles) transmit Wuchereria bancrofti.
  18. 18Blood smear + witch: Diagnosis is confirmed by finding microfilariae on a thick blood smear, ideally drawn at night.⭐ Microfilariae show nocturnal periodicity.
  19. 19Slingshot granules + witch: Eosinophilia is a common finding in W. bancrofti infection.
  20. 20"Diet and Carb" + witch: Diethylcarbamazine is effective against W. bancrofti, targeting microfilariae and adult worms.⭐ Doxycycline (against Wolbachia) is also used.
  21. 21Wolfman: Toxocara canis, a tissue nematode that causes toxocariasis (visceral and ocular larva migrans).
  22. 22Poop bag: Infection is acquired by ingesting food or soil contaminated with eggs from dog (or cat) feces.⭐ Think of a toddler eating dirt or a child with puppy exposure.
  23. 23Sleep mask: Ocular larva migrans results from larval migration to the eye, causing significant visual impairment or blindness.⭐ A retinal granuloma can mimic retinoblastoma.
  24. 24Slingshot granules + wolfman: Eosinophilia is a common finding in Toxocara infection, especially visceral larva migrans.
  25. 25Bent chair legs + wolfman: Albendazole treats visceral toxocariasis; ocular toxocariasis is difficult to treat and management aims to prevent further eye damage (e.g., corticosteroids).
  26. 26Lagoon swamp creature: Loa loa, a tissue (filarial) nematode (African eye worm).
  27. 27Lumps on arms and legs: Loa loa causes recurrent Calabar swellings: transient, soft, slightly red, itchy, migratory subcutaneous swellings, mainly on the extremities.⭐ Calabar swellings are transient and migratory.
  28. 28Worm in eye: Migration of the adult worm across the conjunctiva is a hallmark clinical feature.⭐ Adult worm visibly crossing the eye = Loa loa.
  29. 29Blood smear + swamp creature: Loa loa is diagnosed by microfilariae in peripheral blood smears or by adult worms seen in the conjunctiva.⭐ Microfilariae show diurnal periodicity.
  30. 30Flies: Deer flies (Chrysops) are the vectors of Loa loa.⭐ Chrysops is also called the mango fly.
  31. 31Pink granules + swamp creature: Eosinophilia is a common finding in Loa loa infection.
  32. 32"Diet and Carb" + swamp creature: Diethylcarbamazine is the treatment of choice for Loa loa, killing microfilariae and adults.⭐ Check for high microfilarial counts before treating to avoid encephalopathy.
  33. 33Bent chair legs + swamp creature: Albendazole is sometimes used when multiple courses of diethylcarbamazine fail to cure Loa loa.
Show the original legend picture

110. Toxoplasma gondii

  1. 1Gandhi cat: Toxoplasma gondii is a CNS protozoan; it is an intracellular parasitic protozoan that can infect almost any warm-blooded animal.
  2. 2Meaty cat food + cysts: Toxoplasma gondii is commonly transmitted via consumption of raw or undercooked meat containing tissue cysts; ingestion of water or vegetables contaminated with oocysts shed in the feces of infected animals, particularly cats; and vertical transmission from an infected mother to her fetus during pregnancy.
  3. 3Egg in cat litter box: Toxoplasma gondii is commonly transmitted via consumption of raw or undercooked meat containing tissue cysts; ingestion of water or vegetables contaminated with oocysts shed in the feces of infected animals, particularly cats; and vertical transmission from an infected mother to her fetus during pregnancy.
  4. 4Pregnant woman: Toxoplasma gondii is commonly transmitted via consumption of raw or undercooked meat containing tissue cysts; ingestion of water or vegetables contaminated with oocysts shed in the feces of infected animals, particularly cats; and vertical transmission from an infected mother to her fetus during pregnancy.
  5. 5Torch: Toxoplasma gondii is a member of the TORCHeS group of infections that are transmitted vertically from mother to fetus: T = Toxoplasma; O = Other (varicella-zoster virus, parvovirus B19, Zika virus, HIV, HBV); R = Rubella; C = Cytomegalovirus (CMV); He = Herpes simplex virus (HSV); S = Syphilis.
  6. 6Immunocompromised cane: Individuals who are immunocompromised, especially those with HIV/AIDS, are at increased risk for severe toxoplasmosis, whereas immunocompetent individuals are usually asymptomatic or develop only mild, self-limited, flu-like symptoms.
  7. 7Red turban: Cerebral toxoplasmosis (toxoplasmic encephalitis) is a common complication of Toxoplasma gondii infection in severely immunocompromised individuals, presenting with headache, fever, confusion, and focal neurologic deficits, and typically showing multiple ring-enhancing lesions on MRI or CT.
  8. 8Multiple shiny ring-shaped lenses on glasses: Cerebral toxoplasmosis (toxoplasmic encephalitis) is a common complication of Toxoplasma gondii infection in severely immunocompromised individuals, presenting with headache, fever, confusion, and focal neurologic deficits, and typically showing multiple ring-enhancing lesions on MRI or CT.
  9. 9Turban pin: A brain biopsy distinguishes cerebral toxoplasmosis from primary CNS lymphoma by showing necrotizing inflammation with T. gondii tachyzoites and bradyzoites in toxoplasmosis, versus dense sheets of atypical B cells in lymphoma. Serologic testing for T. gondii IgG/IgM can also help establish the diagnosis.
  10. 10Cat carrying a torch, looking at a cat with milk on its head, a water bowl on its head, and a flash bulb: The classic triad of congenital toxoplasmosis consists of intracranial calcifications, hydrocephalus, and chorioretinitis.
  11. 11Milk on head: In congenital toxoplasmosis, intracranial calcifications are calcium deposits in areas of brain tissue damaged by the parasite.
  12. 12Water bowl on head: In congenital toxoplasmosis, hydrocephalus occurs when inflammation caused by the parasite obstructs cerebrospinal fluid flow, leading to enlargement of the brain's ventricles.
  13. 13Shaking head: Seizures can occur due to intracranial calcifications, as the damaged brain tissue acts as an epileptogenic focus, and due to hydrocephalus, which increases intracranial pressure and disrupts cortical function.
  14. 14Flash bulb: In congenital toxoplasmosis, chorioretinitis is inflammation and necrosis of the retina and choroid caused by Toxoplasma gondii, which can lead to visual impairment.
  15. 15Deaf Beethoven: Congenital toxoplasmosis may also result in hearing loss due to direct parasitic invasion and inflammation of the cochlea and auditory neural pathways, leading to sensorineural damage.
  16. 16Dyed sulfur eggs: Sulfadiazine, in combination with pyrimethamine, is a cornerstone in the treatment of toxoplasmosis.
  17. 17Pyramid pattern: Sulfadiazine, in combination with pyrimethamine, is a cornerstone in the treatment of toxoplasmosis.
  18. 18Sulfur egg priced at $100: Prophylaxis with TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) is recommended for Toxoplasma IgG-positive HIV patients with a CD4+ count below 100 cells/µL to prevent reactivation of toxoplasmosis in immunocompromised patients.
  19. 19"+" kite with an IgG antibody sign: Prophylaxis with TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) is recommended for Toxoplasma IgG-positive HIV patients with a CD4+ count below 100 cells/µL to prevent reactivation of toxoplasmosis in immunocompromised individuals.
  20. 20Immunocompromised cane + $100 bill: Prophylaxis with TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) is recommended for Toxoplasma IgG-positive HIV patients with a CD4+ count below 100 cells/µL to prevent reactivation of toxoplasmosis in immunocompromised individuals.

111. Trematodes

Exam snapshot
  • Schistosoma: cercariae penetrate skin in freshwater; eggs with lateral spine (S. mansoni, S. japonicum) or terminal spine (S. haematobium).
  • S. mansoni/japonicum cause hepatosplenic disease, periportal fibrosis and portal hypertension; S. haematobium causes hematuria and bladder squamous cell carcinoma.
  • Clonorchis sinensis: raw freshwater fish in Asia; bile ducts, pigmented gallstones, cholangitis, cholangiocarcinoma.
  • Paragonimus westermani: raw crab/crayfish; lung fluke with cough and hemoptysis mimicking TB; operculated eggs in sputum or stool.
  • Treatment: praziquantel for all three groups (Clonorchis alternative: albendazole).
  1. 1San Franschisto Aquatic Park: Schistosoma spp. are freshwater blood flukes. Snail-released cercariae penetrate human skin during water exposure and cause schistosomiasis (intestinal, hepatic or urinary, depending on species).
  2. 2Swimmer: Cercariae are free-swimming larvae in contaminated freshwater lakes, rivers and ponds (Africa, Asia, South America), so swimming, wading or bathing puts people at risk. Skin penetration is followed by migration to the liver.
  3. 3Snails by fish: Freshwater snails are the intermediate hosts for Schistosoma and release the infectious cercariae. Snail control and avoiding contaminated water help prevent infection.
  4. 4Liver-shaped coral: After skin penetration, larvae enter the blood and mature into adult worms in the portal venous system of the liver. Acute infection may cause Katayama fever (fever, urticaria, eosinophilia).
  5. 5Blue fish swimming towards liver-shaped coral: Normal portal flow carries blood from the gut toward the liver. This sets up the direction the worms then reverse.
  6. 6Swimming opposite direction: Adult worms migrate against portal flow into the mesenteric (or vesical) venules, where they lay eggs. The eggs, not the worms, cause disease via granulomatous inflammation.
  7. 7Merman statue: Schistosoma mansoni (Africa, Caribbean, South America) lives in the mesenteric veins and causes intestinal and hepatosplenic disease.
  8. 8Lateral fin + merman statue: S. mansoni eggs have a prominent lateral spine, seen on stool microscopy.⭐ Mansoni = lateral spine. Eggs are seen in stool.
  9. 9Japanese tourist: Schistosoma japonicum (East Asia) lives in the mesenteric veins and causes intestinal and hepatosplenic disease.
  10. 10Round fish + japanese tourist: S. japonicum eggs are round with a tiny, inconspicuous lateral spine. Eggs are seen in stool.
  11. 11Aquarium swimmer: Swimmer's itch (cercarial dermatitis) is a transient allergic skin reaction to cercariae of bird schistosomes. The larvae die in the skin because humans are not their host, so there is no systemic disease.
  12. 12Porthole crack/degraded liver coral: Chronic egg deposition causes granulomas, periportal ('pipestem') fibrosis and cirrhosis, leading to presinusoidal portal hypertension with splenomegaly and varices. Liver function is often relatively preserved.⭐ Schisto = presinusoidal portal hypertension (hepatosplenomegaly, varices).
  13. 13Yellow coat: Hepatic schistosomiasis can cause jaundice and cholestasis because egg-induced periportal inflammation and fibrosis obstruct bile flow.
  14. 14Swordfish with terminal bill: Schistosoma haematobium (Africa, Middle East) lives in the vesical venous plexus around the bladder.
  15. 15Terminal bill: S. haematobium eggs have a large terminal spine and are found in urine, not stool.⭐ Haematobium = terminal spine, eggs in urine.
  16. 16Bleeding jellyfish: S. haematobium causes urinary schistosomiasis, often with terminal hematuria, dysuria and later obstructive uropathy.
  17. 17Crab + swordfish: Chronic S. haematobium infection is associated with squamous cell carcinoma of the bladder (not the usual urothelial carcinoma).⭐ S. haematobium = squamous cell carcinoma of the bladder.
  18. 18Pretzel + fish: Praziquantel is the treatment of choice for all Schistosoma species. It acts by increasing calcium permeability and paralyzing the worm.
  19. 19Orca: Clonorchis sinensis is the Chinese (Asian) liver fluke, found in East Asia.
  20. 20Snails + orca: Snails are the first intermediate host (releasing cercariae that encyst in freshwater fish). Humans are infected by eating undercooked or raw freshwater fish.
  21. 21Seagull: Adult flukes reside in the bile ducts. Eggs and worms cause chronic inflammation, ductal dilatation, mechanical obstruction and thickening of the biliary walls.
  22. 22Sclerosed rope: Chronic infection can lead to sclerosing cholangitis (recurrent pyogenic cholangitis), with inflammation and destruction of bile ducts.
  23. 23Crab + orca: Clonorchis is a known risk factor for cholangiocarcinoma, a malignancy of the biliary epithelium.⭐ Clonorchis = cholangiocarcinoma.
  24. 24Black rocks + seagulls: Infection can cause pigmented gallstones (bile stasis and bilirubin precipitation), which can lead to biliary obstruction and cholangitis.
  25. 25"Capped" seagull eggs: Clonorchis eggs are small and operculated (capped), seen on stool exam. Eggs have a lid and a shouldering rim.
  26. 26Pretzel + orca: Praziquantel is the treatment of choice, with albendazole as an alternative.
  27. 27Penguins: Paragonimus westermani is the (Japanese/Asian) lung fluke, endemic to Asia and parts of Africa and Latin America.
  28. 28Red speckled lung spots: Flukes encyst in the lung and cause chronic cough, hemoptysis (rusty sputum) and cystic lesions. It can mimic tuberculosis, so consider it in endemic-area patients with TB-like symptoms and a negative TB workup.⭐ Hemoptysis + eosinophilia in a patient from Asia: think Paragonimus, not TB.
  29. 29Snails + penguins: Snails are the first intermediate host, releasing cercariae that encyst in crustaceans. Humans are infected by eating them.
  30. 30Crab legs: Infection is acquired by eating raw, undercooked or pickled freshwater crabs or crayfish containing metacercariae.
  31. 31"Capped" penguin eggs: Paragonimus eggs are operculated (capped) and found in sputum or stool, because swallowed sputum passes into stool.
  32. 32Pretzel + penguins: Praziquantel is the drug of choice for Paragonimus westermani.
Show the original legend picture

112. Treponema pallidum

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: painless genital chancre (primary), palm and sole rash/condyloma lata (secondary), gummas, aortitis, tabes dorsalis, Argyll Robertson pupils (tertiary); stillborn/rhinitis/Hutchinson teeth (congenital)
  • Spirochete, too thin for Gram stain, not culturable; visualized by dark-field microscopy
  • Dx: nontreponemal screen (VDRL/RPR) then confirm with treponemal test (FTA-ABS, TP-PA); VDRL titer tracks treatment response
  • Treatment: IM benzathine penicillin G for all stages (IV penicillin G for neurosyphilis); desensitize if allergic, especially in pregnancy
  • Buzzwords: chancre, condyloma lata, tree-barking aorta, Argyll Robertson pupil, saber shins, Hutchinson teeth, Jarisch-Herxheimer
  1. 1Pale spiral galaxy: Treponema pallidum is a spirochete that causes the sexually transmitted infection syphilis.
  2. 2Spiral staircase: T. pallidum is a spiral-shaped spirochete that causes syphilis.
  3. 3"Darkfield galaxy": Dark-field microscopy allows direct visualization of T. pallidum, but it needs a sample taken directly from a lesion and is not commonly used.⭐ Used for chancre or condyloma lata fluid; not for routine screening.
  4. 4"VDRL" on screen: VDRL is a nontreponemal test that detects antibodies to cardiolipin-lecithin-cholesterol complexes; it is a convenient, nonspecific screening test that can give false positives. RPR is another nontreponemal test that detects the same antibodies and is used for screening and for monitoring treatment response.⭐ Nontreponemal titers fall with successful treatment.
  5. 5"MONO, RF, SLE, LEP, DRG": Conditions that can cause a false-positive VDRL include mononucleosis, rheumatic fever (RF), systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), leprosy and drugs (IV drug use).⭐ Mnemonic: MONO, RF, SLE, LEP, DRG (also pregnancy and other viral infections).
  6. 6"Field Telescope A-Ab": A positive VDRL/RPR is usually followed by a specific treponemal test, such as the FTA-ABS (fluorescent treponemal antibody absorption) test, to confirm syphilis.⭐ Treponemal tests (FTA-ABS) usually remain positive for life.
  7. 71st level: Syphilis is divided into early (primary and secondary), latent (asymptomatic) and late (tertiary) stages, each with distinct clinical manifestations; this level represents primary syphilis.
  8. 82nd level: Syphilis is divided into early (primary and secondary), latent and late (tertiary) stages; this level represents secondary syphilis.
  9. 9Late night outside: Syphilis is divided into early (primary and secondary), latent and late (tertiary) stages; the outside represents late (tertiary) syphilis.
  10. 10Poked in butt + 1st level: In primary syphilis, a painless chancre appears at the site of inoculation (genitals or anus); it heals spontaneously in about 3-6 weeks.⭐ Painless, indurated ulcer with clean base, unlike painful chancroid.
  11. 11Solar system model + 2nd level: Secondary syphilis is a systemic disease with rash, fever, lymphadenopathy and mucous membrane lesions.
  12. 12Red gloves and boots + 2nd level: A widespread maculopapular rash that often involves the palms and soles is characteristic of secondary syphilis.⭐ Rash on palms and soles: secondary syphilis, RMSF, coxsackie.
  13. 13Planet with a lot of bumps + 2nd level: Condyloma lata are flat-topped, moist, highly infectious lesions of secondary syphilis on the genitals, perineum, perianal region, axillae and inner thighs.⭐ Not to be confused with HPV condyloma acuminata.
  14. 14Looking at planet with a lot of bumps through telescopes: Live T. pallidum spirochetes can be seen within condyloma lata by dark-field microscopy.
  15. 15Cratered moon + outside: Gummas are noninfectious, soft tumor-like lesions with firm necrotic centers that form in tertiary syphilis from chronic granulomatous inflammation; they can occur in skin, bone, liver or other organs and cause local tissue destruction.
  16. 16Aortic tree + outside: Syphilitic aortitis in tertiary syphilis produces "tree-barking" of the aorta and can lead to an ascending aortic (aortic root) aneurysm.⭐ Aortic root dilation can lead to aortic regurgitation.
  17. 17Tree roots + outside: Tertiary syphilitic aortitis is caused by endarteritis and destruction of the vasa vasorum (small vessels supplying the aortic wall) by T. pallidum.
  18. 18Damaged posterior columns + outside: Tabes dorsalis, a form of late neurosyphilis, is degeneration of the dorsal columns and dorsal roots, causing impaired proprioception and vibration sense, progressive sensory ataxia, lancinating pains and loss of deep tendon reflexes.⭐ Wide-based gait, positive Romberg, and Charcot joints.
  19. 19Argyle sweater + light in eyes + outside: Argyll Robertson pupils accommodate but do not react to light ("prostitute's pupil"); they are a highly specific sign of neurosyphilis (tertiary), caused by midbrain (pretectal) damage that disrupts the light reflex while sparing accommodation.⭐ ARP: Accommodation Reflex Present, Pupillary Response Absent.
  20. 20Orion's constellation: Congenital syphilis is transmitted from an infected mother to the fetus and causes a constellation of findings: early (first 2 years, mainly systemic and mucocutaneous) and late (after 2 years, skeletal, dental and ocular stigmata).⭐ Can cause stillbirth and hydrops; screen all pregnant women.
  21. 21Sabre by shins + outside: Saber shins are anteriorly bowed tibias in late congenital syphilis, from periosteal inflammation and abnormal bone growth; usually bilateral.
  22. 22Saddle + outside: Saddle nose, collapse of the nasal bridge from cartilage destruction, is a feature of late congenital syphilis.⭐ Early congenital syphilis may present with snuffles (rhinitis).
  23. 23Chattering teeth + outside: Hutchinson teeth (blunted, notched incisors) and mulberry molars (abnormal enamel on molar cusps) are features of late congenital syphilis.
  24. 24Earmuffs + outside: Congenital syphilis can cause sensorineural hearing loss in both early (birth to 2 years) and late (after 2 years) stages.⭐ Hutchinson triad: notched incisors, interstitial keratitis, 8th nerve deafness.
  25. 25Purple pencil + 1st level: Intramuscular benzathine penicillin G is the preferred treatment for all stages of syphilis, including primary; in allergic patients, desensitize.⭐ Penicillin is the only treatment recommended in pregnancy.
  26. 26Purple pencil + 2nd level: Intramuscular benzathine penicillin G is the preferred treatment for secondary syphilis; in allergic patients, desensitize.
  27. 27Purple pencil + outside: Benzathine penicillin G is the preferred treatment for late (tertiary) syphilis, with desensitization in allergic patients; neurosyphilis requires IV aqueous penicillin G.
  28. 28"Jarisch-Herxheimer comet viewing": The Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction is triggered by rapid lysis of T. pallidum after starting antibiotics, with release of endotoxin-like products and cytokines.⭐ Most common with treatment of early syphilis.
  29. 29Fiery + icy comet: The Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction causes fever and chills, sometimes headache, myalgia and worsening of the rash, within hours of treatment.⭐ Supportive care and antipyretics; not a penicillin allergy.

113. Trichomonas vaginalis (Flagellated protozoan)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: sexually active woman with foul-smelling, frothy yellow-green discharge, vulvovaginal itching and dysuria; strawberry cervix.
  • Flagellated protozoan with an undulating membrane; trophozoite only (no cyst); infects squamous epithelium.
  • Diagnosis: wet mount showing motile pear-shaped trophozoites with jerky motility; vaginal pH above 4.5; NAAT.
  • Treatment: metronidazole or tinidazole for patient and all sexual partners.
  • Buzzwords: strawberry cervix, frothy discharge, tumbling motility, pH greater than 4.5, no cyst.
  1. 1Magic trichs: Trichomonas vaginalis is a unicellular protozoan parasite.
  2. 2Magic scarves: It is a motile organism with four anterior flagella and an undulating membrane.
  3. 3Trophy: T. vaginalis exists only as a trophozoite; it has no cyst form (so it survives poorly outside the host).⭐ No cyst form means transmission is almost exclusively sexual.
  4. 4Couple kissing: Trichomonas vaginalis is a sexually transmitted infection of the urogenital tract.
  5. 5Cobblestone sidewalk: T. vaginalis has a tropism for squamous epithelium and causes infection and inflammation of the vulva, vagina, cervix, and urethra.
  6. 6Smiling delivery man: Asymptomatic carriage, primarily in men, contributes to spread; treating sexual partners reduces this risk.
  7. 7Vaginal flowers: T. vaginalis causes vulvovaginitis: inflammation of the vulva and vagina with erythema, burning, itching, and discharge.
  8. 8Burning trash can: Burning sensation (dysuria and vulvovaginal irritation) is a feature of the vulvovaginitis.
  9. 9Itching dog: Pruritus is a feature of the vulvovaginitis.
  10. 10Leaking fluid: Vaginal discharge is a feature of the vulvovaginitis.
  11. 11Frothy green puddles: Trichomonas causes a classically copious, yellow-green, frothy, malodorous vaginal discharge.⭐ Frothy, malodorous green discharge is the classic descriptor.
  12. 12Strawberry hat trick: Cervical inflammation with erythema, edema, and punctate hemorrhages gives the 'strawberry cervix' (colpitis macularis).⭐ Strawberry cervix is specific but seen in a minority of cases.
  13. 13$4.50 and up: Trichomonas raises vaginal pH (basic environment), so pH testing shows 4.5 or greater.⭐ pH above 4.5 is also seen in bacterial vaginosis; BV has clue cells and a positive whiff test.
  14. 14Leaking package: In men, infection is usually asymptomatic but may cause urethritis with dysuria and a thin white discharge.
  15. 15Pear puddle: Saline wet mount shows motile, pear-shaped trophozoites with a characteristic jerky, tumbling motility.⭐ Immediate wet mount is the fastest diagnostic test; NAAT is more sensitive.
  16. 16Metro: Metronidazole is effective against Trichomonas vaginalis.⭐ Warn about the disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol.
  17. 17Metro couple: All sexual partners must be treated to prevent reinfection and further transmission; therapy of choice is metronidazole (or tinidazole).
  18. 18Condom hat: Consistent barrier contraception (condoms) prevents re-transmission until treatment is complete.

114. Trypanosoma brucei

  1. 1Prince Bruce: Trypanosoma brucei, a parasitic protozoan that causes African sleeping sickness, has three subspecies: T. b. gambiense and T. b. rhodesiense, which infect humans, and T. b. brucei, which does not infect humans and is instead used as a laboratory research model.
  2. 2Sleeping princess: Trypanosoma brucei, a parasitic protozoan that causes African sleeping sickness, has three subspecies: T. b. gambiense and T. b. rhodesiense, which infect humans, and T. b. brucei, which does not infect humans and is instead used as a laboratory research model.
  3. 3Single pink ribbon: Trypanosoma brucei protozoa are motile with a single flagellum.
  4. 4Variable tent colors: Trypanosoma brucei protozoa have variable surface glycoprotein coats that constantly undergo antigenic variation.
  5. 5Gambia spot on map: Trypanosoma brucei gambiense, the anthroponotic form of African sleeping sickness, is primarily carried by humans and transmitted via tsetse fly bites; it causes a slow, progressive disease in riverine and forested areas of Western and Central Africa.
  6. 6Rhodesiense spot on map: Trypanosoma brucei rhodesiense, which causes African sleeping sickness, is transmitted to humans via tsetse fly bites from infected wild and domestic animals, such as antelopes and cattle, causing a rapidly progressing disease in the savannahs and woodlands of East and Southeast Africa.
  7. 7Tea fly: The tsetse fly (Glossina species) injects metacyclic trypomastigotes of Trypanosoma brucei into humans; in the blood, these transform into the bloodstream form and multiply.
  8. 8Pricked finger: A bite from a tsetse fly can cause a red, raised trypanosomal chancre at the inoculation site during the first stage of African sleeping sickness, often with regional lymphadenopathy.
  9. 9Dripping red ink: After inoculation, trypanosomes travel from the skin to the blood and lymphatics, initiating the first (hemolymphatic) stage of African sleeping sickness, which progresses slowly in T. b. gambiense infection and rapidly in T. b. rhodesiense infection.
  10. 10Rolling hills: The first (hemolymphatic) stage of African sleeping sickness presents as a nonspecific febrile illness with headache, malaise, weakness, pruritus, and lymphadenopathy, featuring characteristic intermittent, spiking fevers that recur over days to months.
  11. 11Ruffles on sleeves and pearl necklace: Trypanosoma brucei infection can cause cervical and axillary lymphadenopathy, especially in the early (hemolymphatic) stage of African sleeping sickness.
  12. 12Sleeping during the day: The second (neurological/meningoencephalitic) stage of African sleeping sickness is characterized by CNS involvement, with neuropsychiatric manifestations including daytime sleepiness, mood changes, psychomotor slowing, seizures, and coma.
  13. 13TrypomastiGOAT + blood spot: On a blood smear, Trypanosoma brucei trypomastigotes appear as slender, slightly curved parasites with a central nucleus, a posterior kinetoplast, an undulating membrane, and a flagellum.
  14. 14Serum: Treatment for first-stage African sleeping sickness uses suramin for T. b. rhodesiense, which is effective for acute peripheral infections, and pentamidine for T. b. gambiense, which is preferred for its better tolerance in the chronic course of the disease.
  15. 15Soap: Second-stage African sleeping sickness is treated with melarsoprol for T. b. rhodesiense, as it crosses the blood–brain barrier to target CNS parasites, while T. b. gambiense infection is treated with the safer and more effective nifurtimox-eflornithine combination therapy (NECT).

115. Trypanosoma cruzi

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: patient from Latin America with heart failure/arrhythmia or megacolon/megaesophagus, or acute periorbital swelling (Romana sign)
  • Protozoan, vector = triatomine (kissing, reduviid) bug defecating at bite site; also transfusion, congenital
  • Dx: acute - trypomastigotes on blood smear; chronic - serology (and PCR)
  • Treatment: benznidazole (first line) or nifurtimox
  • Buzzwords: Chagas disease, Romana sign, dilated cardiomyopathy, megacolon, megaesophagus, amastigotes in cardiac muscle
  1. 1PanAmericas Cruise Line: Trypanosoma cruzi is a protozoan parasite found in the Americas and causes Chagas disease.
  2. 2Coach's gas: Chagas disease (American trypanosomiasis) is a vector-borne parasitic infection caused by T. cruzi that can affect the heart, gastrointestinal tract and other organs.
  3. 3Peacock feather logo: T. cruzi appears long and thin on a blood smear, often C- or S-shaped, with an undulating membrane and a single flagellum with a large kinetoplast.
  4. 4TRI atom logo: The triatomine bug (reduviid or kissing bug) is the vector of T. cruzi.⭐ Bites near the face, often at night.
  5. 5A Master Goat yoga pose: Amastigotes are the replicative forms of T. cruzi.
  6. 6Stay WITHIN and the fun will begin: Amastigotes replicate intracellularly.⭐ A in amastigote = "A for Assimilated inside the cell".
  7. 7Triple Master Goat yoga pose: Trypomastigotes are the non-replicating, infective, extracellular forms of T. cruzi; they circulate in the blood, are detectable by microscopy, and are crescent-shaped and flagellated.
  8. 8Cruise destination map: T. cruzi is endemic in Latin America, with cases extending from the southern U.S. to Argentina and Chile.
  9. 9Substandard zone: Substandard housing (cracks in mud or thatch walls) raises the risk of vector exposure and transmission.
  10. 10Sipping red punch: T. cruzi can be transmitted by blood transfusion (and organ transplantation).
  11. 11Vertical slide: T. cruzi can be transmitted vertically (mother to fetus).
  12. 12Child on cruise: Many T. cruzi infections occur in childhood, including congenital Chagas disease, which is an acute-phase infection with high parasitemia.
  13. 13Pooping on Triple Master Goat: The triatomine bug bites to take a blood meal and then defecates near the bite; trypomastigotes in the feces enter through the wound, mucous membranes or conjunctiva when the bite is scratched or rubbed.⭐ The bite wound is not the source; the feces are.
  14. 14Muscle-bound A Master Goat yogi: Trypomastigotes enter host cells (especially muscle and nervous tissue) and transform into amastigotes that replicate intracellularly.
  15. 15Heart + crop top: T. cruzi has tropism for myocardium and the myenteric plexus of the gastrointestinal tract.
  16. 16Triple Master yogi's flame bandana: Acute infection is usually mild, with fever, malaise and nonspecific symptoms lasting about 8-12 weeks.⭐ Most acute infections are asymptomatic or nonspecific.
  17. 17A Master yogi's swollen eye: A chagoma is an inflammatory nodule at the parasite entry site; when T. cruzi enters through the conjunctiva, the unilateral painless periorbital edema is called the Romana sign (firm, red, swollen, may contain edema, monocytes and intracellular amastigotes).⭐ Romana sign = unilateral periorbital swelling; a classic Chagas buzzword.
  18. 18Reflected Triple Master Goat pose: Acute-phase Chagas disease is diagnosed by seeing trypomastigotes on a blood smear (also PCR).⭐ Chronic disease is diagnosed by serology, since parasitemia is low.
  19. 19Baby coach's gas: Congenital Chagas disease is part of the acute phase with high parasitemia; findings can include low birth weight, hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice or edema, though many infants are asymptomatic.
  20. 20Pallid shirt: Anemia can occur in congenital Chagas disease, likely from high parasitemia, inflammation and effects on red cell production.
  21. 21Liver and spleen pouch: Hepatosplenomegaly can occur in congenital Chagas disease, likely from high parasitemia and immune-mediated organ enlargement.
  22. 22Grandfather clock: The chronic indeterminate phase is a 10-30 year asymptomatic period during which patients are seropositive.⭐ About 20-30% progress to symptomatic chronic disease.
  23. 23Inflated heart pool + ECG tracing + clumped sticks: Chronic cardiac Chagas disease causes dilated cardiomyopathy with myocardial hypertrophy, heart failure, arrhythmias and thromboembolism (apical aneurysm with mural thrombus), from parasite-mediated injury and chronic inflammation.⭐ Chagas is a leading cause of dilated cardiomyopathy in Latin America; right bundle branch block is common.
  24. 24MEGA drainage pipe: Chronic T. cruzi infection can cause megacolon and megaesophagus with dysmotility, from destruction of myenteric plexus neurons (fibrosis).⭐ Megaesophagus resembles achalasia (dysphagia).
  25. 25Bug spraying: Insecticides are a key preventive measure to control triatomine (reduviid, kissing bug) populations and reduce transmission.
  26. 26Under renovation: Improving substandard housing is a key preventive measure to reduce triatomine infestation and Chagas transmission.
  27. 27TV screen: Vertical transmission is prevented by screening programs for women of childbearing age.
  28. 28Forward BEND pose: Benznidazole is used to treat acute or early chronic Chagas disease; for women identified through screening it can also help prevent congenital Chagas disease if given before a desired pregnancy.⭐ Benznidazole is contraindicated in pregnancy, so treat before conceiving.
  29. 29Spilled milk: During acute Chagas disease, women are advised to avoid breastfeeding because circulating parasites could potentially be transmitted to the infant.
  30. 30FUR MOCcasins: Nifurtimox is an approved treatment for Chagas disease (acute, reactivated, and children with chronic infection) but is second line to benznidazole.⭐ Nifurtimox is also used for sleeping sickness (with eflornithine) in West African disease.

116. Varicella-Zoster Virus (Herpesviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Chickenpox: pruritic vesicular rash in crops at different stages, preceded by fever and malaise; shingles: painful unilateral dermatomal vesicular rash.
  • Enveloped linear dsDNA virus, Herpesviridae (HHV-3); spread by respiratory droplets; latent in dorsal root (sensory) ganglia.
  • Clinical diagnosis; PCR or DFA of lesion; Tzanck smear shows multinucleated giant cells (not specific, also HSV).
  • Acyclovir (famciclovir or valacyclovir for zoster); live attenuated varicella vaccine in childhood; recombinant Shingrix for zoster prevention.
  • Dew drop on a rose petal; lesions in different stages; dermatomal; postherpetic neuralgia; zoster ophthalmicus (V1); congenital varicella syndrome.
  1. 1Zeus: Varicella-zoster virus (VZV), a DNA virus in the Herpesviridae family (HHV-3).⭐ VZV = human herpesvirus 3.
  2. 2Cool colors: VZV is a DNA virus (linear double-stranded DNA).
  3. 3Hermes: VZV belongs to the Herpesviridae family.
  4. 4Wrapped in toga: VZV is an enveloped virus.
  5. 5Chickens: VZV primary infection causes chickenpox (varicella): a pruritic rash of small fluid-filled blisters.
  6. 6Sweat: Fever, headache, fatigue, and loss of appetite (prodrome) often appear 1-2 days before the chickenpox lesions.
  7. 7Holding head: Same prodrome: fever, headache, fatigue, and loss of appetite typically precede the rash by 1-2 days.
  8. 8Water gun spray: VZV is transmitted primarily by respiratory droplets (airborne) and by contact with vesicular fluid.⭐ Highly contagious; airborne isolation is required in hospitals.
  9. 9Dew on rose petals: VZV vesicles are likened to "dew drops on a rose petal": grouped vesicles or pustules on an erythematous base that may rupture into shallow ulcers and then crust over as they heal.⭐ Classic buzzword: dew drops on a rose petal.
  10. 10"All ages welcome": Chickenpox lesions appear in varying stages of development and healing at the same time (macules, papules, vesicles, crusts), unlike many other viral rashes in which the whole rash moves through the stages together.⭐ Lesions in different stages = varicella (vs. smallpox, where all lesions are in the same stage).
  11. 11Tank + giant spots: A Tzanck smear (scraping of a lesion base) historically showed multinucleated giant cells; this is not specific, as HSV gives the same finding, so PCR is used for specific identification.⭐ Tzanck smear: multinucleated giant cells (VZV and HSV).
  12. 12Adult coughing: Adults are more likely than children to develop complications of chickenpox; one of the most serious is varicella pneumonia.
  13. 13Red turban: Encephalitis is a rare but serious complication of VZV; it presents with headache, fever, altered mental status, and seizures, and (like HSV) can involve the temporal lobe. Cerebellar ataxia is also a classic complication in children.
  14. 14Immunocompromised cane: Immunocompromised patients are at elevated risk of severe VZV disease, including varicella pneumonia and encephalitis.
  15. 15"Live show" syringe: The varicella vaccine is a live attenuated vaccine, typically given in childhood (two doses).⭐ Live vaccine: contraindicated in pregnancy and severe immunodeficiency.
  16. 16Recycling bin: Acyclovir reduces the number of lesions and duration of illness if started within 24 hours of rash onset.⭐ IV acyclovir for immunocompromised patients and severe disease.
  17. 17Sleeping on dorsal tree roots: After primary chickenpox, VZV becomes latent in the dorsal root (and cranial nerve) sensory ganglia and can reactivate later as shingles.
  18. 18Elderly people + immunocompromised cane: Stress, aging, decreased cell-mediated immunity, or underlying disease can trigger reactivation of latent VZV, causing shingles (herpes zoster).
  19. 19Shingles: VZV reactivation causes shingles (herpes zoster), which predominantly affects adults over 50 and immunocompromised individuals.
  20. 20Zeus holding pain bolt: Shingles (herpes zoster) causes an intensely painful vesicular rash ("dew drops on a rose petal") in a unilateral dermatomal distribution (the skin supplied by one spinal nerve root); pain is often worsened by even a gentle touch or breeze.⭐ Zoster does not cross the midline.
  21. 21Rose petals falling across abdomen: Same rash description: shingles (herpes zoster) causes an intensely painful vesicular rash ("dew drops on a rose petal") in a unilateral dermatomal distribution (the skin supplied by one spinal nerve root); pain is often worsened by even a gentle touch or breeze. Here the icon shows a thoracic or abdominal band-like dermatomal distribution.
  22. 22Chest bolt: Postherpetic neuralgia, a complication of shingles, is persistent burning pain in the affected dermatome lasting long after the rash has resolved.⭐ Treat with gabapentin, pregabalin, TCAs, or topical lidocaine/capsaicin.
  23. 23Red eye patch: Herpes zoster ophthalmicus results from reactivation in the ophthalmic division of the trigeminal nerve (V1); it presents with a unilateral forehead rash, red eye or vision changes, and can cause vision loss.⭐ Hutchinson sign (vesicles on the nasal tip) signals nasociliary involvement and high risk of ocular disease.
  24. 24"Live show - seniors only 60 and over": Historically, the live attenuated Zostavax vaccine was recommended for people 60 and over to prevent shingles and postherpetic neuralgia; it has been replaced by Shingrix, a recombinant subunit vaccine, which is now used for shingles prevention (adults 50 and over).⭐ Shingrix = recombinant glycoprotein E vaccine, preferred; non-live.
  25. 25"Max occupancy 200": HIV patients with a CD4+ count above 200 cells/uL are eligible for the live varicella-zoster vaccine; it is contraindicated below this threshold.⭐ Live vaccines are avoided if CD4 <200.
  26. 26Family's recycling shirts: For VZV-induced shingles, famciclovir and valacyclovir are effective treatment options (along with acyclovir).
  27. 27Violet recycling bin: Same point: famciclovir and valacyclovir are both effective for treating shingles (better bioavailability and less frequent dosing than acyclovir).
  28. 28Torches: VZV is part of the TORCHeS group of congenital infections: Toxoplasma, Other (varicella-zoster virus, parvovirus B19, Zika virus, HIV), Rubella, CMV, Herpes simplex virus, and Syphilis, which are transmitted vertically across the placenta.
  29. 29Pregnant lady: VZV exposure during early pregnancy (first or early second trimester) carries the highest risk of congenital varicella syndrome: dermatomal cutaneous scarring, limb hypoplasia, and eye abnormalities such as chorioretinitis or microphthalmia.⭐ Maternal varicella near delivery (5 days before to 2 days after) risks neonatal varicella; give VZIG.
  30. 30Torn seams + plucked eye + stubby limbs: Representation of congenital varicella syndrome: scarring in a dermatomal pattern (torn seams), eye defects such as chorioretinitis or microphthalmia (plucked eye), and limb hypoplasia (stubby limbs).
Show the original legend picture

117. Vibrio spp

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: profuse rice-water diarrhea (cholera) from contaminated water in poor sanitation; dehydration, hypovolemic shock.
  • Comma-shaped, oxidase-positive gram-negative rods; grow on TCBS agar (yellow, sucrose-fermenting) and in alkaline media.
  • Cholera toxin permanently activates Gs (ADP-ribosylation), raising cAMP and opening CFTR, causing massive Cl-/Na+/water secretion.
  • Treatment: aggressive oral/IV rehydration; antibiotics (doxycycline or azithromycin) shorten severe disease.
  • V. parahaemolyticus: raw seafood gastroenteritis; V. vulnificus: raw oysters/seawater wound infections, hemorrhagic bullae, sepsis, especially in liver disease or iron overload.
  1. 1Red canoe: Vibrio species are comma-shaped (curved), gram-negative, oxidase-positive bacilli. They are motile by a single polar flagellum (darting motility).
  2. 2Blue rings: Vibrio are oxidase positive: they produce cytochrome c oxidase, an enzyme in the aerobic respiratory chain, which turns the oxidase reagent blue/purple. Oxidase positivity distinguishes Vibrio (and Campylobacter, Pseudomonas) from Enterobacteriaceae such as E. coli.
  3. 3Bags of rice: Vibrio cholerae causes profuse watery "rice-water" diarrhea (flecks of mucus in clear fluid) with no blood and little inflammation. Patients can lose liters per hour, leading to severe dehydration, hypovolemic shock and electrolyte disturbances (hypokalemia, metabolic acidosis).
  4. 4Outhouse: V. cholerae is transmitted fecal-orally via contaminated water or food, especially where sanitation is poor and during disasters or crowding. Humans and aquatic environments (shellfish, plankton) are the reservoirs.
  5. 5BASE: Vibrio grow preferentially in alkaline (basic) media, e.g., alkaline peptone water; the selective plate is TCBS agar, where V. cholerae forms yellow colonies (ferments sucrose).
  6. 6Yellow ducks with lemon shaped bodies: Vibrio are extremely acid-labile and are killed by stomach acid, so a high infectious dose is needed. Patients with reduced gastric acidity (e.g., PPI use, pernicious anemia, achlorhydria) are more susceptible.
  7. 7Raft rope attached to river shore: The toxin-coregulated pilus (TCP) of V. cholerae allows attachment to the intestinal mucosa (without invasion). It is also the receptor for the CTXφ phage that carries the cholera toxin genes.
  8. 8"Toxic" symbol: Cholera toxin (an A-B toxin) permanently activates Gs protein via ADP-ribosylation, overactivating adenylate cyclase and raising cAMP in intestinal epithelial cells, which activates CFTR channels and increases Cl- secretion into the lumen. The lumen becomes negatively charged, so Na+ follows the electrochemical gradient, and water follows osmotically, producing profuse watery rice-water diarrhea.
  9. 9GaS stove: The toxin permanently activates Gs (the stimulatory G protein), keeping adenylate cyclase on. Mechanism: Gs overactivation raises cAMP, activates CFTR, increases Cl- secretion, which draws Na+ and then water into the lumen, causing rice-water diarrhea.⭐ Cholera toxin ADP-ribosylates Gs (locks it on); pertussis toxin ADP-ribosylates Gi.
  10. 10cAMP: Elevated cAMP in intestinal epithelial cells activates protein kinase A and CFTR channels, causing chloride secretion into the lumen (cAMP is the central second messenger of the toxin). The lumen becomes negatively charged, Na+ follows, and water follows Na+ osmotically.
  11. 11Salty peanut water: The net result is massive loss of Cl- and Na+ with water into the lumen, producing profuse watery diarrhea; the stool is isotonic and electrolyte-rich. Chloride secretion drives the sodium and water loss, which causes dehydration, with hypokalemia and metabolic acidosis (bicarbonate loss).
  12. 12Drinking water: Oral rehydration is the primary treatment of cholera; it works because glucose-coupled Na+ uptake (SGLT1) remains intact. Antibiotics (doxycycline, azithromycin) and IV fluids are reserved for severe cases.⭐ Treat cholera by replacing fluids; the toxin effect is irreversible, so symptoms last until epithelial cells turn over.
  13. 13Eating oysters: V. parahaemolyticus and V. vulnificus are marine bacteria that infect humans through ingestion of contaminated seafood, especially raw oysters, or via open wounds exposed to seawater. V. parahaemolyticus is the most common food-related Vibrio infection in the United States, typically causing self-limited gastroenteritis. V. vulnificus is the most deadly Vibrio species, causing severe wound infections and sepsis with fever, chills, hypotension, and large fluid-filled bullae that can become necrotic and progress to necrotizing fasciitis.⭐ V. vulnificus is worst in patients with liver disease (cirrhosis) or iron overload (hemochromatosis); treat with doxycycline plus a third-generation cephalosporin (ceftazidime) and surgical debridement.

118. West Nile virus

  1. 1Warm colors: Flaviviridae are RNA viruses.
  2. 2Positive sun: Flaviviridae are positive-sense RNA viruses.
  3. 3"NEW FLAVORS!" fruit punch stand: The Flaviviridae family includes viruses in the genera Flavivirus and Hepacivirus, five clinically relevant members of which include dengue virus, yellow fever virus, Zika virus, West Nile virus, and hepatitis C virus. Many flaviviruses are arboviruses (ARthropod-BOrne viruses) transmitted to humans by insect vectors, such as mosquitoes of the Aedes or Culex species, whereas hepatitis C virus is primarily bloodborne and not vector-transmitted.
  4. 4Single straw: Flaviviridae possess a non-segmented, single-stranded RNA genome.
  5. 5Robe: Flaviviridae are enveloped viruses.
  6. 6Hep C fruit punch flavor: Hepatitis C virus is a member of Flaviviridae (i.e., the Hepacivirus genus).
  7. 7Brown rubber dinghy: Dengue virus (Flavivirus genus) is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito and causes dengue fever ("breakbone fever"), characterized by high fever, severe myalgias/arthralgias, headache, and rash.
  8. 8Mosquitos bothering man in dinghy: Dengue virus (Flavivirus genus) is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito and causes dengue fever ("breakbone fever"), characterized by high fever, severe myalgias/arthralgias, headache, and rash.
  9. 9Two broken bone-shaped oars: Dengue virus (Flavivirus genus) is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito and causes dengue fever ("breakbone fever"), characterized by high fever, severe myalgias/arthralgias, headache, and macular rash. There are four main serotypes (DENV-1 to DENV-4), all of which can cause dengue fever, but DENV-2 is particularly notorious for more severe manifestations.
  10. 10Red blood cell-shaped life preservers: Dengue can progress to dengue hemorrhagic fever when thrombocytopenia, plasma leakage, and hemorrhagic manifestations are present, often as the disease progresses to secondary infection.
  11. 11Blue and red ribbons coming off the renal-shaped dinghy: Severe dengue can lead to acute renal failure and hypovolemic or septic shock due to plasma leakage, dehydration, and multiorgan dysfunction.
  12. 12Man in dinghy floating on the river by himself: Treatment of dengue virus (Flavivirus genus) infection is primarily supportive, with hydration as the cornerstone to prevent or manage plasma leakage and shock in severe dengue.
  13. 13Yellow water buffalo: Yellow fever virus (a positive-sense RNA virus in the Flaviviridae family) is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito, causing yellow fever characterized by fever, jaundice, myalgia, and hemorrhagic manifestations; treatment is mainly supportive.
  14. 14Mosquitos bothering the buffalo: Yellow fever virus (a positive-sense RNA virus in the Flaviviridae family) is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito, causing yellow fever characterized by fever, jaundice, myalgia, and hemorrhagic manifestations; treatment is mainly supportive.
  15. 15Liver spot on the yellow buffalo: Yellow fever virus may damage hepatocytes and Kupffer cells, causing hepatocellular necrosis and inflammation that lead to hepatomegaly and impaired bilirubin metabolism, which manifests clinically as jaundice.
  16. 16Back hump: Yellow fever can cause severe myalgia (muscle pain), especially in the lumbosacral region, due to viral-induced myositis and cytokine-mediated muscle inflammation.
  17. 17Red stool: Yellow fever is a hemorrhagic fever that can cause bleeding diathesis (bloody diarrhea, melena, hematemesis, epistaxis, and petechiae) due to hepatocellular injury (impaired clotting factor synthesis), thrombocytopenia, and endothelial damage.
  18. 18Syringe: A live attenuated vaccine is available to prevent yellow fever and is especially recommended for those traveling to parts of Africa and South America.
  19. 19Nile bird: West Nile virus, transmitted by Culex mosquitoes and maintained in birds as reservoir hosts, is mostly asymptomatic but can cause West Nile fever, characterized by fever, headache, myalgia (muscle pain), and rash, or can progress to neuroinvasive disease; treatment is mainly supportive.
  20. 20Mosquitos swarming around birds: West Nile virus is transmitted by Culex mosquitoes and maintained in birds as reservoir hosts. Infection is mostly asymptomatic but can cause West Nile fever, characterized by fever, headache, myalgia (muscle pain), and rash, or can progress to neuroinvasive disease; treatment is mainly supportive.
  21. 21Red turban on head: West Nile virus can cause neuroinvasive disease, such as encephalitis (inflammation of the brain parenchyma), presenting with fever, headache, altered mental status, seizures, and focal neurological deficits.
  22. 22Neck brace: West Nile virus can cause neuroinvasive disease, such as meningitis (inflammation of the meninges), presenting with fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, and nausea.
  23. 23Floppy neck: West Nile virus can present with asymmetric acute flaccid paralysis (damage to anterior horn cells, much like poliovirus-induced poliomyelitis).
  24. 24Flapping wings: West Nile virus can present with signs of neurological dysfunction, such as seizures, altered mental status, focal deficits, and a Parkinson-like movement disorder, among others.
  25. 25Comatose bird: West Nile virus can cause serious neuroinvasive disease, which can result in coma.
  26. 26Hippo with a "C" earring and a "This way to the Hep Sea" sign: The hepatitis C virus is a member of Flaviviridae (i.e., the Hepacivirus genus).
  27. 27Yellow hippo: Hepatitis C causes hepatitis (inflammation of the liver), typically presenting with fatigue, malaise, jaundice, right upper quadrant discomfort, and hepatomegaly (enlargement of the liver).
  28. 28Liver spot: Hepatitis C causes hepatitis (inflammation of the liver), typically presenting with fatigue, malaise, jaundice, right upper quadrant discomfort, and hepatomegaly (enlargement of the liver).

119. Yersinia enterocolitica & pestis

Exam snapshot
  • Y. pestis: plague; bubonic (painful buboes after flea bite), septicemic, or pneumonic (person-to-person); reservoirs are rodents and prairie dogs.
  • Y. enterocolitica: fever, abdominal pain, bloody diarrhea, pseudoappendicitis (RLQ pain) from contaminated pork, milk or dog feces; grows in the cold.
  • Bipolar (safety-pin) staining of gram-negative rods; Yops injected by the type III secretion system.
  • Plague treatment: streptomycin (or gentamicin) or doxycycline; killed vaccine exists only for high-risk people.
  • Y. enterocolitica can trigger reactive arthritis and erythema nodosum.
  1. 1English terrier: Yersinia enterocolitica is a gram-negative bacillus (a member of Enterobacteriaceae that does not ferment lactose) and a cause of enterocolitis.
  2. 2"Yersin's pets": Yersinia pestis is a gram-negative bacillus (a pet name pun for Yersinia and the animal reservoirs of plague) and causes plague.
  3. 3Red hues: Both Y. pestis and Y. enterocolitica are gram-negative bacilli (pink on Gram stain), with an outer membrane containing LPS. They are facultative anaerobes and non-lactose fermenters.
  4. 4Puppy poop: Y. enterocolitica is part of the normal intestinal flora of dogs and can be transmitted via dog feces (fecal-oral). Pigs (raw or undercooked pork, such as chitterlings) are another classic source.
  5. 5Daycare-aged toddler: Outbreaks of Y. enterocolitica can occur in daycare settings through fecal-oral spread. The most commonly affected groups are infants, young children, older adults and immunocompromised individuals.
  6. 6Milk: Y. enterocolitica can be transmitted through improperly handled or contaminated (e.g., unpasteurized) milk and dairy products.
  7. 7Icicles: Y. enterocolitica can survive and multiply at refrigeration temperatures (about 4°C, per the usual teaching; the original picture reads unclearly), posing food safety risks. In the lab, cold enrichment is used to isolate it.⭐ Cold-growing organisms: Yersinia enterocolitica and Listeria.check original
  8. 8Safety pin: Y. pestis and Y. enterocolitica show bipolar (safety-pin) staining, in which the two ends of the rod-shaped bacteria stain much more intensely than the central region under microscopy (best seen with Wright-Giemsa or Wayson stain).
  9. 9Glass capsule: Sketchy shows capsules on Yersinia: Y. pestis has a capsule (the F1 capsular antigen), which resists phagocytosis.
  10. 10Red stool: Y. enterocolitica causes enterocolitis with fever, abdominal pain, and bloody diarrhea, often in children, and may also involve mesenteric lymph nodes and the terminal ileum.
  11. 11Licking abdomen: Y. enterocolitica infection may mimic appendicitis (pseudoappendicitis) with RLQ pain, due to terminal ileitis and mesenteric lymphadenitis; it can also trigger reactive arthritis and erythema nodosum afterward.
  12. 12Rodent: Rodents (rats, prairie dogs, ground squirrels) are the primary reservoirs for Y. pestis; in the US, plague occurs mainly in the Southwest.
  13. 13Flea: Fleas (e.g., the rat flea) transmit Y. pestis between animals and humans by biting; the bacteria block the flea's foregut, so the flea regurgitates them into the bite wound.
  14. 14Armpit lumps: Painful, swollen lymph nodes (buboes), usually axillary or inguinal, are classic for bubonic plague (Y. pestis), appearing with fever after a flea bite.⭐ Buboes + fever after rodent/flea exposure in the Southwest US = plague.
  15. 15Red shirt: Disseminated Y. pestis infection (septicemic plague) causes tissue necrosis, DIC, cutaneous hemorrhage (purpura, acral gangrene, hence "Black Death"), septic shock and multi-organ failure. Pneumonic plague (primary or from bubonic spread) is highly lethal and can spread person-to-person by respiratory droplets.
  16. 16Turkey baster: Yersinia species possess a type III secretion system, a needle-like apparatus that injects Yops (Yersinia outer proteins) into host cells; these proteins help the bacteria evade host immune responses and promote their invasion and intracellular survival (they inhibit phagocytosis and cytokine signaling).
  17. 17Sai: Aminoglycosides (especially streptomycin; gentamicin is also used) treat Yersinia infections, especially plague.check original
  18. 18Bicycle tire: Tetracyclines (doxycycline) are also effective against Yersinia spp. and are used for treatment and post-exposure prophylaxis of plague.
  19. 19"Vaccinate + KILLED": An inactivated (killed) Y. pestis vaccine exists, used only for high-risk individuals (lab workers, endemic field workers, etc.); it is not routinely recommended for the general public.
